A Friend Asked: Essays on Self-as-an-End · Volume II

A Friend Asked, Volume II: Living as an End
有朋友问 · 卷二：作为目的而生活

Han Qin (秦汉) · 2026
DOI: 10.5281/zenodo.23959195 · Full PDF on Zenodo · CC BY 4.0
About this volume

A Friend Asked: Essays on Self-as-an-End, Volume II: Living as an End is a collection of applied-philosophy essays that begins with ordinary questions. This volume contains the general preface and sixty-five essays, arranged in the order in which they were originally written and organized into five parts: “Inner Life,” “Knowledge and Consciousness,” “Education, Freedom, and Relationships,” “Power and Public Life,” and “New Forms of Instrumentalization and Human Resistance.”

Volume II brings the Self-as-an-End (SAE) framework into concrete questions concerning death, love, loneliness, education, freedom, consciousness, power, public life, and artificial intelligence. The essays minimize specialist vocabulary and approach philosophy through situations that remain open to general readers.

Author's Note

These essays did not begin with a finished theory in search of examples. They began with questions from friends: Why does a person become afraid? Why does a technology inspire both excitement and unease? Why does an institution turn people into instruments in the name of protecting them? The writing often came before the answer, and judgments changed in the face of new facts and counterexamples. In gathering these pieces into a book, I have not tried to erase the particular circumstances in which they arose. I hope, instead, that the questions will remain visible after the news has receded. Readers need not accept every conclusion here. If these essays can help a question be asked more precisely, they will already have done part of their work.

Translator's Note

The Chinese and English editions of this book are published separately, but their volumes, parts, essay numbers, and hidden stable source IDs correspond exactly. The translation is based on the edited Chinese manuscript, not on a line-by-line transcription of the original X posts. Platform residue, passing references, and conversational phrasing that depended entirely on the moment of posting were addressed during the Chinese editing process. The English text preserves the historical setting of each essay while adding only the minimum context needed by readers who may not share the same news memory.

Self-as-an-End (SAE) remains in English throughout the book. It is not a synonym for self-interest, self-actualization, or autonomy. The term extends a Kantian concern with persons as ends in themselves into a broader question: whether a person is recognized, within the institutions and social structures that shape their life, as the ultimate source of the ends they live by rather than as a means for goals imposed by an external system. Where the distinction matters, the translation separates Kant's formulation from the author's expanded use of Self-as-an-End without adding an apparatus of scholarly notes.

Core terms are kept consistent. Instrumentalization and self-instrumentalization retain distinct meanings, while terms such as alienation, agency, and subjectivity are distinguished according to context. Consistency, however, does not mean mirroring Chinese syntax sentence by sentence. The translation aims to preserve the force of the judgments, the humor, the hesitation, and the author's habit of correcting himself. Sentence length and paragraphing have sometimes been adjusted for natural English, but no new argument has been added.

Chinese institutions, classical texts, internet culture, films, television series, and games are explained only where comprehension requires it. Personal names follow the Romanized forms commonly used by the people concerned, and titles are kept consistent after their first appearance. Relative dates have been anchored to the historical moment of composition, and predictions remain predictions rather than being rewritten in light of later events. References already present in the essays remain part of the prose; no new system of citations or notes has been imposed.

Some wordplay, kinship-inflected forms of address, and political resonances cannot be carried over at equal weight. In such cases, the translation favors the function the expression serves in the argument, with a brief explanation in the text only when indispensable. Translation is itself an act of interpretation. The aim here is not to remove every ambiguity on the author's behalf, but to let English readers encounter the same questions that confront readers of the Chinese original.

General Preface

Writing as a Form of Prayer

I could hear the friendly teasing in the question, but I also thought it was a good one, and more meaningful than it first appeared.

Kafka, yes, the Kafka who wrote The Metamorphosis, once said that writing was a form of prayer. I agree. First, why prayer rather than some other metaphor? Sincerity, passion, and persistence can all describe serious writing, but they describe so many things that they can be made to fit almost anything. Prayer is different. It has a very specific structure: we speak to someone or something that is under no obligation to answer us.

Every part of that sentence matters. We are speaking, but we are not merely talking to ourselves. Our words have a direction. They are addressed to someone. Yet that person owes us no reply. However precisely, sincerely, or forcefully we speak, we cannot demand an answer.

This is where a writer stands. We can take responsibility only for our half, for putting the words into the world. Whether understanding occurs belongs to another person, and it may not occur at all. We cannot complete the process by ourselves.

Once that structure becomes clear, several other things come into focus.

The first is that writing asks us not to close a question too quickly.

Imagine someone writing, “I have so much, and yet I am still unhappy.” They could immediately add, “So I must be ungrateful.” The essay would be over, and the question would have been shut down before it had really begun. Or they could keep writing: What exactly do I mean by unhappy? Can I value what I have and still be unwilling to continue living under a particular arrangement? Why have the conditions other people consider sufficient failed to address what is actually troubling me?

Here, writing is not simply a way to express emotion. It keeps a ready-made answer from replacing the real question too soon. Writing resembles prayer because the writer does not have to play the role of someone who has already figured everything out. The writer can admit, “I do not yet understand, but I am willing to look carefully.”

Flannery O’Connor’s A Prayer Journal is a good example, and a genuinely religious one. She asks to become a better writer and to have her work published, but she also examines her own hunger for success. She prays for clarity and asks whether her desires might be obscuring what she is trying to understand. She does not reduce prayer to “Please give me what I want.”

From this we can draw a way of writing that does not depend on religious faith: I hope for an answer, but I am also willing to discover that what needs to change may be not only the answer, but the way I ask the question.

The second is that writing not only asks the world to listen to me. It also allows the world to correct me.

If an essay’s only task is to prove that its author was right all along, then the evidence, the people in the essay, and even the reader will all be arranged as tools in support of the conclusion. Another kind of writing allows what resists us to remain on the page. We may love a beautiful sentence and delete it when we discover that it is inaccurate. We may begin by casting a person as the embodiment of an idea, only to find that the particulars of their life refuse the label. We may think the argument is complete, then return to rewrite it when a reader points out a counterexample.

In such moments, writing does more than amplify the voice of the “I.” It also limits the “I’s” power to explain everything however it pleases. Writers sometimes describe revision this way in interviews: at a certain point, a sentence is no longer merely something they own; it begins to answer back. This is a description of the experience of writing, not a claim that inspiration comes from some supernatural force.

Of course, being willing to be corrected does not mean abandoning judgment. We still have to decide whether a correction deserves to be accepted or is merely another untested demand. Openness does not mean accepting everything indiscriminately.

The third is that writing longs for a response, but cannot turn that response into a command.

We can make a sentence more precise, describe an experience more concretely, and strengthen an argument. None of that guarantees that another person will understand, much less that they must agree. To write for readers we do not yet know therefore involves an unguaranteed trust: perhaps someone else is facing a similar confusion; perhaps they will pause over a particular sentence; perhaps they will disagree, but in doing so help me see what I could not see on my own.

Nor must a response always come from someone else. A private journal may give one’s future self a chance to look back and understand one’s present self more fairly.

Sometimes a lack of response is a real disappointment, but it does not cancel the value of having spoken. At the same time, writing seriously does not entitle me to anyone’s attention. I hope to reach you, but I do not claim ownership over your response. I believe communication is worth attempting, but I do not assume it must succeed.

This metaphor also reveals something else: prayer done badly and writing done badly fail in almost exactly the same ways.

One failure turns prayer into a demand that says nothing but “Give me this.” Its equivalent in writing is a sentence that is always asking for something: agreement, sympathy, recognition. Another form of prayer is performed for an audience, with posture more important than substance. Its equivalent is writing designed mainly to look profound. A third begins with the answer already settled and merely recites it. Its equivalent is an essay whose conclusion is fixed in the first paragraph and whose middle merely builds a road to it. All three are still forms of speech, but the structure in which “the other person owes me no answer” has disappeared. They do not actually need a response. They need only a witness.

Prayer is also often imagined as waiting for some power to take care of things on our behalf. In this metaphor, the opposite is true.

We cannot guarantee that a neglected experience will eventually be understood fairly, but we can begin by writing about it without distorting it further. We cannot guarantee that everyone will treat others with seriousness, but we can refuse to reduce the people in our writing to props that prove our point. We cannot guarantee that our theory is correct, but we can state its premises clearly, preserve the objections, and mark what remains unresolved.

A wish does not have to remain only a wish simply because its fulfillment is not guaranteed. It can enter into very concrete acts: how a sentence is written, how an example is handled, whether a revision is honest.

For the same reason, this kind of “prayer” need not be gentle or serene. It can contain anger. It can protest harm. It can also express gratitude for something good. Writing down an ordinary moment with one’s family need not serve any conclusion. It may simply recognize that the moment deserves to be seen, that our busyness should not make it disappear merely because it was not spectacular. In such moments, writing is not asking life for more. It is responding to the life that has already happened.

Finally, prayer may explain why we write with such seriousness, but it cannot serve as evidence that what we have written is true. An essay may begin in pain, hope, or care, but its argument must still withstand scrutiny. Sincerity does not make a conclusion true, and an explanation does not become valid merely because it comforts us. Conversely, the willingness to delete a claim that offers great comfort but cannot survive examination may be the clearest expression of a writer’s honesty.

Of course, it is also a mistake to sanctify writing into a mission one is never allowed to stop. Writing does not require us to suffer continuously, to produce words every day, or to turn every experience into a work.

What moves me most about the metaphor, then, is that it places limitation and hope in the same sentence: I cannot guarantee understanding, yet I remain willing to understand; I cannot guarantee that I will be heard, yet I remain willing to speak carefully; I cannot guarantee that I am right, yet I am willing to let what I write receive an answer.

It does not say, “May the world prove me right.” It says: may I be able to see what does not conform to my wishes and still write about it honestly; may these words reach others without prescribing their answers for them.

Volume II: Living as an End

Volume I asked how a person might avoid being reduced to an instrument by institutions and technology. Volume II brings the question back into the interior of life. Death, loneliness, love, forgiveness, education, freedom, and power are not problems a single concept can solve once and for all. These essays move closer to the places where choices are made: how we face a past that cannot be relived, how we live with others, and how we preserve a genuine capacity to change course within new technologies and public orders. Living as an end is not a permanent state one attains. It is the repeated refusal, in concrete action, to reduce oneself or another person to a use.

Part I: Inner Life

Hope without expectation does not mean losing all hope for life. It means refusing to bind oneself to a predetermined outcome. From there, this part enters questions of death, loneliness, love, regret, forgiveness, and the direction of a life. Inner life is no simpler than the public world. Here too, we can treat ourselves as projects in need of constant optimization and other people as roles meant to fill our absences. There is no quick method for acquiring peace in these pages, only a slower work of discernment: what is fear, what is attachment, and what kind of life are we willing to bear?

1. Hope Without Expectation

A friend asked what I meant by “hope without expectation.” It means that when we stop pinning our hopes on a particular outcome, we no longer collapse into despair. Without despair, we can put our feet on the ground. And precisely then, what we are doing begins to carry genuine hope.

2. If No One Knew, Would We Still Remain Moral?

A friend asked an old question. Suppose you could do something wrong and no one would ever discover or punish it. Could most people resist? Or does much of what we call morality depend on the fear of being caught?

The question is indeed old. So old that Plato presents an identical one in Book II of The Republic.

The story goes like this. After an earthquake splits the ground, a shepherd finds a golden ring in the fissure. He discovers that when he turns its setting inward, he becomes invisible, and when he turns it back, he reappears. He finds a way into the palace, seduces the queen, conspires with her to kill the king, and takes the throne himself.

The story is told by Glaucon, one of Socrates' interlocutors. When he finishes, he poses a challenge. Suppose there were two such rings, one given to a just person and the other to an unjust one. Do you think the just person would remain just? Glaucon says no. No one could hold out. Justice is therefore never something people freely desire; they observe it only because they have no choice.

Twenty-four centuries later, not a word of the question has changed. Plato wrote the whole of The Republic in answer to it.

My answer to my friend came in three sentences: wanting to, but not daring; wanting to, but choosing not to; never wanting to at all.

Another friend later remarked that the question itself reveals a difference in experience. Those who refrain only because they are afraid cannot make those who never want to do it feel what they feel, and vice versa.

I think that is half right. The difference is real, but it is asymmetrical, and the direction of the asymmetry is the opposite of what most people assume.

To explain why, I first need to place those three sentences within a structure.

In my Self-as-an-End (SAE) philosophy, the emergence of a person proceeds through a series of cuts, each of which marks a level. Three of those cuts matter here, from 13DD to 15DD. The full derivation appears in my general methodology; I will use only these three levels here (https://self-as-an-end.net/papers/methodology.html).

13DD, the self. It is not merely that something is operating; I am operating. Negation turns back upon itself, and the “I” appears. This level forms the boundary between nature and freedom. Before it, everything can be fully described by natural science. After it, there is an “I.”

14DD, the self as an end. One does not act without reasons. Action acquires a reason, and the question “why” appears. This is the apex of the individual level: treating oneself as an end in oneself.

15DD, recognition of the other. I am not the only end. Another person is recognized as an end in themselves.

The word “recognition” matters here. It is not the same as “seeing.” Seeing and acknowledging are two different acts. SAE judgment has only two variables: what the other person actually possesses, and what you are able to see. Failing to see what is invisible to you is not immoral. Seeing what you ought to see but refusing to acknowledge it is no longer a problem of vision. Recognition of the other means acknowledging what one has seen.

Something even more important must also be noted. The relation among these three levels is additive, not substitutive. Recognizing another person as an end does not mean ceasing to regard oneself as an end. It means that there is now more than one end. If someone surrenders themselves in order to make another person their end, that is not movement upward but regression. SAE has only one moral law: one must oppose the suppression of any person's remainder, and “any person” includes oneself.

One more thing must be made clear, or the next three sections will be read backward.

In SAE, each person gives themselves the law and obeys their own law. This holds at all three levels, not only at the highest.

The law arises from within. Its content takes the form of the level to which you have developed. Alignment with your own law is morality; deviation from it is immorality. What follows, then, is not a story in which some people possess morality and others do not. It is a story of three different laws, each sincerely held by the person to whom it belongs.

Now we can ask the original question again.

Wanting to, but not daring. The thought arises, and what stops it is the pair of eyes outside.

That does not mean this person has no law. They have one, but its content includes the structure that gives them meaning, and that structure is external. When the structure is absent, when those eyes are truly closed, the law has nothing left to say.

This is not weakness of will. It is simply the form the law takes at that level.

Wanting to, but choosing not to. The thought still arises, but what stops it is not whether someone else can see. The person has ends of their own. Doing the wrong thing would damage something they genuinely want or harm the person they are becoming. Whether anyone is watching therefore ceases to be a variable. This person has passed through the stage of not daring and now chooses not to act. The two may look similar, but the reason has changed. It no longer runs from the outside in, but from the inside out.

Yet we should describe this accurately. There is still a pair of eyes watching the self, and those eyes are keeping accounts. The calculation asks whether this act adds to or subtracts from me.

And the account can produce another result.

What if the wrongdoing serves my end? What if it advances precisely what I truly want? The law at this level does not forbid that possibility. It may even require it. The person then begins to fight with themselves, and both sides of the fight are their own. One side says, “This will destroy me.” The other says, “This is exactly what I want.” No third party can adjudicate, because plaintiff and defendant are the same person.

That is where inner conflict begins. It is not indecision. It is a structural feature of this level.

Most people stop here. That is why the original question is a genuine and painful question for most people. At this level, the answer really can go either way.

Never wanting to at all. What happens here is not a stronger act of resistance or a more careful calculation. The thought does not arise. This does not mean it has never arisen. It means there is no longer a need to entertain it, because during the earlier stages of not daring and choosing not to, the person has already thought it through.

In their eyes, the other person is an end, not a variable in their ledger. The account is never opened. The lawsuit has no opposing sides. The option does not appear on the list, and there is nothing to restrain.

This does not mean the person no longer has ends of their own. Those ends remain fully present. What has changed is that there is now something else in the room that does not enter their ledger.

There is another point especially vulnerable to misreading. This person does not act this way because an external norm demands it. No one is ordering them, and no book is policing them. Their own law has taken this form. Having arrived there, they cannot do otherwise.

This has nothing to do with nobility. Water boils at one hundred degrees Celsius. Boiling is not an extra virtue of water. At that temperature, that is simply the state water is in.

The original question therefore measures not the strength of one's morality but what the questioner thinks morality is. It assumes that morality is a process of resisting temptation. That assumption holds only at the first two levels.

And, to be honest, the skeptical view identifies the mechanism correctly. It merely mistakes its nature.

At the first level, what restrains you is the pair of eyes outside. At the second, it is your own ledger. Both levels are indeed calculating costs. The only difference is where the cost lands. The skeptic is right about that.

But the account itself is the law of each of those levels, given and obeyed by the person themselves. To say that it does not count as morality is to measure them by the law of another level. In SAE, measuring others by one's own law has a name: ethics, not morality.

Only at the third level does calculation disappear, because there is no account to settle.

At that level, the question is not more difficult. It no longer makes sense.

Look again at the ring. It is actually a very precise instrument.

It does only one thing: it subtracts “being seen” from the equation. Whatever remains after that subtraction is the true content of your law.

When a person at the first level puts on the ring, what has been subtracted is the entire content of their law, so nothing remains. This is the level Glaucon describes.

When a person at the second level puts it on, one item is removed from the ledger. The account remains, but it becomes harder to balance. The ring does not liberate this person. It turns up the volume of the inner conflict.

When a person at the third level puts it on, nothing happens. Visibility never entered the equation. Subtracting a term that was never present leaves the equality unchanged.

Glaucon's challenge sounds irrefutable because he assumes everyone occupies the same level. He says no one can hold out. At the level he assumes, that is true.

By this point, you may already have had a reaction of your own. The easiest thing to recognize is the inner conflict: the feeling of being pulled back and forth between “This will destroy me” and “This is exactly what I want.” If you recognize it, you know where you are. If you read the words “the account is never opened” and think they are merely a performance of virtue, that too is a coordinate. No one needs to tell you.

One aside: these three names are coordinates, not labels. They do not describe three kinds of people. The same person may recognize another as an end in one matter and remain at the stage of wanting to but not daring in another. In SAE, good and evil describe direction, not position. There are no finally condemned evil people, only diagnosable operations. So do not rush to paste a label on yourself or anyone else.

My friend then asked a second question. Can people move gradually upward from the lower level, or is the threshold fixed at birth?

Not only can they move; they must begin at the first level. There is no way to skip it.

The reason is structural, not inspirational. Each level is produced by making a cut in the preceding one. Without an “I,” there is no self that can become an end. Without treating oneself as an end, there can be no “also” in the sentence “You are also an end.” The third level is cut from the material of the second. Without the material, the cut cannot be made.

There is a second reason, and it is even more decisive.

Because the law arises from within, it can only be reached; it cannot be given. A law handed to you by someone else is not your law. Even if its content is identical word for word, what you have received is a sentence, not the law itself. A shortcut is not merely difficult. No such route exists, because there is no channel through which this arrival can be transmitted.

What skipping produces, then, is not recognition of the other. It produces someone who has memorized the lines of recognition. They say the right things, and the reason they say them is fear that they will be caught saying the wrong things.

The appearance belongs to the third level; the interior remains at the first. This is why moral indoctrination always fails. You can insert the conclusion, but not the process of arrival. SAE calls it colonization when someone claims to arrive for another person and then inserts the conclusion. Waiting for a person to unfold until the law appears of its own accord requires no instruction; this is cultivation.

Yet the fact that everyone must begin at the first level does not mean everyone will reach the third. This is a process with no guarantee of completion. Some people remain at one level for their entire lives, and remaining there is not failure. Each level generates its own law from within, and that law is genuine morality at that level. Alignment with the law of one's own level is morality; departure from it is immorality. Which level one occupies is a separate question.

We can now return to the claim that the two sides cannot understand each other's experience.

The symmetry is false.

From above, there is understanding, because one has traveled through what lies below. From the third level, you can see the moral phenomena of every preceding level. You can describe the law of the first and of the second because your field of vision contains them. The person who no longer entertains the wrongdoing knows perfectly well what it feels like to want to but not dare. That is where they began.

From below, the problem is not failure to understand but invisibility. It is not that one has failed to look closely enough. From that position, there is nothing there to see. In the same way, a person at the third level cannot see the law of a fourth level because they have not reached it.

This is why the two groups cannot converse, and why their failures of conversation differ. The person who has made the journey knows what the other is saying. The person who has not arrived does not merely disagree. The thing being described does not exist within their field of vision.

This invisibility is structural, not a defect. Not having arrived is not evil. The second level is not bad. It is simply not the third.

But the next sentence must be said, or this essay becomes a license for arrogance.

Structural invisibility is real. Using “you cannot see it” as a shield is something else.

Once the claim that those below cannot see what is above has been established, it becomes an extraordinarily convenient weapon. Anyone facing criticism can say, “You fail to understand because your level is too low.” The statement cannot be falsified, and it ends the questioning at once. Using a judgment of levels to exempt oneself from scrutiny has a name in SAE: a structure pretending to be a law. That is colonization itself.

Judgment still has only two variables: what the other person actually possesses, and what you can see. If you see what ought to be seen and refuse to acknowledge it, you are lying. That is not a question of levels.

Nor would someone genuinely at the third level use hierarchy to silence another person's questions. Their law requires them to oppose the suppression of any person's remainder, including the person questioning them. To use level as a means of suppressing a critic is precisely an act of falling away from the third level.

One final point.

This essay does not ask anyone to go anywhere.

SAE has only one moral law: one must oppose the suppression of any person's remainder. That law is directed at oneself, not at others. It is not bestowed by one person upon another. Each person arrives at it for themselves. Its direction is universal, because it concerns any person, but its binding force falls only upon the person who holds it. You cannot use it to demand that another person act in the same way. The instant you do, your morality has been projected outward as ethics, and ethics is precisely what this framework seeks to dismantle.

All I can do, then, is lay it out here. If your law resonates with it, that is your own remainder unfolding, not the result of my persuading you. If it does not resonate, then let each continue to resonate in its own way. No one has lost anything.

3. Why Do We Understand the Principle Yet Still Fail to Act?

A friend asked why we can understand all the principles yet still fail to act on them. Why is the unity of knowledge and action so difficult?

This too is an old question. Someone asked exactly the same thing five hundred years ago, and the questioner was no outsider. He was Xu Ai, one of Wang Yangming's closest students.

At the very beginning of the Instructions for Practical Living, Xu Ai is arguing with others about the unity of knowledge and action. Unable to settle the debate, he goes to his teacher. His example is this: everyone today knows that one should be filial to one's father and respectful to one's elder brother, yet people fail to be filial or respectful. Knowledge and action must therefore plainly be two different things.

Yangming's answer is brief: such people have already been cut off by selfish desires; this is not knowledge and action in their original form. There has never been someone who knows but does not act. To know and not act is simply not yet to know.

At first glance, this looks like wordplay. You say that you understand but cannot do it; he says that means you do not understand. There seems to be no reply, because he has simply removed your premise.

But the examples he gives next are not games.

He cites the Great Learning: “loving a beautiful sight as one naturally loves beauty, and hating a foul odor as one naturally hates a foul odor.” Seeing beauty belongs to knowledge; loving it belongs to action. At the very moment one sees it, one already loves it. One does not first see it and then separately form an intention to love it. Smelling a foul odor belongs to knowledge; hating it belongs to action. At the moment one smells it, one already hates it.

Then comes the person with a blocked nose. Though a foul odor may be before him, he has not smelled it, so he does not particularly hate it. He simply does not yet know the odor.

Yangming gives an even more concrete formulation: to know pain, one must already have felt pain; to know cold, one must already have been cold; to know hunger, one must already have been hungry.

Notice the crucial phrase: one does not first see it and then separately form an intention to love it.

Forming an intention is the step we ordinarily assume: first we know, then we decide to act. Yangming says genuine knowledge contains no such step.

The question then becomes: What exactly is that step? Why is it sometimes indispensable and at other times entirely unnecessary?

A principle is universal, while the situation before you is particular. A principle does not arrive with instructions for use. No principle comes with a manual explaining what it requires here and now. There is a distance between a universal sentence and the particular matter before us.

What travels that distance is judgment.

Kant wrote specifically about this. He argued that in cognition as in morality, judgment borrows its a priori principles rather than possessing principles of its own. It therefore cannot be taught. It can only be exercised and practiced, and much of it may even be a natural gift.

That sentence is worth pausing over. Principles can be taught; judgment cannot. Yet judgment is exactly what stands between a principle and an action.

So when you understand but cannot act, the problem is not that you understood and then became lazy. The sentence in your hand has not yet become a usable judgment.

Placed within a structure, this becomes much clearer.

In the previous essay, I described three levels in the SAE philosophical framework (https://self-as-an-end.net/papers/methodology.html).

13DD, the self. It is not merely that something is operating; I am operating.

14DD, the self as an end. One does not act without reasons. Action acquires reasons, and one treats oneself as an end in oneself.

15DD, recognition of the other. I am not the only end. Another person is recognized as an end in themselves.

The third formulation is, in fact, Kant's famous principle: persons are ends in themselves and must never be treated merely as means. SAE follows Kant throughout, and especially at this level.

Judgment takes three entirely different forms across these levels.

At the first level, a person hears the principle but does not understand it, so no judgment can be made.

They can repeat the sentence and may sincerely accept it. But in a situation where no one points out what to do, they do not know what the sentence requires. If someone tells them explicitly, they can comply. Where nothing is specified, there is a blank.

The principle is an instruction handed in from outside, not a reason that has grown from within. They can observe it in the situations covered by the instruction. Beyond those situations, it has nothing to say.

This is not weakness of will. The judgment has not yet been made, so there is nothing to carry through.

At the second level, the person understands and can make the judgment, but more than one judgment appears, and the judgments fight each other.

They do not merely understand the wisdom of going to bed early; they can explain it to others. But those two hours awake at night are the only hours in the day that belong to no one else.

Both sides are things they genuinely want. Both sides are their own. No third party can adjudicate, because plaintiff and defendant are the same person.

This level contains a great deal of inner conflict for precisely that reason. It is not a matter of strength or weakness of will. This is simply what the level looks like.

And there is something still more troublesome hidden within it: understanding itself can be entered in the ledger.

At this level, “I am a person who understands this principle” is itself an asset, and one that can be redeemed without paying the cost of action. You read a sentence that feels profoundly right, and the comfort in that instant is genuine. The reward has already been paid. Your saved items multiply, your bookshelves fill, and you do not move.

Yangming wrote about this five hundred years ago: people divide knowledge and action into two things. They think they must first know and only afterward act. “For now,” they say, “I will study and discuss, doing the work of knowing. Once my knowledge is genuine, I will do the work of acting.” As a result, they never act throughout their lives, and throughout their lives they never know.

Most people remain at this level for a long time. That is why the question is real and genuinely painful for most of us.

At the third level, there is no need to form that separate intention.

Once another person is recognized as an end, judgments of this kind become immediate and do not pass through a ledger. The other person is not a variable in my account, so there is no calculation to make and no two sides to fight.

To be precise, this does not mean judgment has become stronger. It means that one category of things that once required calculation no longer needs to be calculated. Other difficult judgments remain difficult. When the needs of two people collide, or when my remainder collides with another person's, the work of cutting and discerning may still take a very long time.

The distinction among the three levels, then, is not strength of will. It is three states of judgment: unable to make one, making several that conflict, and no longer needing to make one.

Now return to Yangming's diagnosis, and something interesting appears.

He says selfish desires have cut us off. Those words precisely describe the second level. The other side of the ledger contains another end of mine, and it blocks this one.

But his own example of the person with a blocked nose describes something else. That person is not blocked by selfish desire. He simply cannot smell. He has not arrived; he has not been obstructed. That is the first level.

Being blocked and not having arrived are two different conditions. In the former, the thing is present but pressed down by something else. In the latter, it has not yet grown. Both are real, but they are not the same.

Yangming saw both. He simply named the whole matter after the second.

One question remains: Why does “understanding” feel so much like understanding?

Because from within, the two experiences feel identical. Receiving a sentence and arriving at a law produce the same inner flash of brightness. No internal test can distinguish them.

The only test lies outside.

Yangming states it plainly. If we say that someone knows filiality or knows fraternal respect, that person must already have practiced filiality or fraternal respect. Surely merely knowing how to talk about them is not enough to say that the person knows them.

The unity of knowledge and action is therefore not a goal to be achieved. It is a measuring instrument. When knowledge and action separate, what the instrument reveals is not weak will. It reveals that the knowledge has not yet arrived.

One misunderstanding should be prevented here. This does not mean reading is useless.

A principle is a structure, something left behind after another person has done the work of cutting. A structure is not waste. It is material. Yangming never said knowledge was unnecessary. He said knowledge is the beginning of action.

The mistake is not reading. The mistake is taking possession of the material for arrival itself. Yangming's full sentence has two halves: knowledge is the beginning of action; action is the completion of knowledge. Take only the first half and you get the condition he described, a lifetime without action.

One final thing.

After reading this essay, you may feel that you understand it. That “understanding” is exactly the kind this essay has been discussing.

This essay cannot give you anything. It can do only one thing: remove a shortcut you thought existed. The remaining route is clumsy. You must walk it yourself, making judgments in particular situations, getting them wrong, and trying again. That is what Kant meant when he said judgment can be practiced but not taught.

So I can only lay the argument here. As in the previous essay, if your law resonates with it, that is your own remainder unfolding, not the result of my persuading you. If it does not resonate, let each continue to resonate in its own way. No one has lost anything.

4. Why Do We Fear Death?

The question usually carries an assumption: fear of death is one thing, the same thing feared by everyone.

But death itself has no content. No one has experienced it and returned to tell us what it is like. Strictly speaking, then, people do not fear death. They fear what death interrupts.

And what it interrupts depends entirely on what we are holding.

Twenty-three centuries ago, Epicurus offered a famous solution. Death, he said, is nothing to us. When we exist, death is not present; when death arrives, we no longer exist. If we and death can never meet, what reason could we have to fear it?

The argument is coherent, clean, and almost impossible to fault. Yet twenty-three centuries have passed, and no one has ceased to fear simply because they read it.

Why does a logically impeccable argument have so little effect in life? That is what this essay is about.

Let us return to the three levels used in the previous two essays (https://self-as-an-end.net/papers/methodology.html).

13DD, the self. It is not merely that something is operating; I am operating.

14DD, the self as an end. One does not act without reasons. One treats oneself as an end in oneself.

15DD, recognition of the other. I am not the only end. Another person is recognized as an end in themselves.

Place the fear of death within these three levels, and three entirely different fears appear.

At the first level, one fears the unknown.

There is already an “I,” but no end that must be pursued has yet taken shape. What the person holds remains formless. Death is therefore not “the interruption of something of mine.” It is simply darkness.

What they fear is the darkness itself. They do not know what lies in it, or whether an “I” will remain there.

This is the most primordial and most universal fear. It needs no reason. Anyone who becomes conscious of an “I” passes through it.

It is also the strongest of the three. The other two fears have particular objects, and where there is a particular object, something can still be said. This fear has no object. There is nothing to discuss.

At the second level, one fears that the account will never be settled.

The person at this level has ends, and they are their own rather than ends inserted by someone else. They know where they are going.

Death therefore becomes something concrete: I have not arrived.

The book is unfinished. The child has not grown up. I have not gone to that place or said those words. What one fears is not darkness, but interruption, the unchecked items remaining on the list.

Most people live at this level, so most discussions of death are really discussions of this fear.

This level also contains a pain absent from the first: the clearer a person's ends, the more specific the fear. Someone who knows what they are trying to do knows more clearly what they stand to lose than someone who has not yet decided.

The first fear has not disappeared. It has merely been covered by something closer and easier to name. At night, when the nearer concern falls silent, the earlier fear can emerge again.

At the third level, one fears that a road will not be walked to its end.

Once another person is recognized as an end, something becomes unavoidable: cultivation.

Cultivation is not instruction, nor is it giving someone a conclusion. It is accompanying a particular person for part of their own road, allowing them to grow and arrive for themselves.

Cultivation has a quality nothing else possesses: it cannot be handed over.

Tasks can be transferred. Money can be left behind. What one has understood can be written down and passed on. But cultivation unfolds over a long period, has a particular person as its object, and is constituted by the two of you together. If you are gone and someone else takes your place, it is not the same cultivation. It is another passage, beginning again from the start.

The third fear takes this form: I am partway along several roads with others, and no one can finish walking my part for me.

No argument can dissolve this fear. It is no answer to say someone else will take over, because the person who takes over is someone else. Nor is it enough to say that I did my best. What one cannot but do belongs to a different dimension from how much effort one has made.

Two things need further clarification.

First, the third level does not cease to fear what the second fears. One's own ends remain, as does the fear of leaving them unfinished. The levels are additive, not substitutive. One fear is not exchanged for another; a new fear is added. And the new one is more difficult because it is not entirely in one's own hands.

Second, and more easily misunderstood, being unable not to turn toward another person and cultivate them has nothing to do with nobility.

What one cannot but do differs from what one is willing, glad, or selfless enough to do. A willing person can choose not to be willing. That is virtue, and it deserves praise. What one cannot but do offers no alternative. It resembles the fact that water boils at one hundred degrees Celsius. Boiling is not a virtue of water.

These three levels are therefore not three spiritual attainments. A person at the third level does not fear death less. If anything, they have more to fear.

Now return to Epicurus. His sentence is actually a precise instrument.

It turns the fact that “I will not be present” from bad news into a reason.

At the first level, it works. The fear concerns the darkness, and Epicurus says that you will not be there, will have no experience, and therefore no darkness will be waiting for you. The unknown is canceled. Epicurus has indeed comforted many people over more than two thousand years, and this is the level he has comforted.

At the second level, the sentence does nothing. An unsettled account has nothing to do with whether I am present. An unfinished book remains unfinished whether I am there or not. The argument touches no part of the fear.

At the third level, the sentence works in reverse. Far from offering comfort, it names the content of the fear exactly. It is because I will not be present when death arrives that I cannot finish walking that road. The premise with which Epicurus cancels fear is the very cause of fear at the third level.

The same sentence produces three results: it helps, it does nothing, and it strikes the fear at its center.

That is why an argument with no obvious logical flaw may have almost no effect in life. It is not wrong. It speaks to one level, while its listeners do not all occupy that level.

Return to the original question.

“Why do people fear death?” may not be precise enough. A more useful question is: Which fear is ours?

Is it the darkness, the unfinished list, or several roads left halfway walked?

They differ so profoundly that words capable of comforting one may do nothing for another, or may even intensify it.

Nor are they three rungs to be climbed. As I have said, the person at the third level may have more fears, not fewer.

This essay cannot make us stop fearing death. Perhaps no essay can.

It asks us to face the fear. Fear of death is not itself frightening. It is evidence that a person has possessed something. We fear losing only what we hold. The first level holds a newly emerged “I.” The second holds one's own ends. The third also holds the ends of others. The more one holds, the more one fears losing. There is nothing shameful in that.

What is frightening is not knowing what we fear. When we do not know, fear remains undifferentiated. An undifferentiated fear cannot be faced or worked through. It can only be pressed down.

Once we know which fear is ours, we can at least ask the next question: What should I do now?

The answer differs for every person, and each can answer only for themselves.

5. How Can We Know That We Are Right?

A friend asked, “How can I know that what I know is right?”

The blunt answer is: you cannot.

Of course, that answer is a little lazy and evasive. It is too easy to say, and the person who says it gets to appear more lucid than the person who does not. Humility may be the easiest thing in the world to perform.

So once that sentence has been spoken, the work has only begun. There are really two questions: Why can we not know, and what do we do after admitting that we cannot?

First, consider a sentence that has long been misquoted.

The line most widely attributed to Socrates is “The only thing I know is that I know nothing.” He did not actually say it. The closest passage in Plato's Apology says that the small advantage Socrates possessed over others was simply that when he did not know something, he did not suppose that he knew it.

The two statements are far apart.

“I know that I know nothing” is a beautiful pose. It ends as soon as it is spoken, and it is a paradox that sounds clever. “Do not suppose that you know what you do not know” is unattractive and offers nothing to boast about. It is a discipline, an act that must be performed again whenever one encounters a particular matter.

The admission that one may be wrong most easily turns into the first sentence.

Why, then, can we not test our own correctness from within?

Because certainty and correctness feel the same.

When we examine an idea inwardly, we use the same apparatus that produced it. The instrument and the object measured are identical. An idea makes sense to us precisely because it fits our other ideas. That pleasurable feeling of everything locking into place is what we call having thought something through.

But falsehood can also lock into place. A completely coherent view may contain no internal contradiction from beginning to end while being crooked as a whole. Coherence guarantees only that the parts do not fight. It does not guarantee that the whole corresponds to anything outside itself.

There is another, more troublesome layer. The more earnestly we reflect on ourselves, the more likely we may be performing earnest reflection. The thought “I am someone capable of self-examination” is itself comforting. Once that comfort has been paid, the examination can stop.

At this point, we hit a wall.

If we cannot know whether what we know is right, how do we know that the statement “we cannot know whether we are right” is itself right?

If it is right, then we know at least one thing, and it does not fully hold. If it is wrong, it collapses at the outset.

There is no way through this wall. Every form of skepticism has struck it.

All I can do is acknowledge that the sentence lies within its own range of fire. I take it as a starting point not because it can survive the objection, but because I have not found a more honest place to begin.

And a claim that includes itself is at least a little closer to honesty than one that exempts itself. Any statement announcing “this law applies to everyone but me” usually has a larger problem.

We have hit the wall, but the matter does not end there.

The wall blocks only one direction: looking from within toward the interior.

No exit inside does not mean no exit at all. The exit lies outside.

We cannot become aware of what lies beyond our knowledge unless someone questions us.

That sentence is the center of this essay. The next several sections explain why it holds.

Another person may see what we cannot. This is not because they are smarter or more correct. They occupy a different position.

Here is the crucial point: our blind spots are not random.

Any view that explains the world necessarily neglects some part of it. What it neglects does not raise its hand. It is simply, quietly absent. In the previous essays, I called this the remainder.

The position of the remainder is determined by the shape of the view itself. No amount of effort from within can illuminate it, because the light with which we search is produced by the very same apparatus.

Another person is different. They have their own view and their own remainder, but their remainder does not occupy the same place as ours. They can therefore see what we omit, just as we may see what they omit.

Being questioned is therefore not a matter of magnanimity, a virtue required only of well-cultivated people. It is the only means of testing. There is no other.

Twenty-four centuries ago, Plato wrote about this in the Gorgias, more directly than almost any account in circulation today.

Speaking to Gorgias, Socrates says that he is the sort of person who is glad to be refuted when he is wrong and glad to refute another person when that person is wrong. Between the two, however, he would rather be the one refuted. To be refuted is to rid oneself of an error; to refute someone else merely rids that person of an error. Removing something bad from oneself brings the greater benefit.

The position of this passage also matters. Socrates says it while deciding whether the conversation should continue. If you are this kind of person too, he tells Gorgias, let us go on. If not, let us stop here.

The ability to accept refutation is treated as a precondition of dialogue itself.

There is another scene in the same dialogue. Polus tries to refute Socrates by calling witnesses, insisting that any number of respectable Athenians would support him. Socrates replies that this is the courtroom style of refutation, which relies on numbers and is useless for truth. He wants only one witness: the person standing before him and actually engaged in the argument. Everyone else counts for nothing.

One person presents a crowd as evidence. The other says a crowd is no evidence at all; only the person who has truly entered the argument matters.

But fairness requires a qualification, and it is more useful to us than the principle by itself.

The Gorgias announces that Socrates would rather be refuted, yet almost the entire dialogue consists of Socrates refuting other people. By the end, Callicles simply stops engaging and offers perfunctory replies.

A dialogue that declares its willingness to be refuted never once allows its author to be refuted.

This is not an attempt to catch Plato out. It is evidence of how difficult the principle is to practice. Stating it is easy. Many people have stated it over twenty-four centuries. Callicles' final refusal to engage is actually the most respectable way of blocking it.

Follow this line a little further, and an uncomfortable conclusion appears.

Another person's agreement does not really help me.

From where I stand, I cannot distinguish why they agree. Perhaps they genuinely checked the reasoning. Perhaps they share my remainder. Perhaps they did not check at all and simply found the statement pleasing. All three look identical to me. In none of the three cases am I required to walk the path again. Nothing moves.

Agreement is comforting, but it carries no information.

Disagreement, by contrast, may bring something.

A disagreement that touches the content forces me down one of two roads. Either I discover that I was wrong, which is a gain, or I walk the path of reasoning again and find that it still holds. Only after that second journey does the sentence “I may be right” acquire its first measure of weight.

Notice that it remains only a possibility. Surviving one question does not make a claim right. It means only that this question did not overturn it.

Three misunderstandings should be prevented.

First, questioning is not voting. A large number of people agreeing does not make something right. When a crowd shares the same view, it shares the same remainder. No number of people can illuminate the part their view excludes. What matters is not the number of people, but the difference in position.

Second, not every disagreement carries information. A disagreement that never touches the content leaves as little altered as agreement does, though it is more unpleasant. This judgment is slippery, however, because it can be used to dismiss anyone. It is suitable only for examining oneself, not for throwing back at another person.

Third, this does not mean we must change whenever someone questions us. The value of questioning is not that the other person must be right. It is that we are forced to walk the road again. We may change by the end, or we may not. But not changing after walking it is no longer the same as not changing before being questioned.

Near the end of the Tractatus Logico-Philosophicus, Wittgenstein makes a strange remark. Whoever understands him, he says, will finally recognize the propositions of the book as nonsensical. The reader must use them as steps in a ladder, climb beyond them, and then throw the ladder away.

Near the end of a book, the author tells you that it is a ladder and should be discarded after use.

What happened later is even more telling. Wittgenstein eventually overturned much of that book and wrote something entirely different.

Publicly rejecting one's earlier work is rare not because ideas never change, but because handing over one's words and allowing them to be cut into, even by one's later self, is costly.

How, then, can we know whether we are genuinely accepting questions or merely appearing to accept them?

Again, the inner state cannot be tested. Only the action can.

When questioned, is your first impulse to cut into the issue or to block it?

Blocking takes many forms, and they usually sound reasonable: You do not understand. You lack the qualifications. Your motives are impure. You have taken me out of context. You did not even finish reading. Every one of these claims may be true.

But they share one feature. They stop the question without allowing the content to move.

Genuine openness to questioning looks much simpler. It means returning to the specific issue and looking again. Even if the conclusion remains “I still think this,” the intervening journey has taken place.

Return to the original question.

Perhaps what we can ask for is not to be right, because rightness cannot be verified from within.

What we can ask for is to remain open to questioning.

The difference is larger than it appears. A truth that cannot be questioned and a falsehood are structurally the same kind of thing: neither will change again. The former does not change because no one can touch it. The latter does not change because no one does. A claim that can be questioned, even if it is wrong at this moment, at least remains in a position from which it can move toward what is right.

If we do not know whether we are right, why continue speaking and writing?

Precisely because we do not know.

An idea kept inside can never be tested. The longer it remains there, the smoother and truer it feels. Only when it is spoken, written down, and handed over does it first become available for another person to touch.

We do not write because we have finished thinking. We write so that the thought can be handed over and checked.

This essay does not claim to know that it is right.

The only thing it can do is state itself clearly enough for you to find where it is wrong.

6. Why Does “I Only Want What's Best for You” Feel So Uncomfortable?

A friend asked why hearing “I only want what's best for you” feels so uncomfortable. The other person clearly means well, yet our instinct is to retreat. Afterward, we may even feel guilty and wonder whether we are simply ungrateful.

First, look at what the sentence itself does.

It appears to be a description of the speaker's motive. But when it reaches the listener, it does something else.

It changes the nature of refusal.

Before the sentence is spoken, disagreement concerns the content: Is this advice right? Is it appropriate for me? Do I have other considerations?

Afterward, disagreement is no longer only about the content. It becomes a judgment about the person: you have betrayed someone's goodwill.

The content no longer needs to be examined. The question of whether the advice is right has been replaced by the question of whether the motive is good. The motive is good, so the person who refuses becomes the ungrateful one.

The discomfort is therefore accurate. What we feel is not offense but obstruction. We have been blocked.

Placed within a structure, the people who use this sentence are not all the same.

At the first level, the self:

People at this level care about others, and the care is genuine. But this is the language they have. Care sounded like this when they were growing up. Other people said the same words when they were being kind to them. So they learn to say it too, without calculation or any wish to control.

They may not even understand what could be wrong with the sentence. To them, it simply means “I care about you.”

At the second level, the self as an end:

Here things become more complicated, and we must distinguish two cases.

In the first, the person is sincere. Helping you in this matter has become one of their own ends, written in their own ledger.

That is exactly the difficulty: when we refuse, we stand in their way. We are not blocking someone else's road. We are blocking an end of their own. So they push harder, with complete confidence in their right to do so, because they genuinely are thinking of us. No one knows that better than they do.

In the second case, the sentence is borrowed. What the person truly wants is something else: our obedience, our compliance with their arrangement, freedom from having to face a choice we have made for ourselves, or simply the outcome they prefer. “I want what's best for you” is the easiest wrapping.

The second case is more common than most people imagine.

But the more difficult point comes next: from the outside, the two cannot be distinguished.

The words are the same. So are the expression and the insistence. We have no way to determine from outside which kind of person stands before us.

Nor can the speaker reliably distinguish the motives from within. The previous essay argued that certainty and correctness feel the same. In the same way, genuinely wanting what is best for you feels exactly like needing you to do what I say while sincerely believing that I want what is best for you.

The sentence works precisely because the two cannot be distinguished. It has the same effect whether the person is sincere or not: it turns refusal into ingratitude. That makes it an excellent tool, so effective that the person using it may not know they are using a tool at all.

At the third level, recognition of the other:

This level does not mean refusing to care.

Recognizing another person as an end means helping and guiding them. Indifference would treat them as something unrelated to oneself.

But at this level, the sentence is not spoken.

Not because the person is especially refined, but because speaking it would be self-contradictory. They need the other person to be able to refuse, to question, and to say, “I do not accept your advice.” The phrase “I only want what's best for you” is exactly what takes that capacity away.

Once the sentence is spoken, the very thing the speaker wanted disappears.

What, then, does it look like to help and guide without saying those words?

Socrates offers an image in Plato's Theaetetus.

He says that he is the son of the midwife Phaenarete and practices the same craft. He attends men rather than women, however, and cares for the labor of the soul rather than the body. A midwife eases labor pains and helps someone through a difficult birth. Socrates says the greatest part of the art is judging whether what has been born is true knowledge or only an illusion.

The crucial point is that he gives birth to nothing himself. He repeatedly says he has no wisdom of his own. The knowledge is not inserted by him; it was already being carried by the other person. He rarely even answers questions. He asks them.

A midwife never says, “I only want what's best for you.” She remains beside the person at the moment of greatest pain.

And this must be clear: delivering a child is not standing idly by. A person who does not care is not in the delivery room.

C. S. Lewis made a precise observation about the sincere forcefulness of the second level. Of all tyrannies, he suggested, the one sincerely exercised for the good of its victims may be the hardest to endure. Other tyrants eventually satisfy their greed. Those who torment you for your own good can continue forever because their consciences approve.

The mechanism is contained in that sentence: goodwill supplies no stopping condition.

When we do something for ourselves and go too far, embarrassment may eventually stop us. When we do it for another person's good, there is no brake. Every additional act can be interpreted as loving them a little more.

Kant offers a sentence that can be placed beside Lewis's. Paternal government, he says, is the greatest despotism imaginable because it treats people as children incapable of judging good and evil for themselves.

So what can we do, if motives cannot be distinguished, including our own?

The answer is the same as in the previous essay: do not try to distinguish motives. Distinguish actions.

One thing can be observed directly: Can the other person still refuse?

The question is not whether they dare to refuse. It is what happens afterward.

If they say no and the matter ends, while the care and the relationship remain, that is guidance. Whether they give birth is their affair; we remain beside them.

If they say no and what follows is escalating pressure, disappointment, “after everything I have done for you,” or “don't say I didn't warn you,” it is no longer guidance. We need them to give birth to the child we have chosen.

The virtue of this test is that it does not ask about motive. It does not require us to decide whether we are sincere, because we cannot make that judgment reliably. It asks only one thing: What happens when the other person says no?

One final point.

The sentence is most dangerous precisely because, when it is spoken, the speaker is usually sincere.

The next time it reaches our lips, perhaps we can first ask ourselves: What will I do if the other person says no?

If the answer is “I will push harder,” it may be better not to say the sentence at all.

7. Why Are We Still Lonely When Surrounded by People?

A friend asked why we can be surrounded by people and still feel lonely.

Half the answer is already hidden in the question. Loneliness is not the absence of people. It is the absence of that person.

The other half of the question is: Who is that person?

The answer differs from one person to another, and even for the same person at different times.

At the first level, the self:

An “I” has emerged, but it is still new and needs confirmation. The support this person needs falls within the idea of the self: you must help me feel that I am myself.

Loneliness at this level means that the people around me have no relation to who I am.

They are not bad, nor are they absent. But their presence does not make this “I” any more substantial. People talk, eat, and hold meetings around me. Everything appears normal. I sit among them feeling like something unrelated to it all.

This is the most common form of loneliness and the hardest to express, because from the outside nothing appears to be missing.

At the second level, the self as an end:

This person has ends of their own and knows that those ends are theirs. What they need therefore changes. They no longer need confirmation that they are themselves. They need someone to recognize what they are doing.

Loneliness at this level means that no one can recognize my end.

More precisely, no one truly understands what it is. The people around you may offer support and encouragement and tell you to keep going. Yet you know they are supporting something vague, not the particular thing you are actually doing.

The story of Boya describes this level. When Boya played the qin with lofty mountains in mind, Zhong Ziqi heard Mount Tai rising majestically. When Boya played with flowing water in mind, Ziqi heard the torrents of a great river. After Ziqi died, Boya smashed his instrument, broke its strings, and never played again, saying that no one remained in the world worth playing for.

Notice what Boya lacked. Not an audience. He had audiences in abundance and was the most celebrated musician of his time. He lacked the person who could say what he was playing.

Applause cannot provide that. It tells us someone is present, not that anyone has heard.

In the Nicomachean Ethics, Aristotle distinguishes three forms of friendship: friendship for utility, for pleasure, and for virtue. The first two, he says, are incidental, because what is loved is not the person but what the person provides. When that provision ends, the friendship dissolves. Only the third loves someone for who they are, and such friends help one another become better.

The first two kinds can fill a room with people. Loneliness at the second level is the experience of being surrounded by them.

At the third level, recognition of the other:

Once another person is recognized as an end, one cannot help engaging in cultivation, accompanying a particular person along part of their own road so that they may arrive for themselves.

The direction of loneliness now reverses.

The first two levels lack someone who can receive me. This level lacks someone who needs to be received.

One wants to cultivate, but there is nowhere to begin.

There are two ancient versions of this sentence, one Chinese and one Greek. They describe two sides of the same thing.

Begin with the Chinese version.

In the “Xu Wugui” chapter of the Zhuangzi, Zhuangzi passes Huizi's grave during a funeral procession and turns to tell his attendants a story.

A man of Ying once smeared a speck of white plaster on the tip of his nose, as thin as a fly's wing, and asked a craftsman named Shi to cut it away with an ax. Shi swung the ax with a rush of air and removed every trace of plaster in a single stroke without injuring the nose. The man of Ying stood motionless, without changing his expression.

Lord Yuan of Song heard of this and summoned Shi. “Do it for me,” he said.

Shi replied, “It is true that I could once do it. But the person who served as my material has been dead for a long time.”

Zhuangzi then says, “Since the Master died, I have had no material on which to work. I have no one with whom to speak.”

Three details deserve pause.

First, Shi had lost none of his skill. What he had lost was the person capable of standing still. This is not the loss of an ability but the loss of its object.

Second, Lord Yuan stood before him, actively volunteering, and he was a ruler, apparently the finest possible candidate. Shi refused. There was someone beside him, but that person was not his material. This is precisely the question my friend asked: Why do we remain lonely when surrounded by people?

Third, what did the man of Ying do? Nothing. He simply did not flinch. The ax came toward his nose, and he stood still.

Classical commentators gloss the word translated as “material” as the place upon which skill is exercised. “I have no material” literally means: I have nowhere to begin.

There is another layer. The person Zhuangzi mourned had contradicted him all his life. In their famous debate over the happiness of fish above the Hao River, Huizi never agreed with him once. The previous essay argued that agreement carries no information. Here stands a man at a grave, mourning his one true opponent.

The Greek version presents the other side of the same matter.

It is the cave in The Republic. Someone breaks free of the chains, leaves the cave, sees the sun, and then returns.

Socrates says that when the person first returns, his eyes have not adjusted to the darkness. He cannot see clearly and needs a long time to become accustomed again. If he is then made to compete with the prisoners in judging shadows, they will laugh at him. They will say that his journey upward ruined his eyes and was therefore not worth making.

Then comes the sentence: if they could seize and kill the person who tried to free them, do you imagine they would not?

Craftsman Shi lacks a nose. The returning prisoner lacks someone willing to open their eyes.

The first has no object. The second meets an object that refuses, and refuses violently. These are the two forms of loneliness at the third level.

Together they say the same thing: loneliness here is not the absence of people. It is the absence of someone willing to stand still.

The man of Ying, standing without changing expression, is the positive image. The prisoners who reach for a weapon are its reverse.

At this point, something must be made clear or this essay will become a certificate we award ourselves.

Loneliness is not evidence of one's level.

“The higher the level, the lonelier the person” is a wonderfully convenient consolation. I am lonely, therefore I must be high up.

The claim is false. Most loneliness is simply loneliness and says nothing about where a person has arrived. And under the additive principle, a person at the third level retains both earlier forms of loneliness while acquiring another.

The cave story closes off the comforting interpretation by itself. The person who returns is the worst performer in the cave. He cannot see the shadows and will lose the contest. To the prisoners, he is the person damaged by the world above. He is not the strongest in that environment, but the most inept.

Plato adds another observation. Dazzled eyes have two possible causes: moving from light into darkness, or from darkness into light. When you see someone looking bewildered, do not laugh immediately. First ask which transition they have made.

The same applies to us. When we see a lonely person, including the one in the mirror, we should not rush to decide which kind of loneliness it is.

One small difference is also worth noting.

In Plato, the philosopher returns to the cave because the city requires it. He is sent back from outside. In SAE, cultivation is not assigned by anyone. One's own law has grown into that form, and on arriving there one cannot do otherwise.

The outward action is the same. One comes from an external commission, the other from one's own law. This difference is exactly what this series of essays has been trying to distinguish.

Return to the original question.

Why are we lonely when surrounded by people?

Because people are not interchangeable. Loneliness never measures how many people are present. It measures what we need at this moment and whether anyone occupies that position.

None of these three forms of loneliness can be solved by adding more people.

The first needs someone who makes the self more substantial. The second needs someone who can hear what the music is saying. The third needs someone willing to stand still while the ax approaches.

There is also a difficult fact to accept: the second form cannot be solved by finding people who agree with us. As the previous essay argued, agreement carries no information. Boya did not need someone to say the music was good. He needed someone capable of hearing whether it was a mountain or a river. A person capable of being right in that way is also capable of being wrong.

The common shape of all three forms of loneliness is this: the person we need must be capable of truly touching us.

And to be capable of touching us is to be capable of hurting us.

There is still a reverse perspective to consider.

Loneliness is not actually frightening.

It is the shape of an absence, and the shape of the absence tells us exactly what is missing.

Earlier I said loneliness is not evidence of one's level. Looking backward, it cannot prove where we have already arrived. Looking forward, however, it points somewhere. Loneliness is the part a level cannot resolve, speaking aloud.

When a person remains at a given level, whatever that level can accommodate does not feel absent. What feels missing is exactly what cannot be entered into its account, the remainder of that level.

Each form of loneliness therefore points in a direction.

The person with the first form goes in search of ends of their own. No amount of confirmation from others can fill the lack. The “I” can become substantial only by having somewhere of its own to go.

The person with the second form moves toward recognizing another person's ends. Waiting for someone who understands what we are playing leads nowhere. When we begin listening for what another person is playing, the situation changes.

The person with the third form looks toward another who is not yet present.

This looks least like an exit, yet it is the most concrete of the three.

Craftsman Shi lost his material, but he did not throw away the ax. Zhuangzi spoke at Huizi's grave and then continued writing. Plato wrote down the story of the man who returned to the cave and was nearly attacked by the prisoners.

To write something down is to extend a hand toward someone not yet present.

The previous essay said that we write not because we have finished thinking, but to hand our thought over for checking. Now we can add the other half: the person to whom we hand it need not be anyone present now.

This is where the third level differs most from the first two. The absence at the first two levels can be filled only by someone actually appearing beside us now. The third need not be. The future other is absent, but not nonexistent. They have simply not arrived.

At the grave, Zhuangzi said, “I have no material on which to work.”

Then he continued writing.

Twenty-four centuries later, that ax has reached the tip of our nose.

Whether we flinch has never been automatic.

8. Why Has Listening Become an Expensive Commodity?

An interesting news story appeared in 2026.

The latest AI boom had created a group of newly wealthy people, and San Francisco had come alive with them. Luxury homes were in demand again. Some listings even said they would accept payment in shares of private companies. None of this was new. It happens in every cycle.

The new part was another story.

In the same city, a business had emerged that charged by the hour, from $3,000 to $6,000. The people offering the service described themselves as knowledgeable about AI, computing power, longevity, and cryptocurrency. Many of their clients were the same people appearing in fundraising news during the day.

Reports described the situation this way: they had money and minds full of ideas, but no one to tell. When they did speak, no one took them seriously. Eventually they discovered that another person's serious attention could be purchased.

The first reaction is probably laughter.

But after laughing, the structure deserves a look. It is so clean that it resembles an experiment.

In the previous essay on loneliness, I mentioned Boya. When Boya played with high mountains in mind, Zhong Ziqi heard Mount Tai. When he played with flowing water in mind, Ziqi heard the torrents of a great river. After Ziqi died, Boya smashed his qin and never played again.
