Boya never lacked listeners. He was the most famous musician of his time and had audiences in abundance. He lacked the person who could say what he was playing.

That is precisely what these people are trying to buy: a Ziqi.

It cannot be bought.

The reason is neither insufficient money nor bad luck in choosing a listener. It lies in the structure of the transaction itself.

When someone charging by the hour enters the room, their situation has already determined what they can say. Their words must make this hour feel worth its price and ensure another booking. Only one response is inherently safe: agreement.

The previous essay argued that another person's agreement carries no information. I cannot distinguish why they agree. Perhaps they truly understand. Perhaps they share my blind spot. Perhaps they were not listening and simply liked the sound of the sentence. All three look the same from where I stand.

Payment simplifies the question. We now have a known answer to why the listener agrees.

Ziqi was valuable precisely because he was not paid. He said the music was a mountain because he truly heard a mountain. If he had heard something else, he would have said so. The possibility that he might say something else is what gave weight to his recognition of the mountain.

That possibility is the first thing the $3,000 to $6,000 purchases away.

What the money buys is not Ziqi, but a person who cannot say the wrong thing. And someone who cannot be wrong is someone whose words count for nothing.

There is a deeper layer, however, and it is what this essay is really about.

A person wants to be heard by another person. Being heard exists only between two people who are both ends in themselves.

Ziqi was not Boya's instrument. He stood there listening because he wanted to listen. His words reached Boya precisely because he occupied a position of his own.

Purchase does the opposite. It turns the other person into a means.

The moment the transaction succeeds, the desired object is already gone. Not because the payment is too small, but because paying destroys the condition under which that object can exist.

This is not funny. It is simply exact.

One point must be made clear before we continue, or this essay may be read as an argument against ambition.

Working hard to make money is not the problem.

Making money is good. There is nothing objectionable in creating something, expanding it, enabling many people to use it, and being rewarded. The things produced during this AI cycle are real. So are the nights those people stayed awake.

The problem has never been pursuit. It is putting something in the wrong place.

Aristotle discusses wealth directly in the Nicomachean Ethics. Wealth is plainly not the good we are seeking, he says, because it is useful only as a means to something else.

Money is the purest of means. It cannot itself be eaten or worn, and it cannot keep you company. Its sole quality is that it can be exchanged for something else. Precisely because it seems exchangeable for everything, it is the means most easily placed in the position of an end.

Once it occupies that position, something follows automatically.

Persons are ends and must not be treated merely as means. Money is a means and must not be treated as an end. These are two opposite misplacements under the same law.

Nor are the misplacements independent. They form a chain. Once money becomes the ultimate end, everything else, including people, can be valued only according to its usefulness in producing money.

The room in San Francisco demonstrates the entire sentence. A person needs to be heard by another agent. The only tool in his hand is one that turns another person into a means. The harder he reaches for the desired thing with that tool, the farther away it moves.

Look one layer deeper, and the luxury homes are the first half of the same story.

When a person organizes life around an end, that end gives tomorrow its shape. They are still changing code at three in the morning because something has not yet been made. Everything sacrificed is recorded in the same ledger.

A luxury home is a receipt.

The receipt proves that the account has been settled. But a receipt organizes nothing. It does not tell you when to get out of bed tomorrow or make you want to return to your desk at three in the morning.

In the essay on death, I wrote that a person at the second level fears dying because the account has not been settled, because items remain unchecked on the list.

Now we can add the other half. Settling the account is painful too.

In one case, items remain. In the other, every item has been crossed off. The same ledger produces two forms of pain at the same level.

The emptiness is therefore not retribution, some punishment for greed. It is part of the structure of the level. This level organizes itself with a ledger, and every ledger has an inherent problem: eventually it can be closed.

It can be closed because it records means. Means are finite. Once completed, they are complete. An end is different. A genuine end is not exhausted because you have done a great deal in its service.

Then comes the room and the person charging by the hour. When the ledger is empty, the first instinct is to repair it with the ledger, because the ledger is the only tool at hand.

This was written down three thousand years ago.

In the second chapter of Ecclesiastes, the speaker who calls himself a king in Jerusalem says that he undertook great works, built houses, planted vineyards, made pools, gathered silver and gold, and withheld from his eyes nothing they desired.

Then he looked back at everything his hands had done and called it vanity, a chasing after wind.

Three thousand years. The sentence has been printed and read countless times, and it has stopped no one.

That deserves a pause, because it is itself a judgment.

In the essay on the unity of knowledge and action, I argued that principles can be taught but judgment cannot. A law can only be reached for oneself; it cannot be given by someone else. Even if a law handed to you is correct word for word, all you have received is a sentence.

Every new generation of the wealthy must therefore collide with this discovery for itself. Not because they do not read. Many read far more than we do. The experience has no channel of transmission.

The fact that Ecclesiastes must continually be reread is the best evidence for this judgment. If the discovery could simply be passed down, there would be no need to read it again.

But fairness requires another sentence, perhaps the most important in the essay.

Adam Smith wrote about a poor man's son seized by ambition. He spends his life pursuing wealth and status, sacrificing tranquility and every pleasure he might once have enjoyed. In old age, sitting among the things he has earned, he discovers that they are useless trinkets, and that maintaining them is more exhausting than acquiring them was.

Smith then adds something else: the deception is beneficial.

People's belief that these things will bring happiness leads them to cultivate land, build cities, invent machines, and organize production. An entire civilization is created by the deception.

The illusion may benefit the species. It may not benefit the person driving the vehicle.

This is not a story about stupidity. The people involved are not stupid. Many are exceptionally intelligent, and what they build is real. It is a story about a tool: the tool that brought them here no longer works here.

One final point, and it is the point this essay most needs to make.

It is easy to laugh at someone who pays $5,000 an hour to be heard. After laughing, we receive something: a sense of our own lucidity. At least we would never do that.

That feeling of lucidity is itself a receipt.

The ledger does not belong only to the rich.

Each of us has a receipt of our own: the position finally obtained, the home, the number, the post finally published and finally shared. The moment of attainment is genuinely pleasurable. By the next morning, the pleasure is usually spent.

The luxury home is simply an enlarged version. Enlargement makes it easy to recognize. The smaller version is harder to see because we are holding it ourselves.

What can we do after the laughter?

This essay will not offer a method. As the previous essays have argued, the road can only be walked for oneself.

But we can look at one thing. If the account you currently hold were settled today, what would you want to do tomorrow morning?

If the answer is empty, then the emptiness is not waiting in the future. It is already present, merely hidden behind an unsettled account.

If the answer contains a particular person, or something you would still want to finish with no one watching, then you hold something beyond the ledger.

That is the thing occupying the position of an end. Money beside it returns to its proper place: a very useful means.

Returning a means to the position of a means does not diminish it. Only then does it become truly useful, because at last it knows what it is for.

What occupies the other position cannot be bought by a receipt, or for $3,000 to $6,000 an hour.

9. What Is the Meaning of Life?

A friend asked what the meaning of life is.

First look at the shape of the question.

It assumes that meaning is something to be found. It exists somewhere, perhaps in a book, in another person, or at some moment that has not yet arrived. If we search in the right place, we can take hold of it, probably in the form of a sentence.

Almost every serious philosophy has answered differently.

It offers not a sentence but a road, one divided into stages, ordered, and requiring time to travel.

This deserves a pause, because it is no coincidence.

If meaning could be given in a sentence, the sentence could be repeated. You could tell it to me; I could write it down; then I would possess it. But a meaning that can be transmitted in this way is plainly not what those philosophies describe. Otherwise the matter would have been settled thousands of years ago. Someone would have written down the sentence, and everyone afterward would only need to copy it.

Wittgenstein states the reason most cleanly.

Two sentences appear many pages apart in the Tractatus Logico-Philosophicus. One says, “I am my world.” The other says, “The sense of the world must lie outside the world.”

Put them together: my meaning must lie outside me.

Wittgenstein then says that the world is the totality of what can be said. What lies outside the world cannot be said. The book therefore ends with the sentence: “Whereof one cannot speak, thereof one must be silent.”

This is not mysticism. It is a conclusion about form: meaning does not belong to the range of things that can be spoken.

It can only be traveled, not stated.

And when something can only be traveled, the written form it takes is a sequence of stages.

In earlier essays, I argued that a law can only be reached for oneself and cannot be given by another person. Here the same point appears in different words. The unsayable and the intransmissible are two sides of the same thing.

What, then, might the road look like?

Place accounts from different eras and cultures beside one another, and a roughly similar order appears.

I will refer several times to a passage from Confucius, so one point should be clarified at the outset. “At thirty, I stood firm; at forty, I was free of perplexity; at sixty, my ear was attuned.” The subject of the whole passage is “I.” Confucius is describing the road he traveled, not prescribing ages for everyone else. Later readers turned it into a timetable for life. They mistook a personal account for an external standard, which is precisely the reading those sentences least support.

The first stage is finding oneself.

Before there is an “I,” the question of meaning has nowhere to land. Meaning is always the meaning of something, and that something does not yet exist.

This stage is usually unattractive: imitation, trial and error, pretending to be someone else, changing one's language from one circle to another. Then one day we discover that some things genuinely matter to us, while others were merely learned.

This is what Confucius meant by standing firm at thirty.

The second stage is finding one's genuine ends.

Once a self exists, the next question is where that self will go.

Notice that these must be one's genuine ends, not ends handed down by someone else. Many people have goals, but their families, professions, or peers supplied them. There is still a road between possessing an end and possessing an end of one's own.

Confucius speaks of being free of perplexity at forty. This does not mean understanding everything. It means no longer swaying from side to side because one knows what one wants.

This is the stage in which life feels most saturated with meaning. Something must be done, and tomorrow acquires a shape because of it.

The third stage is turning toward others.

Here the direction changes. The question is no longer only what I want, but whether I can recognize that another person is also a source of ends that do not exist to serve me.

Confucius speaks of the ear being attuned at sixty. The phrase is exact: one can listen, without first translating another person's words into the question of whether they are useful to oneself.

History has repeatedly described these three stages under different names.

Kierkegaard wrote a book whose title can be rendered literally as Stages on Life's Way. The aesthetic stage lives in immediate experience and lacks a unified self. The ethical stage chooses the self and assumes obligations; the self becomes a task. Then comes the religious stage.

Nietzsche wrote of three transformations: the camel, the lion, and the child. The camel bears what others load upon it. The lion says no and wins the right to say, “I will.” Then comes the child.

In psychology, Erikson gives a similar sequence: identity, intimacy, and generativity. He defines generativity as establishing and guiding the next generation, whose opposite is stagnation.

One person's example is especially worth considering because he revised his own account.

Almost everyone has seen Maslow's pyramid, with self-actualization at its summit. In 1969, after suffering a heart attack, he wrote two articles for a newly founded journal and added another level above self-actualization.

He described it as functioning as an end rather than a means and entering into relation with oneself, significant others, people in general, nature, and the cosmos.

Here was someone who began in psychology rather than philosophy, reached the top of his pyramid, discovered something above it, and used the vocabulary of ends and means to describe what he found.

He also admitted that it was difficult to say clearly what lay beyond self-actualization.

Fairness requires another point. Maslow's hierarchy was later subjected to a well-known test that found no supporting evidence. It therefore cannot serve as proof. Its value lies elsewhere. Someone who followed an entirely different path arrived at roughly the same place and picked up the same words.

Yet the most revealing feature of these accounts is not where they resemble one another.

It is where they fail to align.

The first two stages are nearly a consensus. A self must first exist; then it must have ends of its own. From Confucius to Kierkegaard to Erikson, no one disputes this order.

All disagreement concentrates in the third stage.

Nietzsche's third transformation is the child, creativity, and a new beginning. It is not another person.

Kierkegaard's third stage is religious and faces the absolute, not another person.

Wittgenstein places meaning outside the world, but there are almost no other people in his book. There are only the “I,” the world, language, and the unsayable beyond. Structurally, he takes the same step described here: meaning must lie outside the self. But he does not identify that outside with another person.

The four stages of life in the Indian tradition end in renunciation. Their direction is withdrawal.

The path in the Zhuangzi equalizes things, dissolving the distinction between self and the ten thousand things. It too turns back inward.

Maslow admits that he cannot explain the final level clearly.

These disagreements might look like evidence that the third stage is the least substantial. In fact, they show the opposite.

No one argues over a place no one has visited.

The first two stages attract little dispute because the road is easy to recognize and many people travel it. The third inspires disagreement because people have arrived there and seen different things. The disagreement itself is evidence that something is there.

Several qualifications must now be made, or this essay will become something else.

First, not everyone will complete all three stages.

This is a process, not a timetable. Some remain in the first stage for a long time. Some spend their lives in the second. Some reach the third and then retreat.

Stopping at a stage is not failure.

Each stage has a law of its own. To live in alignment with the law of one's stage is to live honestly. That has nothing to do with how far one has traveled.

Second, speeds differ, and there is no correct speed.

Some complete the first stage at twenty. Some do not begin until fifty. Others follow a different sequence. Any chart aligning stages with ages mistakes the journey of one person for the scale by which everyone must be measured.

Third, this essay cannot supply the answer, and must not.

If it could tell you the meaning of life in a sentence, that would prove everything it has said to be wrong.

It can do only one thing: explain why the question offers a road rather than an answer, and sketch the road's possible form so that a person may have some sense of where they are.

Return to the question.

What is the meaning of life?

The question cannot be answered. It can only be lived through.

But the fact that someone is asking already tells us something.

To ask the meaning of one's life, a self must first exist whose meaning can be at stake. If you can voice the question, the first stage has already been completed.

The next two stages must be walked for oneself. We can study the roads others have taken. Afterward, we still have to lift our own feet.

10. Should Children Practice Speed Arithmetic?

The real question is what children are being trained to do when they practice speed arithmetic. Once that is answered, we can decide whether the training is worthwhile.

Begin with a fact that is easily skipped: speed training genuinely helps. It is not a fraud.

Human working memory is small. In a moderately complicated problem, if elementary calculations continue to occupy mental space, too little remains for the truly difficult part. The more automatic the basic operations become, the more room is left for thought. This is well established in educational research and needs no defense.

The problem is therefore not speed.

It lies elsewhere, and seeing it requires us to avoid an appealing but invalid criticism.

The criticism says that speed arithmetic trains mindless reflexes and therefore obstructs thought.

That conclusion does not follow, because a response that no longer requires thought is not something inferior.

An experienced driver does not derive the proper moment for braking while driving. A fast reader does not consult a dictionary word by word. Someone who truly understands a matter may judge a related situation directly, without an intermediate moment of “let me think.”

Skill consists precisely in allowing something once thought through to settle beneath attention, where it no longer occupies mental space.

If the criticism were valid, practicing music, handwriting, and vocabulary would all impede development. Plainly they do not.

The real question is not whether training makes something automatic, but what lies beneath the automatic response.

Beneath musical technique lies music. You no longer think about fingering so that you can think about how the phrase should sing.

Beneath fluent reading lies meaning. You no longer struggle to recognize characters so that you can think about what the passage says.

What lies beneath the automaticity of speed arithmetic?

A set of formulas.

A formula is the finished product of reasoning performed by someone else. The child receives the conclusion, not the derivation. The faster and more accurately the child executes it, the less need there is to know why it works.

That is the problem, and it has nothing to do with speed.

The structure of the training excludes “why” from the score. It does not reward understanding. It rewards correctness in the absence of understanding. If a child who understands and one who does not achieve the same speed, their scores are identical.

What does not enter the score will not be optimized. This requires no ill will from anyone. It is the natural result of any evaluative system.

At this point, we can set speed arithmetic aside. It is not an exception but the purest example.

Many school subjects do the same thing, though less thoroughly.

Students memorize templates for solving problems: when you see this type, use this method. Essays acquire fixed structures for introductions and conclusions that reliably earn points. Historical events come with standard lists of significance and consequences; write down the whole list and receive the grade.

What these forms of training teach in common is not their respective content but something more fundamental: correctness comes from following instructions.

You do not need to know why the template works, only to recognize the type of problem. You need not have considered what an event means, only to reproduce the required points. A correct answer is a correct answer, and once it has been given, no one asks anything more.

Once this attitude takes shape, it does not remain on a mathematics exam.

Someone repeatedly rewarded from childhood for “following the steps and getting it right” will meet any ready-made account by first receiving it, memorizing it, and using it. They judge whether a claim is good by whether it works, whether someone says it is right, and who that person is.

In their experience, correctness has never been derived for oneself. It comes from outside and is assessed from outside.

That is exactly the condition in which someone else can most easily decide for you.

Not because the person is unintelligent. The opposite may be true. The more completely a child excels at this training, the smoother the channel becomes, because experience has confirmed the same lesson repeatedly: receive what comes from outside, follow it, and win.

Where does the problem arise?

Not in skill. Skill is good. Not in training. Training is necessary.

The problem lies in position.

Skill is properly a means. We practice until we no longer need to think in order to free the mind for what must be thought about. Its entire value lies in serving what comes next.

When skill itself becomes the end, however, when it becomes something to compete over, certify, and use as proof of a child's excellence, the positions have changed.

The previous essay described the misplacement of means and ends through money. Money is the purest means. Placed in the position of an end, it becomes empty, because all its content lies elsewhere.

The same movement appears here with a different object.

This time the problem is more serious because the child is placed in the position of a means.

Once a skill becomes an end, the child becomes its vehicle. The faster the child calculates, the more completely the end is realized. Whether the child has asked why falls outside the end and therefore does not matter.

A structure that treats a person as a means usually requires no malicious person. It requires only a score sheet.

One point must be clear, or this essay will become an argument against teaching children anything.

It does not oppose training or deny the importance of fluency.

A person who does not know multiplication facts cannot do any moderately complicated mathematics. That is true. Telling children to “explore freely” while withholding solid foundations is not respect. It is abandonment.

The distinction lies in one question: Is there room for “why”?

Two children may each practice a hundred problems. If the first asks why the formula works, someone answers seriously, perhaps even saying, “Try to see whether you can work it out.” For the second, the question never appears. If it does, the answer is, “Don't worry about that now. Get your speed up first.”

The two forms of training may look identical on a report card.

How can we tell which one a child is receiving?

Ask a direct question: Can they explain why the method works?

There is nothing wrong if they cannot. Most children cannot, which is normal. Many things must first be used and understood later.

So ask a second question, the one that really matters: Have they ever wondered why?

If they have wondered, tried, and become stuck, they simply have not arrived yet. That is a matter of time.

If the question has never occurred to them, or if hearing it feels strange, like asking something one should not ask, the issue is no longer time. It means that in their experience “why” does not belong to learning.

In the first case, the road remains unfinished. In the second, no one has ever pointed out the road.

One final point.

We all grew up within some version of this training, and many of us succeeded because of it. This essay is not only about other people's children.

The adult version of the same action persists. Faced with something new, we first look for a ready-made account. Once we find and receive it, we stop thinking further.

When learning a skill, we first search for a methodology. When encountering a difficulty, we look for someone else's summary. When reading an argument, we first ask who said it and how many people agree.

None of this is wrong. Materials are meant to be used.

The question is whether “why” follows.

Whatever another person has already cut and shaped for us, what we receive is the stone, not the strike of the chisel. The stone can be used, and used well. But only the stroke one makes oneself belongs to oneself.

What we can give a child is actually very limited.

We cannot give understanding. A child must arrive there alone. What we can give is permission for the question to exist.

If a child knows that “why” may be asked, that someone will take it seriously, and that it will not be cut off by “don't worry about that now,” the child will walk the rest of the road.

11. Why Does a Path Everyone Praises Begin to Feel Wrong?

A friend described a feeling that was difficult to explain.

Everyone says the path is good. Many people take it. Experienced predecessors are already there, and the methods are mature. He follows it and does reasonably well. Yet the farther he goes, the more something feels wrong. He cannot say what the mistake is or identify a particular event to blame.

The feeling is common and has one distinctive feature: from outside, everything is right. There has been no failure or upheaval, and no one has treated him badly. Nothing that can be pointed to appears to be a problem.

Let us begin by asking what this path contains.

1. The path contains everything, including the most reassuring thing of all

The advantages of a well-traveled path are real. They should not be underestimated.

Others have gone before. The methods are mature, predecessors can offer experience, the standards of evaluation are ready-made, and earlier travelers have marked the places where mistakes occur. The path is lower in risk and higher in efficiency. At every moment, you know how far you have progressed.

It also contains the most reassuring thing of all: mutual confirmation.

The people on the path assure one another that they are going the right way. Look how many of us are here.

But one distinction must be made precisely.

Confirmation can prove that a path is viable. It cannot prove that the path is yours.

The first fact is useful. A path successfully traveled by many people has indeed been successfully traveled. That information is true.

The second question is unaffected by numbers.

When a group shares the same way of traveling, it also shares the same blind spot. No matter how many people join it, they cannot illuminate what their way of seeing excludes.

Yet the two facts are easily conflated. Because many people have reached the destination, “this path can be traveled” becomes “this path should be traveled.”

2. It does not lead somewhere bad; it leads somewhere belonging to someone else

Where, then, is the problem?

It is not that the path leads to a bad place. It leads to a real, reachable destination. That is why it is broad.

The problem is elsewhere. Its direction is not yours, but someone else's.

The path was worn into the ground by other people's ends. Those who follow it arrive quickly and steadily, only to discover that they have reached a place someone else wanted to go.

This is probably the unease my friend described.

He is not traveling badly, nor is the path defective. The path is carrying him very efficiently toward a destination that is not his. Nothing along the way can reveal the problem because there is nothing wrong with the path itself.

That is why he cannot say what is wrong. Everything that can be pointed to is correct.

The third chapter of the Bhagavad Gita says this more directly than anything I could devise: better to perform one's own duty imperfectly than another's duty well. Better to die in one's own duty; another's duty brings danger.

Notice the final clause. The original word means, quite literally, that it brings fear or danger. Doing another person's work well is not safety in this passage. It is danger.

That sentence supplies something a purely structural account cannot.

To say that each person has a path of their own is only a description of structure. It assigns no weight to the fact. It does not tell us what it is worth or whether the price should be paid. The Gita gives it weight all the way to the bottom: better to die on one's own path.

A structural claim alone cannot derive that sentence.

The sentence, however, has a problem of its own. Traditional commentaries often bind “one's own duty” to caste and stage of life. You should remain in the position into which you were born. Read that way, it does not say each person has a road of their own. It says each person has a cell of their own.

The same sentence can defend social order or individuality.

This essay uses the latter reading. If I did not state that openly, I would merely be borrowing an ancient sentence for courage.

3. What about the other path?

At this point, the next thought arises almost automatically: then I should take the other path.

And an old sentence is waiting for that thought.

In the seventh chapter of the Gospel of Matthew: enter through the narrow gate. Wide is the gate and broad is the road that leads to destruction, and many enter through it; small is the gate and narrow the road that leads to life, and few find it.

The passage is widely known. For that reason, it is especially easy to misread.

The easiest interpretation is: because few people take the road, those who do are special. The next step is “I have entered the narrow gate; I belong to the few.”

Anyone can make that inference. It produces superiority and humility at once and cannot be refuted. Before going further, then, we must clarify what “narrow” means.

(Several other sources will appear below. They are neither evidence for these claims nor illustrations of them. I place them together because each illuminates something the others cannot, and some illuminate precisely the defects in the claims themselves.)

4. The road is not empty because the gate is narrow

Begin by reversing the usual view.

It is not that few people travel because the gate is narrow. The place appears narrow because few people travel there.

Why are there so few? Because the gate is your own.

This does not mean there is one narrow gate in the world that everyone must find. The opposite is true. Each person has one, and each is in a different place.

The gate is wide enough for only one person. That is true for everyone. It is narrow for you, for me, and for whoever stands before their own.

Entering one's own gate therefore distinguishes no one. Everyone has one. Finding yours does not place you above anyone else. It means only that you are walking your road.

Narrowness is not an honor. It is a measurement.

Nietzsche expresses this in the shortest possible form. In Thus Spoke Zarathustra, someone asks him where “the way” is. He replies: this is my way; where is yours? There is no such thing as the way.

The sentence is almost impossibly clean. Where Nietzsche's own book goes next is another matter. The last of his three transformations is the child, creativity, and a new beginning. After asking, “Where is your way?” he takes no further outward step toward another person.

He gives this section its sharpest sentence while also marking the sentence's boundary. Saying that each person has a road and moving toward another person are two different things.

5. The difficulty is the absence of anything to follow

What makes the narrow road difficult?

There is nothing to use as a reference.

No one has gone this way before. There is no predecessor's experience to borrow, no companion's confirmation to receive, and no marker along the way to tell you that you are right.

You do not know whether the direction is correct, how far remains, or whether anything waits at the end. None of the benefits listed in the first section are available here.

The difficulty is not passing through the gate. It is continuing without confirmation.

The only confirmation is arrival, and it can never be obtained in advance while traveling.

An old teaching can be placed here. Near his death, the Buddha instructed his followers to be lamps unto themselves, to take refuge in themselves, and not in another.

At the final moment, the founder of a religion told his followers not to depend on him.

But the original teaching contains two lamps, not one. It continues: take the Dharma as your lamp and refuge.

That deserves a pause, because it touches a real problem.

If everything must be reached for oneself, what is the second lamp?

One answer is that the law points universally and applies to anyone, but binds only the person who holds it. It is not a manual to be copied, yet neither is it something each person invents at will.

This is close to the image of the two lamps, but not identical. The Buddha's second lamp is given. It can be heard and passed down. If a law can only be reached for oneself, however, what can be passed down are words about it, not the law itself.

The difference is not small. It is the most difficult point on this whole road. I place the passage here because without it, the difficulty would never become visible.

6. Narrow does not mean one's own

One danger must be prevented because it is the most common modern version of the claim.

Being unconventional does not mean one is walking one's own road.

Narrowness tells us only that few people are on the road, not that the road is yours.

Three people may copy the same obscure source. Their road will be exceedingly narrow without a single inch having been made by them. An esoteric theory, a position no one accepts, or a deliberate rejection of everything mainstream can be both narrow and entirely borrowed.

What is borrowed does not become one's own merely because it was borrowed from an obscure place.

The test is therefore not how many people travel there. It is whether I cut this step for myself or simply received it.

At this point, a sentence from Chapter 41 of the Daodejing should be mentioned.

That chapter says that when the highest kind of person hears the Way, they practice it diligently; when the middling person hears it, they half believe and half doubt; when the lowest hears it, they laugh aloud. Then come six Chinese characters meaning: if it were not laughed at, it would not be sufficient to be the Way.

Those words turn ridicule into evidence of correctness.

If you laugh at me, that proves I am speaking the Way. It is a perfect device. It cannot be refuted because every objection has already been absorbed as favorable evidence.

The fact that it grows within one of the most revered classics tells us more than anything else could.

Such immunizing devices are not merely defects of character. They grow naturally from claims that only a few can understand. The moment you say, “Most people cannot see this,” you create an unfalsifiable line of retreat.

This essay belongs to the same class.

The Daodejing passage is therefore not cited to confirm anything. It is a warning: everything said above about narrowness can be converted into an exemption from scrutiny in exactly the same way.

7. My reading of “destruction”

Return to the heaviest word in the passage from Matthew. The broad road leads to destruction.

The word is usually read as an eschatological judgment: the person takes the wrong road and is punished at the end.

I can only state how I read it. This is my interpretation, not the original meaning of the scripture. A first-century text did not speak in the language of agency used below. I am transplanting the sentence, and readers are free to reject the transplant.

The same Gospel contains a more direct line: what does it profit a person to gain the whole world and forfeit their life?

Notice its structure. Gaining the whole world means that the road worked and the traveler reached the destination and received everything. The cost falls nowhere else. It falls upon the traveler.

Read in this direction, the broad-gate passage is not a threat but a description.

To walk another person's road all the way to the end is to have no “you” walking it from beginning to end.

What is lost is not something withheld as punishment at the destination. It is what has been leaking away throughout the journey.

On this reading, destruction is not a penalty imposed from outside. It is a property of the road itself and requires no one to enforce it.

Again, this is only my reading. The scripture says the road leads to destruction. Reading that destruction as the loss of oneself is my addition.

The passage from the Bhagavad Gita requires no such addition. It directly says that another's duty brings danger. That is already in the text.

8. The lock installed by the text itself

The same chapter of Matthew contains another passage close to the concern of this essay, and it requires no transplantation.

Immediately after the two verses about the narrow gate comes a warning against false prophets, followed by the claim that they will be known by their fruits. Then comes the harshest passage: many will say on that day, “Lord, Lord, did we not prophesy in your name?” The answer is, “I never knew you.”

These are the people most certain that they have already entered the narrow gate.

The chapter's structure is therefore: first the narrow gate, immediately followed by a warning to those convinced they are already inside.

The claim and its safeguard sit beside each other. In later circulation, everyone remembers the first part. Few mention the second.

It forms a pair with the six characters from the Daodejing.

One ancient text places a lock beside its own claim. Another leaves a back door at the same point.

Both now lock or open upon us.

9. Narrowness is a moment, not an attribute

There is another point, perhaps the most concrete in the essay.

Narrowness is not the essence of the road. It is the road's present condition.

It is narrow because no one has passed there. Once someone passes, the surrounding ground grows broad.

Every road now broad enough for countless travelers was once narrow. The first person to think, live, or act that way had no road before them. It is broad now because someone walked it.

This can be checked. For most of today's broad roads, we can look back and identify the first traveler.

But the point must be stated precisely or it becomes an inspirational slogan.

What widens after someone passes is not the gate but the ground around it.

Each gate belongs to a person. What you leave becomes material for others, and you also prove that passage is possible. But the gate another person must cross is still their own.

The finished product can remain. The stroke of the chisel cannot.

The gate therefore remains where it was, still wide enough for only one. The next person must walk for themselves.

Each generation must cross pathless ground again, not because its predecessors traveled badly, but because the gate is indexed to the person.

10. Two things about other people's roads

First, another person's narrow gate cannot measure your own.

If their gate was difficult at a certain point, yours need not be difficult there. Suffering does not prove you are right; the absence of suffering does not prove you are wrong. The claim that greater difficulty means greater truth mistakes another person's terrain for a standard.

Second, you cannot point out where another person's gate is.

You can say that a gate once stood here. You can leave behind what helped during your own passage. You can remain beside them.

But you cannot say, “Your gate is over there.” To say it is to decide the form of another person's road, when the road's entire meaning lies in being made by that person.

The most we can offer is this: do not walk for them. Remain beside them when the going is hardest.

In closing

Return to my friend's question.

Why does a path everyone praises begin to feel wrong?

Because everything that can be checked is correct. The one thing wrong with it is precisely what cannot be checked: it is not yours.

The place that belongs to you probably has no road yet.

The gate is not narrow because its threshold is high or admission is scarce. It is narrow because there are no footprints.

It is narrow for everyone. Each person has a gate, and each gate admits only one.

Walking a road without footprints does not place you above anyone. It means only that the road ahead has not yet been widened by travel.

Whether it will become wider depends on whether you make it through.

And the reason you must do it yourself is simple: a road may be left for others, but the act of crossing can only be your own.

12. What Is Love?

A friend asked what love is, adding specifically: not only romantic love.

The addition matters because it opens the question more widely. A parent's love for a child, friendship, the relation between teacher and student, even the care that arises for someone met only a few times, all belong to the question.

Still, we must begin with romantic love, because the most popular account appears there and governs almost everyone's imagination of love. And love that truly lasts eventually reaches the same place as love in its broader forms.

The account can be expressed in two words: one's “other half.”

1. The sphere split in two

The idea has a very ancient source.

In Plato's Symposium, Aristophanes tells a story. Human beings, he says, were not originally as we are now. They were round, with four arms and four legs and enormous strength. After offending the gods, they were split in half.

Since then, each person has been half a being, spending a lifetime searching the crowd for the half cut away. Once found, the two cling to one another and long to grow back into one.

The story has been told for twenty-four centuries. It is told so beautifully that almost no one now recognizes it as an account. We take it for a fact.

Its structure is clear: I am missing a piece, and you have exactly the shape of that piece.

Several things follow automatically.

Because I am incomplete, I must search. The standard is whether the pieces fit. Once they do, I am finished.

But a deep problem is hidden here. If the other person is the piece that completes me, what I want is not actually that person. I want the shape.

Anyone who can fill it will do.

This is not a reproach. It is only a description of the structure, which does not point toward a person.

2. Wholeness and completion

We must therefore clarify the foundation of everything that follows.

A person is whole. They are not half of something.

No one is born missing a piece held by another person. You do not need to find someone in order to become a whole person. You already are one.

But wholeness is not completion.

Wholeness means that you do not lack a part held by someone else.

Completion means that you have finished growing and will no longer change.

A person can be whole and still very far from finished.

The two are often confused, and their confusion produces two opposite problems.

One looks forward: if I am incomplete, I must find the person who can complete me. This treats a person as a component.

The other looks backward: if I am whole, no one need concern themselves with me, and I need concern myself with no one. This mistakes wholeness for completion and not lacking a part for having no need to grow.

In fact, a person who lacks no component continues to grow, perhaps for an entire life.

A new question follows. If no one is needed to complete us, what place does another person have?

3. The desire to become one destroys what it seeks

Consider first the deepest desire: to become one.

The desire is real. There is no need to pretend otherwise. Many people have known moments when two long to become one, and such moments can be good.

But if union is treated as the actual goal, a strange result follows.

If two people truly become one, there is no other person left.

And without another person, no one can see you.

This point should be unfolded slowly.

Whether a person is cherished cannot be declared by that person alone. You may tell yourself all day that you are wonderful, but the sentence has no weight. To carry weight, it must come from another person, and that person must genuinely be another: someone with judgment of their own, who may disagree and is entirely free not to see you that way.

It is because they could see you otherwise that their seeing you this way counts.

The desire is therefore self-contradictory. What it seeks can be given only by someone who is not itself, while what it proposes to do is eliminate that person.

Hegel describes a related scene, though he is discussing something else. Two people meet. Each wants recognition from the other, and neither will recognize first. They fight. One wins and reduces the other to a subordinate. The victor soon discovers that recognition from someone he has crushed into subordination is worthless.

The methods are opposite, but the result is the same. Overpowering another person and dissolving into them both prevent that person from remaining a person.

Love, then, does not lie in the direction of becoming one.

4. What is it, then? Helping someone become themselves, not shaping them

If love is neither completion nor fusion, what is the other person's place?

I want them to become themselves.

The sentence sounds gentle, but it is the sentence in this essay most vulnerable to abuse, so it must immediately be constrained.

Helping a person become themselves does not mean making them into something.

I do not know what they will become. This is not modesty but fact. They are still growing, and they do not know either. If I already hold a blueprint, what I want is not the person but the blueprint.

The previous essay recalled an ancient story. Two gods saw that Hundun, the being of primal chaos, lacked the seven openings of the human face. Meaning well, they carved one opening into him each day. On the seventh day, Hundun died. They bore no malice. They were simply too certain about what a person should become.

What can we do instead?

Something quite simple.

Do not walk for them. The road is theirs, and no one else can make the crossing in their place.

Remain beside them. Be there when the road is difficult, not only when they want advice.

Speak when speech is called for. To see something and say nothing is not respect but indifference. The person is still growing and will sometimes go astray. Someone who never points anything out treats them as a completed object incapable of change.

And when it is our turn to be addressed, do not retreat. This is the hardest part, and it is where reciprocity begins.

5. Need is something else

One thing must be said, or the essay will feel unreal.

Love of course contains need. We need the person to be present, to answer the phone, to know they are still there. That is real and nothing to be ashamed of.

What this essay distinguishes is not need itself, but need directed at a function.

There is a simple test.

Could someone else provide what you need from this person?

If so, what you want is a function: someone to listen, someone to prove that you are worthy, someone to prevent loneliness. Anyone could perform it, so the need never pointed toward this person in the first place.

If no one else could provide it, then what you need is the person. In that case, what you need is precisely the part of them that moves of its own accord, has other thoughts, and differs from you.

Such need is irreplaceable because its object is exactly what you do not control.

6. Helping one another become

In the Nicomachean Ethics, Aristotle distinguishes three kinds of friendship: friendship for utility, friendship for pleasure, and a third kind.

The first two, he says, are incidental. The person is not loved for themselves but for what they provide. When the provision ends, the relationship dissolves. Aristotle is not condemning anyone. He is describing the replaceable position the other person occupies in those two relations.

The third he calls friendship based on virtue. Here the person is loved for themselves. He writes specifically that such friends help one another become better.

He wrote down the idea of helping one another become twenty-three centuries ago.

He adds three practical observations: such friends are rare; the friendship requires a long time; and both people must be capable of it. One side is not enough.

These sentences fully describe the difficulty. This is not a state one person can achieve alone.

Rilke expresses the same idea more warmly, and just as precisely, in his letters to a young man.

Love, he says, is not surrender, devotion, or merging with another person. He even asks what kind of union that would be if it were confused and unfulfilled.

His alternative is this: love is the work by which a person matures and completes a world within themselves, and does so for another person.

Notice the sentence's shape. I do not give myself up for you, nor do I merge with you. For you, I become myself.

Rilke offers two further sentences.

One says that the highest responsibility between two people is to guard each other's solitude.

The other is finer: a worthier love consists of two solitudes that protect, distinguish, and honor one another.

Distinguish one another. Those words rarely appear in an account of love, yet they are the meaning of this entire essay.

Rilke also says something more concrete: love is good because love is difficult. For one human being to love another may be the most difficult and weighty task given to us. Everything else is preparation for it.

In closing

Return to my friend's question.

What is love?

It is not finding the piece that completes you. There is no such piece, because you were never missing it.

Nor is it becoming one. Once two become one, the person capable of seeing you has disappeared.

It is something more like this: there is someone whom you hope will become themselves, and your hope carries no blueprint.

You do not know what they will become. You want only to remain beside them and watch it happen; to speak when speech is needed and not retreat when it is your turn to listen; to let them walk their road while you walk yours.

If they happen to see you in the same way, two people walk the same stretch of ground, each on a road of their own, looking after one another.

It is not one person becoming half of two. It is two whole people, each growing a little more fully into themselves.

This is rare. It takes a long time. One side cannot accomplish it.

But it exists. And it is much better than the sphere split in two.

13. Was Confucius's Journey the Chinese Odyssey?

With the recent popularity of the film The Odyssey, a friend asked whether Confucius's travels among the states were the Chinese version of the same story.

It is a good question, and on the surface the resemblance is real. Both stories involve years of wandering, severe hardship, and doors closed against the traveler. Odysseus spends ten years at sea. Confucius remains abroad for fourteen, leaving Lu at fifty-five and returning only at sixty-eight.

Those fourteen years were not easy. Passing through Kuang, Confucius was mistaken for Yang Hu, whom he resembled, and surrounded by the local people for five days. In Song, while he and his disciples practiced ritual beneath a great tree, the military commander Huan Tui tried to kill him and had the tree cut down. In Zheng, separated from his disciples, Confucius stood alone outside the east gate and was described as looking like a homeless dog. Between Chen and Cai, the group went without food for seven days, and his followers became too weak to stand.

Yet the structures of the two stories are almost exact opposites. Their differences are far more interesting than their similarities.

1. Ithaca is always there; the place Confucius seeks never appears

Odysseus is returning to Ithaca, to the island, the room, and the person waiting for him.

Every episode in the epic is a variation on being prevented from returning: the Cyclops, the goddess's island, the storm at sea. The obstacles may number a thousand, but the destination never disappears. It waits from beginning to end.

Confucius is not trying to return home. He is looking for a place that will put his teaching into practice.

He leaves Lu because it has no use for him. He travels through Wei, Cao, Song, Zheng, Chen, and Cai, reaching the border of Chu. Sima Qian says he approached more than seventy rulers and none employed him. Later scholars consider the number exaggerated, but the point is correct: none of the rulers he met was prepared to implement what he proposed.

In fourteen years, the place never once appeared.

That is the fundamental difference between the journeys.

An end entirely within one's own hands can be completed. However difficult the path, if the person remains alive and moving, arrival is possible.

An end that requires another person to receive it cannot be completed unilaterally.

Odysseus wants to return. However dangerous the return, it is something he can accomplish.

Confucius wants his teaching to be put into practice. That is not in his hands. No distance traveled, effort made, or intelligence applied can guarantee it.

The problem is not insufficient effort. Ends of this kind simply cannot be completed by one person.

2. A journey without a waiting destination cannot become an epic

Follow this distinction and something interesting appears.

The Odyssey has a complete shape: departure, suffering, return. That shape depends on Ithaca's remaining where it is. Once a destination exists, every event in between becomes an “obstacle.” Without one, those events are simply things that happened.

There is no Travels of Confucius narrating those fourteen years.

In the Analects, only scattered fragments remain: a few dozen characters about going hungry in Chen; Zilu spending the night at the Stone Gate and meeting its keeper; the madman of Chu passing Confucius's carriage while singing; encounters with hermits working in the fields who mocked him.

No arc connects the fragments. They do not point toward a destination because no destination exists.

Form and content therefore agree. A road with no destination waiting at its end cannot be written as an epic. It can leave only fragments.

Confucius thus reveals one of the Odyssey's premises: the epic can be an epic because the island remains there.

3. One man spends a lifetime calculating how something may be done; the other does what he already knows cannot be done

Homer's first description of Odysseus calls him a man of many turns, resourceful and cunning.

Everything he does is a calculation of how to succeed: the wooden horse, the disguise as a beggar, binding himself to the mast to hear the Sirens. He is the person who always finds a way. The force driving the entire Odyssey is this search for a way.

In the Analects, a stranger says something about Confucius.

Zilu spends a night at the Stone Gate. In the morning, its keeper asks where he comes from. “From Kong,” Zilu replies. The keeper asks:

“Is that the man who knows it cannot be done and does it anyway?”

A gatekeeper says everything in one sentence.

What is most striking is that it is not a disciple's praise but a passerby's judgment. Even the gatekeeper could see that the thing would not succeed.

Did Confucius himself fail to see it?

He saw it. In the Analects, Zilu says, “That the Way cannot prevail has long been known.”

This is not the story of someone who thought he would succeed.

4. Knowing it cannot be done is not the same as having no choice

A distinction deserves pause because it is easily blurred.

Earlier essays spoke of what one cannot but do. When a person arrives at a certain place, their law has taken that form and they cannot act otherwise.

“Knowing it cannot be done and doing it anyway” adds something. The person sees clearly that success will not come, and still acts.

To have no choice can sound like the absence of information. To know it cannot be done means possessing all the information, including the most discouraging fact, and continuing nevertheless.

This blocks a common misreading.

“Walk your own road” is easily heard as “and therefore it will lead somewhere.” If the road is truly yours, the world will eventually reward you.

Confucius's fourteen years say the opposite. It was his road, and it did not lead through.

Not one of the rulers he met put his teaching into practice. Walking one's road guarantees nothing.

That is not a pleasant sentence. But without it, everything previously said about one's own road becomes a veiled promise.

5. What the Odyssey illuminates in return is more weighty still

The comparison cannot run in only one direction. The Odyssey contains two things absent from the story of Confucius, things the preceding essays might too easily omit.

The first is the bed.

Odysseus returns, kills the suitors, and stands before Penelope. She does not recognize him.

Their son is distressed and asks how his mother can act this way. The nurse has recognized the scar on his leg. Penelope still refuses.

Then she does something. She tells a servant to move the bed out of the bedroom and prepare it for the guest.

Odysseus explodes. The bed cannot be moved, he says, because he made it himself. An olive tree grew in the courtyard. He used its living trunk as a bedpost, built walls and a roof around it, and fashioned the bed from the tree still rooted in the ground. No one could move it without cutting down the tree.

Only then does Penelope recognize him.

The scene deserves a slow reading.

She does not identify him by appearance, because appearance can be imitated. She does not trust his own words, because words can be invented. She tests him with something only the real person could know.

More remarkable still is the nature of the object: he made it with his hands, it is rooted in the earth, and it cannot be moved.

These essays have repeatedly discussed verification. Independent verifiability is a condition of a claim's validity. A claim that cannot be checked is structurally indistinguishable from a false one.

But there is something we have not considered.

The person performing the verification is not the one who returned. It is the one who stayed and waited.

We have treated verification as the task of the person who goes out: investigate, question, cut into the claim. In this story, the man who has traveled for ten years and endured everything is impatient to be recognized. The woman who has remained in the room for twenty years insists on testing him first.

The person eager for recognition and the person who insists on verification have exchanged their usual places.

Turn this light back on Confucius, and it reveals an absence.

What Confucius lacked during those fourteen years was not a ruler willing to employ him. He lacked someone willing to test him.

Employment and testing point in opposite directions. To employ is to accept; to test is to begin with doubt.

The rulers who received Confucius offered courtesy, and courtesy does not test. The first thing Duke Ling of Wei asked was how much salary Confucius had received in Lu. Later he asked about warfare. On another occasion, while the two were speaking, Duke Ling looked up at birds flying overhead and let his attention leave Confucius. That was not verification but appraisal.

Those who truly doubted him walked away. The madman of Chu sang while passing his carriage. Confucius got down and tried to speak with him, but the man quickened his pace. The hermits working the fields mocked him and then refused further conversation.

Confucius met either courtesy without testing or ridicule that tested and left. No one said, “I do not believe you, but I will stay and see.”

The previous essay argued that another person's agreement carries no information, while disagreement may carry something.

Courtesy is agreement and carries nothing. Ridicule is disagreement, but it leaves as soon as it has spoken.

Only disagreement that remains is Penelope's kind.

Confucius therefore lacked neither supporters nor critics. He lacked someone who doubted him and did not leave.

There is another layer that fits the bed exactly.

Penelope can test Odysseus because she possesses something: the bed. He made it, it is rooted in the ground, and it cannot be moved.

Verification requires a shared object that cannot be forged.

During those fourteen years, Confucius's teaching had not yet taken that form. It existed in his words, his actions, and his exchanges with his disciples. No object stood rooted in the ground, immovable and available for comparison.

The second thing is the end itself.

Odysseus travels for ten years for a room, a bed, a particular woman, and a particular son.

We have spoken repeatedly of ends that require another person to receive them and things that cannot be completed unilaterally. In doing so, we may begin to imagine that a completable end is somehow inferior, that a road with an end cannot be deep.

That is wrong.

What Odysseus seeks is what most people are actually traveling toward. It is not low.

A person willing to exchange ten years for a return to several particular people needs no elevation into something grander.

6. Homer refuses to stop at arrival

The Odyssey itself adds another element.

In the underworld, the prophet Tiresias gives Odysseus a prophecy. He will return home and kill the suitors. But that will not be the end.

Afterward, he must carry an oar inland until he meets people who have never seen the sea. They know neither ships nor salt and mistake the oar on his shoulder for a winnowing shovel. There he must plant the oar in the ground, sacrifice to the sea god, and only then return home.

Homer therefore writes explicitly that the return to Ithaca is not the end.

A man who travels for ten years only to reach home must set out again after arriving, this time toward a place whose people have never even heard of the sea.

Even the epic of homecoming refuses to make arrival the ending.

7. He never found Penelope, so he made the bed

Return to Confucius.

At sixty-eight, he returned to Lu. That was not arrival but a change of direction.

He did not reenter politics. He organized classical texts and taught students. Tradition attributes to his later years a sentence: “It has been so long since I dreamed of the Duke of Zhou.”

The sentence probably means that he knew the event he desired would not occur in his lifetime.

Once he knew, he neither abandoned the work nor continued knocking on rulers' doors.

He arranged his teaching into a form that could remain.

Now the earlier line can be completed.

Verification requires a shared, immovable object. That was precisely what Confucius lacked during his fourteen years, so no one could truly test him. They could only honor or ridicule him.

What did he do after sixty-eight?

He never found the person willing to test him, so he made the bed himself.

What he organized became something rooted in the ground, immovable, and available to any later person for comparison.

In the essay on loneliness, I mentioned Craftsman Shi. After the man of Ying died, Lord Yuan of Song asked Shi to repeat his feat. Shi said he could still wield the ax, but the person who served as his material had long been dead. Zhuangzi said this at Huizi's grave, and then continued writing.

Confucius performed the same act more concretely. Unable to find someone who could test him, he left behind something that could be tested and handed it to a person not yet born.

In closing

Return to my friend's question.

Was Confucius's journey among the states a Chinese Odyssey?

No. What does not resemble the Odyssey is what matters.

The end of Odysseus's road waited for him throughout. The road could therefore be completed, and he completed it.

The end of Confucius's road never appeared. It required another person to receive it, and he did not meet that person in his lifetime.

Another difference may be even more worth remembering.

Odysseus had a Penelope, someone who waited twenty years and, at the moment of his return, doubted him before recognizing him.

Confucius had no one like that. He met courtesy without verification and ridicule that verified and left. No one doubted him and stayed.

In the end, he made the object of verification himself and left it there.

The endings are asymmetrical.

Odysseus returns home and the story ends, though Homer specifically adds that he must depart once more, as if he knew a completed journey leaves no further story.

What Confucius left has been accepted, overturned, reinterpreted, and used as a weapon against other things for more than two thousand years.

All of that is verification.

He did find the people he was waiting for, just not while he was alive.

To ask which man is greater is to ask a poor question.

The home Odysseus sought was real, and people were waiting for him. People were also waiting in Confucius's home during his fourteen years abroad, and he did not return.

This is not a hierarchy. Two different things were placed in the position of an end.

One lay within the traveler's own power and existed upon arrival. The other lay in another person's hands and might remain absent for an entire life.

And if the second kind of end is finally realized, the traveler did not realize it alone.

14. How Should We Face “If Only I Had”?

A friend asked what to do when we cannot stop thinking, “If only I had done this or that.”

There is plenty of advice on the subject, and most of it does not work. Some tells us not to look back. Some says everything happens for the best. Between them lies the instruction to make peace with oneself.

Perhaps these sayings fail not because they are bad, but because they treat two different things as one.

Regret has two entirely different structures.

One is: I did not know then; I know now.

The other is: I knew then and did it anyway.

They must be discussed separately. What is true of the first sounds evasive when applied to the second. What is true of the second is too heavy for the first.

1. When we did not know

Begin with the first kind.

It has a strange feature that becomes obvious once stated:

We can now see the mistake precisely because we took that step.

The vision capable of recognizing the problem is not something we always possessed and simply forgot to use. That stretch of road gave it to us.

Only after changing jobs do we discover what matters to us. Only after trusting the wrong person do we recognize a certain tone of voice. Only after ruining something do we understand which step cannot be omitted.

All these forms of judgment are products of the experience itself.

So when we ask, “How could I have failed to see it?” the problem was not that our former self lacked intelligence. We did not yet possess the glasses. The experience itself ground their lenses.

Kierkegaard wrote a sentence in his journals that has since been quoted countless times: life can only be understood backward, but it must be lived forward.

The sentence is usually treated as a reflection. It actually identifies a hard problem of sequence. Understanding and living move in opposite directions, and the directions cannot be reversed.

The first kind of regret judges the person we were with the judgment we possess now.

That earlier person did not have it. The problem was not a refusal to use it. It did not exist.

This is not consolation. Consolation would say that we were not wrong. The judgment that the choice was wrong may stand; the time at which we expect the judgment to have been available does not.

2. Of the two roads we compare, only one is real

The first kind of regret contains another, subtler problem in the way it compares alternatives.

The words “if only” are followed by another road. That road is being paved now.

When we pave an imaginary road, we include only what we can currently imagine.

It contains no accidents, no turns that were impossible to foresee, and none of the troubles that emerge only halfway through. It is flat and unobstructed because it is a model, not a span of lived time.

The road we actually traveled contains every pothole.

We therefore compare a real road containing all its accidents with an imagined road containing none.

This does not mean the other road would necessarily have been worse. It may truly have been better. We do not know and never will.

It means only that we do not possess that road for comparison. We possess something made today in the shape of today's wishes.

3. When we knew at the time

The second kind of regret is entirely different and should not be dissolved.

We knew it was wrong and acted anyway. We wanted convenience, feared trouble, sought a small advantage, or simply refused responsibility in that moment.

This regret is valid. It does not confuse the sequence of time or reveal insufficient judgment.

None of the preceding explanations applies, and none should. To say “I didn't know then” about something one plainly knew is to excuse oneself.

But one thing is worth saying, and it is not consolation.

The fact that the act still hurts tells us something.

A person suffers from having betrayed something only because that thing remains within them. If it were gone, the act would feel normal, or would not be remembered at all.

The pain is therefore evidence: we still recognize the line we failed to hold.

This does not make the act unimportant. It matters. It means only that the place still hurting is exactly what we have not lost.

4. If another person was hurt

A qualification must be inserted here, or the previous section will slide in a bad direction.

If the second kind of regret concerns another person, regret should not be directed primarily at our own feelings.

To chew repeatedly on one's guilt is still an activity centered on oneself. It may be sincere, but it points inward while the person affected remains outside.

What can be done is often plain: speak clearly, make restitution, or simply let the other person know that we understand. Sometimes nothing can be done. That too is real, and then we carry it.

In any case, the central question should not be “How can I feel better?”

5. The force of regret exists now

One feature deserves attention.

The content of regret concerns the past, but all its force exists in the present.

Someone says, “I regret not learning that subject.” The sentence sounds as though it concerns the past. Its only living part is that the person wants to learn now.

Someone says, “I regret not treating that person better.” The living part is that they now know what such care means and are capable of giving it.

The past cannot move. The force can land only in the present because the present is where it already resides.

This does not mean converting regret into motivational fuel. That account is too quick and often false. It identifies only the location of the force. It is not in the place to which we cannot return, but in the place where we stand.

6. Nietzsche's question

Nietzsche wrote a passage many people treat as a metaphysical claim. It is really a question.

Suppose, he says, that late one night a demon slipped into your loneliest loneliness and told you: this life you now live must be lived again, and lived countless times. Nothing will be new. Every pain, every joy, every thought and sigh will return in the same sequence.

Then Nietzsche asks whether you would throw yourself down and gnash your teeth, or say that you had never heard anything more divine.

The question does not ask whether our lives are good. It asks what relation we bear to the road we have traveled.

It also contains a rarely noticed feature: we cannot pass the test by changing the past. We can pass only by changing the present, because the life to be repeated includes every part beginning now.

7. A necessary qualification

Kierkegaard's sentence has a second half that is often omitted.

People remember that life can only be understood backward, he says, but forget that it must be lived forward. He also makes a finer point: no moment of life can ever be completely understood because time never stops to let us see the whole.

This sentence cuts into the argument of this essay and must therefore be included.

The vision with which we judge the past today will itself be judged later.

Looking back ten years from now, we may discover that today's judgment was also crude and omitted things we could not yet see.

This does not mean we should avoid judgment. It means we should not treat today's judgment as a final verdict.

That changes the weight of both kinds of regret. In the first, the “mistake” we identify today may not be the final conclusion. In the second, neither may today's condemnation of ourselves.

In closing

Return to my friend's question.

What should we do when we keep thinking “if only”?

First distinguish which kind of regret it is.

If we did not know then, the judgment may be valid while the timing we assign to it is not. We can see the mistake because the event gave us the eyes with which to see it. That vision was a product of the road, not a condition the road should already have possessed.

And the road used for comparison was paved today. It contains no accidents. It is smooth because no one has traveled it.

If we knew at the time, we should not dissolve the regret. It is real. The fact that it continues to hurt means we still recognize the line we crossed. If another person was involved, our attention should move toward them rather than inward toward our own feelings.

The words “if only” are always followed by a road we have made ourselves.

The place where we stand now was reached by another road, one that was actually traveled.

15. Why Do We Keep Changing the Measure of Our Worth?

A friend made an interesting observation.

When people are young, they feel powerless before physical strength. In middle age, they feel powerless before wealth. In old age, they feel powerless before wisdom.

The observation is perceptive, but it rests on a false premise. Once that premise is removed, the matter becomes clearer.

The premise is that the three measures follow age.

1. A person who does not grow may still envy wealth at eighty

Begin with what does not hold.

Not everyone reaches old age and begins to admire wisdom.

Many people compare wealth at sixty and continue at eighty. They compare their children's incomes, the locations of their homes, the differences in old classmates' pensions, and where each person's children work.

They never reached the third measure because it does not arrive automatically with age.

The three measures are therefore not three ages, but three stages. They often accompany age because time takes things away and forces us to change measures. But losing one measure does not mean replacing it with one of our own.

Many people are merely forced from the first to the second, where they remain for life.

2. Powerlessness measures not what is missing, but how many measures remain

Why do these things produce a sense of powerlessness?

The usual explanation is that we care most about what we lack. That is incomplete because it does not explain why the measure changes.

I think the real mechanism is this: at each stage, only one measure remains in a person's hand, and losing by the only measure means losing everything.

At the beginning, a person has no money, position, or experience. Physical strength is the only thing available for comparison. It matters not because strength is inherently so important, but because the other measures are not yet available.

At the next stage, physical strength has begun to decline while experience has not yet become what we would call wisdom. Money is the clearest, most easily ranked measure, and the one that most visibly displays differences.

Powerlessness therefore reveals not “what do I lack?” but “how many measures do I currently have?”

The fewer the measures, the more total each defeat becomes.

3. Their rotation proves that none is fundamental

The very fact that the three measures rotate tells us something else.

Anything replaced by the next measure is not fundamental.

If strength were fundamental, people would not later turn to money. If money were fundamental, no one would turn from it to something else. The rotation proves that the measures are interchangeable.

They can replace one another because they share a quality: each can be compared and ranked, showing who has more and who has less.

What powerlessness actually measures is therefore none of the three things. It measures the fact that I am standing on a scale and someone is above me.

What the scale measures is secondary.

4. The third measure is the most dangerous, and dangerous in a different way

If a person truly reaches the third stage, the powerlessness there differs from the first two.

In the first two, they know they are comparing external things. Strength belongs to the body; money is a number. Neither is plainly identical with the self.

Wisdom feels like the self.

In the final comparison, a person believes they are comparing what kind of person they are. To lose by the first two measures is to lose something. To lose by the third feels like losing oneself.

Another fact intensifies the pain.

Time takes away the first two, so defeat can be attributed to time. No sixty-year-old considers it a moral failure to be physically weaker than someone of twenty.

Wisdom is the only one of the three that time is supposed to give rather than take.
