Losing to another person in wisdom therefore cannot be attributed to age. One cannot say, “I have grown old.”

5. Those who remain at the second stage rarely feel that they have stopped growing

Something unflattering but important must be said.

A person who remains at the measure of money rarely believes they have stopped. They are more likely to think they have seen through everything. Talk of cultivation or spiritual attainment is merely a beautiful consolation invented by people who cannot succeed.

The view is coherent at that level.

On the scale of money, wisdom produces no reading. It yields no number, cannot be ranked, and cannot be exchanged for anything concrete.

Whatever a measure cannot register does not exist from the measure's point of view.

The essay on the narrow gate made a similar point. The people on the broad road are not failing to look closely. The other thing does not exist within their coordinates.

That is why outside words have difficulty moving this stage. Anything said must first pass through its measure, and the measure cannot register the words.

6. Earlier measures grow lighter as one moves upward, though they do not disappear

An easy formulation must be corrected here.

In the essay on the fear of death, I argued that a person who travels farther loses none of the earlier fears but simply acquires another.

Powerlessness works somewhat differently.

The earlier forms remain, but they genuinely grow lighter. The reason appeared in the second section.

The intensity of powerlessness lies not in the measure itself but in the proportion of the whole self it occupies.

At twenty, losing a comparison of strength may keep someone awake all night. At fifty, they will probably laugh and move on.

This is not because the person no longer values bodily health. The difference is that strength now occupies one small part rather than the whole.

The measure and its reading remain. What changes is the share of the self represented by that reading.

This is why fear and powerlessness differ at this point.

Fear has an object. It concerns the loss of a particular thing. Possessing additional things does not make us less afraid of losing the original one.

Powerlessness is relative. It measures one's position on a scale. The more scales we possess, the less any one position represents the whole.

But we should not conclude that moving upward makes life more comfortable.

The new measure is the most painful. Its reading cannot be attributed to time, and it feels like the self.

The precise formulation is therefore: the old burdens become lighter, while the new one is heavier. Whether their sum is greater or smaller cannot be said and will differ by person.

7. Confucius and Aristotle say the same thing, and it contradicts the opening observation

A passage in the Analects closely resembles this question.

Confucius says that the exemplary person guards against three things: in youth, when the vital energies are not settled, against sensual desire; in maturity, when the energies are strong, against combativeness; in old age, when the energies have declined, against acquisitiveness.

The final word is easily mistaken for the acquisition of wisdom or attainment. Traditional commentaries agree, however, that it means material gain.

Huang Kan's commentary is blunt: “Gain means greed for gain. The old are prone to greed and must therefore guard against it.” He gives a reason. Youth resembles spring and summer, whose active force gives things outward. Old age resembles autumn and winter, whose receptive force gathers inward. The old therefore tend to accumulate and become greedy.

The Huainanzi is even shorter: human nature is reckless in youth, violent in maturity, and fond of profit in old age.

Fond of profit in old age. The object is wealth, not wisdom.

Here Confucius and Aristotle meet.

Describing old people in the Rhetoric, Aristotle also says they love money because they have learned how difficult it is to acquire and how easy it is to lose.

Separated by thousands of kilometers, both place greed in old age.

That seems to contradict my friend's observation, which associates old age with envy of wisdom.

Who is right?

Both, because they are describing different people.

Confucius and Aristotle describe the old person who did not move upward, the person from the first section who still compares children's incomes and pensions at eighty. As age leaves fewer things within their grasp, they grip what remains more tightly.

My friend's old person is the one who moved upward.

The two do not conflict because age was never what separated them.

Another word deserves notice: “guard.”

Confucius does not say, “This is what naturally occurs in old age.” He says, “This is what one must guard against.”

If age automatically produced a good state, no warning would be needed.

The three stages are not three ascending steps but three warnings against default directions, places toward which we slide without vigilance.

That agrees with the central argument of this essay: upward movement is not automatic.

We can extend the warning one step further, though this is an inference Confucius himself did not make.

At the stage of wisdom, the corresponding form of “gain” would be the desire to treat wisdom as something one can possess, count, and compare.

Finally, the three warnings are still not a timetable for other people. In the essay on meaning, I noted that in Confucius's account of setting his heart on learning at fifteen and standing firm at thirty, the subject throughout is “I.” The same holds here. Later readers turn it into an age chart, mistaking a personal account for an external standard.

8. Who gave us the measures?

We can now ask a deeper question.

Why do people hold so few measures?

Not because of age. The measures were given by other people.

Strength can be compared because a ready-made method of comparison exists. Wealth can be compared because its method is already available and precise to the last unit. Neither measure was cut by oneself. Both can be received and used immediately, which makes them convenient.

Convenience has a price. A ready-made measure can register only what it was designed to measure.

If every measure in a person's hand was received from elsewhere, the places where they feel powerless will be few, and changing from one to another will never escape the same category.

By contrast, something a person has cut for themselves usually cannot be compared with anyone else's, because it belongs to no ready-made scale.

What cannot be compared produces no ranking and therefore no powerlessness.

This does not make the person superior. It means only that they possess something that does not participate in ranking. Once even one such thing exists, the condition of having “only one measure” disappears.

In closing

Return to my friend's observation.

In youth we envy strength, in middle age wealth, and in old age wisdom.

The three often appear in this order, but they are stages rather than ages. Someone who never arrives may remain at the second measure at eighty.

The observation is useful not as a chart against which to compare one's age, but as a prompt for a question answerable now: By which measure was the thing that makes me feel powerless assessed, and who gave me that measure?

If the measure was received, the powerlessness does not really say that I am inadequate. It says that I am using someone else's scale.

If I cut the measure myself, it will probably produce no such reading, because no one else appears upon it.

As for the final stage, Confucius's warning still applies, though it must be moved.

He warns the old against greed for wealth. If someone truly reaches the measure of wisdom, there is a corresponding danger: treating wisdom as something that can be owned, counted, and compared.

The moment we want to possess wisdom, it becomes another comparative measure.

At that moment, we have fallen from the third stage back into the second.

16. Should We Forgive?

A friend asked: a long time has passed, and the person is still here. Should I forgive them or not?

Let me begin with what this essay will not provide. It will not tell anyone whether they should forgive.

This is not evasion. Any account that decides the matter for another person has already gone wrong. The first section explains why.

1. Three ready-made answers, none of which holds

Three familiar accounts of forgiveness cover three directions. Each contains a problem.

The first says that forgiveness is magnanimity, the generosity a person ought to possess.

This treats forgiveness as something one person can dispense. When someone says, “I forgive you,” they occupy the position of granting a pardon.

The position is difficult. It turns a matter between two people into one person's judgment upon another.

The second account has become especially popular in recent years: forgiveness is not for the other person but for you. It means setting yourself free.

This is compassionate, and it addresses a real problem. Resentment is exhausting.

But it also does something that may sound harsh when stated: it reduces the other person from a person to a stone in my mind.

They are no longer someone with thoughts of their own, capable of change and further action. They are a variable in my emotional state, and I release them so that I may feel lighter.

An earlier essay said that to treat a person as a means is to value them according to their usefulness to me.

“Let go for your own sake” handles the person according to their effect on my feelings.

The third direction refuses forgiveness: they did that thing, which proves what kind of person they are. They will be that way for life.

This too has a problem, and it is the easiest to overlook.

The structure of refusing forgiveness fixes a person to an event. I do not look at who they are now or ask what they later became.

In the essay on love, I said that a person is whole but not complete. They are still growing.

To treat someone still growing as a finished object incapable of change is itself to stop treating them as a person.

All three answers touch something real, and all three fail somewhere. Perhaps the problem lies not in the answers but in the word.

2. “Forgiveness” confuses three different acts

Look closely, and people may mean three entirely different things when they speak of forgiveness.

The first is to stop demanding.

No longer demand compensation or an apology, no longer need the person to suffer, no longer conduct the trial repeatedly in one's mind.

One person can do this alone, and it concerns that person's own actions.

The second is to restore a relationship.

Treat the person again as someone with whom one can associate, speak, and sit at the same table.

One person cannot do this alone because a relationship belongs to two. The other person must be present and must have moved.

The third is to cancel the event.

Treat it as if it never happened, or as if it did not matter very much.

No one can do this.

3. Why no one can perform the third act

Because the event happened.

It remains in the world and in the person who was hurt. Nothing anyone says can move it from “having happened” back to “not having happened.”

This sounds obvious, but it has a concrete consequence.

Any forgiveness whose goal is to act as though the event never occurred is false.

Many people become stuck here. They believe forgiveness requires them to say the event did not matter. It plainly did, so they cannot say it, and they conclude that they are not good enough.

The problem is not their goodness. They are being asked to do something impossible.

Of the two possible acts, the first concerns how one acts, and the second requires another person. Neither requires anyone to say that the event did not matter.

4. Forgiveness is not decided; it grows

There is another mismatch, of the same kind discussed in the essay on regret.

That essay argued that expecting one's former self to see what only the present self can see is an error of sequence.

Here the mismatch is this: forgiveness is often treated as a decision. “I decide to forgive you.”

If the person's heart still hurts, the sentence is a decision, not something that has already occurred.

When forgiveness truly occurs, it is more like suddenly noticing one day that the event no longer sounds so loud when remembered. It grows; it is not decided.

“I decide to forgive you” is often false not because the speaker is insincere, but because it speaks of something organic as if it were a switch.

This has another consequence. If forgiveness grows, no one is obliged to make it grow by a particular deadline.

No one is obliged to make it grow at all.

5. Confucius refuses cheap forgiveness

A brief passage in the Analects refuses it decisively.

Someone asks Confucius what he thinks of repaying resentment with virtue.

Confucius asks in return: with what, then, will you repay virtue?

His answer is: repay resentment with uprightness, and virtue with virtue.

The crucial word is “uprightness.”

It does not mean revenge. Revenge would repay resentment with resentment, which Confucius does not say.

Nor does it mean forgiveness. That would be repaying resentment with virtue, the very suggestion he has rejected.

The closest sense is to treat the matter as it actually is.

Call the event what it is. Neither enlarge nor diminish it. Do not say it never happened because the other person is pitiable; do not say it was heavier than it was because we are in pain.

Confucius's question is the real blade. If I repay even those who harm me with virtue, what remains with which to repay those who treated me well?

Cheap forgiveness wrongs not only oneself. It deprives goodness of weight. If every action receives the same response, good and harmful acts become indistinguishable in their consequences.

6. Arendt: forgiveness as a remedy for irreversibility

In The Human Condition, Hannah Arendt places forgiveness somewhere very different.

Human action, she argues, has two fundamental difficulties. What has been done cannot be recalled, and what will happen cannot be known.

Human beings developed two responses: forgiveness addresses the first; promises address the second.

Without forgiveness to release us from the consequences of what we have done, she writes, our capacity to act would remain bound forever to a single deed. We would remain its victims, like an apprentice unable to break the spell they cast.

Notice who is bound.

Arendt means not only the person harmed, but also the person who acted.

If everything a person has done remains attached to them forever and can never be loosened, they can never begin anything new. The rest of their life becomes only an aftereffect of that one deed.

Forgiveness, for Arendt, therefore concerns the person rather than the act.

The act remains. It happened. What is released is the person, so that they may still do something else.

This distinction also reveals the cost of refusing forgiveness. To nail a person forever to what they have done is to declare that the rest of their life can only be its consequence.

Arendt also draws a line. Some acts, she believes, can neither be punished nor forgiven. They exceed the capacity of human affairs to deal with them.

That line must also be stated. An essay on forgiveness that implies everything should be forgiven becomes a form of pressure.

Some things need not be forgiven. The sentence requires no defense.

7. Asking forgiveness can itself use another person

There is another layer rarely discussed.

In Simon Wiesenthal's book The Sunflower: On the Possibilities and Limits of Forgiveness, he recounts being taken, while imprisoned in a concentration camp, to the bedside of a dying SS soldier. The man confessed what he had done and asked for forgiveness.

Wiesenthal said nothing and walked out.

He later wrote down the event and asked dozens of people what they would have done. The book gathers their conflicting answers.

The request contains a structure worth examining on its own.

The soldier wanted release. He did not seek the people he had harmed; they were dead. He sought a Jew. Any Jew would do.

In that moment, Wiesenthal was not addressed as himself. He was used as the representative of a category, with the function of allowing another person to die in peace.

The request itself therefore treated a person as a means.

The same structure appears more generally.

In the essay on “I only want what's best for you,” I wrote that the sentence transforms another person's refusal. “I disagree” becomes “you have betrayed my goodwill.”

A request for forgiveness can sometimes do the same.

A person apologizes sincerely and sincerely asks to be forgiven. The sincerity is real. But after the request, if the other person says no, that “no” is no longer merely a judgment. It becomes an act of cruelty.

The pressure changes direction. I harmed you, but now the question is whether you can be generous enough to me.

This does not mean we should not apologize. An apology and a request for forgiveness are different acts.

In an apology, I state clearly what I did and hand that statement over. In asking forgiveness, I also ask for something in return.

The first remains entirely on my side. The second requires the other person to give.

8. What remains?

Put these points together, and little can be said, but what remains is concrete.

A person can stop demanding. This requires no cooperation and does not require saying that the event did not matter.

A person cannot cancel the event. No one can.

Nor should anyone be required to complete forgiveness by a certain time. It is not a decision but something that grows.

Two things should not be done.

We should not nail a person forever to the event, because they are still growing, and to treat someone still growing as a finished thing is not to treat them as a person.

Nor should we cover our own pain. The event continues to sound within us. Pretending that it does not sacrifices ourselves to a respectable formulation.

The two prohibitions may seem to point in opposite directions. They are the same: do not turn the other person into a finished object, and do not erase one's own remainder.

Whether forgiveness eventually grows cannot be decided by these principles.

9. The final night of the Iliad

I want to end with a scene. It gives no answer, but it is the closest image I know of what genuine forgiveness might look like.

Near the end of the Iliad, Achilles kills Hector and drags his body around the city.

That night, Priam, the old king of Troy, enters the Greek camp alone and finds Achilles.

The first thing he does is kneel and kiss Achilles' hands. Homer adds a line: these are the hands that killed many of his sons.

Priam does not say, “I forgive you.” Nor does he demand an apology. He asks for his son's body.

Achilles looks at the old man and remembers his own father.

Then the two weep together. Priam weeps for his son. Achilles weeps for his dead friend and for the father he will never see again.

Afterward, Achilles returns the body and grants the Trojans a truce long enough to complete the funeral.

No one in the scene speaks the word “forgiveness.”

Hector remains dead. Achilles knows that his own life will be short. The event has not been canceled and cannot be.

Only one thing occurs that night: two enemies each see that the person across from them is also a person.

In closing

Should we forgive?

The question may be too large. The word contains three acts, one of which no one can perform.

We can stop demanding. That requires no one's permission and does not require declaring the event unimportant.

We can hope that one day the event will sound less loudly when remembered. Hope cannot produce this. We can only wait for it to grow.

We cannot act as if the event never happened.

And there are two things we should not do: nail the person forever to that day, or cover our own pain.

Forgiveness itself may resemble what occurred in the tent that night. No one gave an object to anyone else. Two people each saw that the one across from them was also a person.

That requires both people to be present.

If the other person is gone, or never intended to be present, it may never happen.

That is all right. No one owes anyone forgiveness.

17. Can a Well-Intentioned Lie Be Right?

A friend asked whether a well-intentioned lie is permissible. Is it right?

The question usually receives one of two ready-made answers. Neither is very useful.

The first says absolutely not. A lie is a lie, and a good motive does not make it true.

The second says to judge by the consequences. If everyone is better off afterward, the lie is permissible.

The first is so rigid that almost no one actually lives by it. The second has a more difficult problem: Who calculates the consequences? Usually it is the person who wants to lie, and that person can always calculate a favorable result.

Perhaps the question is not whether we should lie, but whether truth and falsehood are the only dimension along which the matter should be judged.

1. The criterion does not lie in truth or falsehood

Begin with a strange fact.

Some statements are entirely false and cause almost no harm. A doctor asks how you feel, and you say “fine” though you feel terrible. The statement is false, but it takes nothing from anyone.

Other statements are true in every word yet thoroughly manipulative.

Someone omits every fact unfavorable to themselves and states only the true ones, assembling them into a completely false picture without telling a single lie.

Truth and falsehood alone therefore fail to capture what matters.

What does matter?

I think it is this: after the statement has been made, can the other person still verify matters for themselves?

2. Three different acts

We should first separate three things that are often confused.

The first is silence.

No one is obliged to tell everything to everyone. Privacy is a right, not a deception.

The second is partial disclosure.

This is more complicated. It may be considerate or manipulative. The difference is whether the other person knows that further parts exist.

The third is manufacturing a belief.

This means placing something contrary to fact in another person's mind without their knowledge and leading them to make a decision on that basis.

Only the third is what we ordinarily call a lie, and only it has a particular consequence: the other person bears the cost of the decision, while I supplied the material on which the decision was based.

3. What does the statement take away?

The criterion can now be stated clearly.

The problem with a statement is not whether it corresponds to fact, but whether it deprives another person of the capacity to verify.

Seen this way, untrue statements can carry radically different weight.

A falsehood punctured by a single question leaves the other person able to ask, investigate, and discover. Though false, it has not canceled any capacity.

A complete, internally coherent false construction that closes every route of verification belongs to another order.

In an earlier essay on Evergrande, I argued that financial fraud does not merely put a lie in a report. It turns something that ought to be verifiable into something that appears open to verification but in fact cannot be checked.

That is the deeper wrong.

This criterion is therefore not more lenient than “never lie.” It is stricter.

It also captures manipulation that contains no false sentence: selecting only favorable truths, creating an environment in which no one can ask questions, or hiding crucial material where another person cannot find it.

Such actions are innocent on the dimension of truth and falsehood. They are not innocent on this one.

4. Plato's example is the most candid of its kind

The Republic contains a famous proposal usually called the noble lie.

Plato has the rulers tell all citizens a story. They are brothers and sisters born from the earth, but when the god made them, he mixed gold into some, silver into others, and bronze or iron into still others. What each person should do is therefore fixed by birth.

Plato says this serves the good of the city. He also acknowledges that the story is invented.

One feature of the design deserves attention: it is made impossible to verify.

Citizens have no way to investigate which metal was mixed into them. This is not a flaw in the design. It is exactly why the story can work.

The essay on “I only want what's best for you” described what that sentence does. It turns another person's refusal from “I disagree” into “you have betrayed my goodwill.”

The noble lie performs the same action at the scale of a city. It decides on behalf of everyone what they ought to believe, on the ground that the belief will be good for them, and removes their capacity to check.

Plato is candid. He knows what he is doing and gives his reasons.

That candor makes the example especially useful. It shows that lies of this kind rarely arise from malice. Almost always, they arise from some version of “I know what is better for you.”

5. Kant's blade is aimed directly at this essay

The argument cannot continue without confronting someone who specifically rejected this case-by-case approach.

Late in life, Kant wrote a short essay responding to a hypothetical case. If a murderer comes to your door and asks whether your friend is hiding inside, may you lie?

Kant's answer is no.

For more than two centuries, the answer has been presented as evidence of deontological madness. But his reason deserves attention because it does not concern consequences.

A lie, he says, damages not only the person deceived but something held in common by humanity: the possibility of taking speech as true.

He too is concerned with structure rather than consequences.

And his real concern is precisely the risk in this essay.

The moment you say, “It depends on the circumstances,” the person judging the circumstances is the person who wants to lie.

Someone who wants to lie can always invent a plausible circumstance.

The objection is substantial, and I will not evade it.

An earlier essay in this series offered one response while discussing “I only want what's best for you”: motives cannot be distinguished, but actions can.

Genuinely wanting the best for someone and needing them to obey while sincerely believing that you want the best feel identical to the speaker. “My motive is good” therefore cannot grant permission.

The criterion cannot be goodwill. It must be observable from outside: after I speak, can the other person still verify matters for themselves?

Lying to the murderer at the door deprives no one of the capacity to verify, because the murderer's current purpose is itself to cancel another person.

Lying to someone who trusts you and will arrange their life on the basis of your words is different.

This does not refute Kant. His warning remains in force and should hang over every discussion of this kind. Whenever “this time is permissible” sounds reasonable, first ask who is judging this time.

6. The minimum requirement

If the matter can be condensed into something usable, I would put it in two sentences.

Say what you did.

Say whom you did it for.

The requirement sounds so modest as barely to count. It does not require saying everything, being selfless, or even being kind.

It asks only that we create no false appearance in these two matters.

Why these two?

Because they are all the other person has with which to judge the relationship.

What you did determines their judgment of the facts. Whom you acted for determines their judgment of you.

To lie about either means that you issued the measure by which they understand the situation and altered its markings.

Everything else may be withheld or described vaguely. That is privacy, not the destruction of the measure.

As an aside, this requirement is low enough for almost everyone to meet, and almost no one meets it consistently.

7. One kind of untruth does not belong to this question at all

One final thing should be separated from the category of well-intentioned lies.

In his dialogues, Socrates often sees exactly where another person's account fails but does not say so. He asks questions.

Sometimes he even pretends not to understand and asks the other person to teach him.

Taken literally, this is untrue.

But it removes nothing. The opposite is true. It leaves the step of arrival to the other person.

An earlier essay compared Socrates to a midwife. She does not give birth for the laboring person. She eases the pain and judges whether what emerges is genuine or a phantom. She withholds the answer not because she wishes to conceal it.

If she states the answer directly, it becomes a sentence handed over rather than something grown by the other person.

Some apparent concealment is therefore structurally opposite to lying.

A lie inserts into another person something they cannot verify. Socratic withholding could give the answer directly but leaves a space so that the other person can take the step.

One inserts; the other leaves room.

So can a well-intentioned lie be right?

The question is framed in terms of truth and falsehood, but that is not the most important dimension.

What matters is whether the other person retains the capacity to verify after the statement is made.

A false statement punctured by one question may take nothing away. An account that leaves no route of inquiry may remove what matters most even when every word is true.

Goodwill is not a permit.

Only the person acting knows whether the intention is good, and that person is the least reliable witness in the matter.

We can observe only the action.

Say what you did. Say whom you did it for.

Everything else may remain unsaid.

18. Should We Help an Elderly Person Who Collapses?

The question is painful, and it stirs one of our deepest doubts. Put differently, it asks whether human beings should still follow the rules of being human.

Let us first establish what happened. At 12:50 p.m. on August 17, 2026, in Qidong, Hunan, a man who had retired a year earlier entered a neighborhood mahjong and card shop and sat on a chair by the door. He knew the proprietor, Xiao Aihua, and had visited almost every day for the previous two weeks. At 12:54:30, Xiao noticed something wrong. She approached and spoke, but he did not respond. She later said she panicked and could not even find where to dial on her phone, so she called for someone nearby to contact emergency services. At 12:55:01, she and two others moved him outside the door. The shop had no air conditioning, the day was hot, and she thought he had suffered heatstroke. A local saying in the Qidong dialect holds that someone with heatstroke must “see the sky,” meaning go outside for fresh air. The ambulance arrived at 1:01 and took him away at 1:03. He did not survive.

It happened that quickly. Less than a minute passed between Xiao's noticing something wrong and the emergency call. Six minutes passed between her moving him and the ambulance's arrival.

What she did during that minute required no instruction. When someone collapses, you go to them, ask what is wrong, call for help, summon an ambulance, and move them somewhere with air. These are the rules of being human. They have no statute, burden of proof, or termination clause. People once dealt with one another according to them. Everything that followed occurred after she did so.

From beginning to end, the shop's cameras recorded all thirteen minutes. The recording became the common starting point of every later disagreement. The family watched it and argued that the rescue had been mishandled and bore some responsibility for the death. They sought 100,000 yuan in compensation. The police watched the same recording and concluded that the man had died of a sudden illness, with no external injury or conflict, and ruled out a criminal case. The video, they said, showed Xiao actively rendering aid throughout, promptly seeking medical help, and acting properly and in good faith.

The camera did not end the disagreement. It moved the disagreement from what occurred to whether the action was right. It could answer the first question, not the second. Xiao moved the man outside at 12:55:01 because she believed he needed to “see the sky.” Someone watching later could call the same act dragging him outdoors. At the scene, an action has one reason. In retrospect, it can acquire many.

One sentence is worth carrying away: only someone who acts leaves an action available for review. Had Xiao remained still, the recording would contain no act open to reinterpretation. A mechanism that scrutinizes actions alone will reliably push people toward inaction. No one needs to be malicious. It requires only a cause of action for improper rescue.

The first formal mediation took place on August 18. The family asked for 100,000 yuan. Chen Xiaomao, a staff member of the local Comprehensive Governance Center, said the mediators explained the legal rules in person. Under China's Civil Code, a party seeking tort damages must prove fault. They also explained that mediation was voluntary and would end if either party objected. The file shows that Xiao insisted she had not been at fault but voluntarily offered 2,000 yuan in humanitarian assistance. The family reduced its demand to 50,000 but would go no lower, and mediation paused. Because the weather was hot, the mediators suggested that the family proceed with cremation that afternoon, which they did.

The second mediation occurred on August 19, with the parties in separate rooms. The family lowered its demand to 20,000 yuan. Remembering that the man was a neighbor, one of Xiao's relatives referred to local custom and similar cases and voluntarily offered 15,000. A report described the next step this way: considering the energy and time consumed by the dispute, and the possibility of secondary problems, the parties gradually reached a consensus at 19,000 yuan. Xiao's husband transferred the money on the spot.

The rules had therefore been explained clearly. The burden of proof was made clear. So was the right to end mediation at any time. Xiao herself first offered 2,000 yuan; her relative first offered 15,000 and introduced the calculation based on similar incidents. Another staff member, Liu Lingang, said, “We have no authority to force anyone to pay.” The body had been cremated the day before the agreement, so objectively there was no possibility of coercion by refusing burial. The parties disagreed about whether such threats had been made earlier. The dead man's wife denied it in her statement; Xiao's side said similar words had been used early on but acknowledged that nothing was actually done.

She took every step herself, and the result was that a person formally found free of fault paid 19,000 yuan.

Her son later explained why. The family had originally wanted to give only two or three thousand. They eventually accepted 19,000 because of Xiao's health. Since the incident, she had been unable to sleep.

This is the most important sentence in the case. The 19,000 yuan did not purchase responsibility, because there was none. It purchased an end to the matter. The price was determined not by right and wrong, but by who could no longer bear the pressure.

At this point, the problem is no longer that no one told her the rules.

Chen Zhong, the head of the Comprehensive Governance Center, put the matter cleanly. In a civil dispute, the person making a claim bears the burden of proof. Mediators lack authority to issue a binding determination. They can only tell both sides that no evidence currently establishes responsibility.

This is not a failure of duty. It defines the position. Mediation is not adjudication and cannot determine liability. It can only explain the rules and facilitate agreement. Explaining a rule and reaching a judgment are different acts. The rule can identify who bears the burden of proof. It cannot declare the matter over. Only agreement can end it, and agreement depends on how much each party can bear.

An official from the Qidong County Justice Bureau spoke candidly. Most local civil disputes are resolved through mediation each year. Mediation is a flexible method that can absorb social conflict and prevent cases from flooding the courts or escalating in place. He also acknowledged how difficult the balance is. Even experienced mediators struggle with the practical problem of protecting a person without fault from loss while not simply passing every conflict upward.

All of this is true. Mediation does absorb conflict and prevent escalation, and the difficulty is real. Precisely because every sentence is true, the structure of the problem becomes clear. A mechanism designed to make matters stop measures success by whether they have stopped, not by who was right. In explaining how 19,000 yuan was reached, the report referred to possible “secondary problems.” Preventing such problems genuinely belongs to the mechanism's purpose, and secondary problems do not care who is right.

What, then, did “voluntary” mean here? Procedurally, Xiao could stand up and say no at any time. The mediator told her so. But after she said no, the body still remained until it was cremated on the afternoon of the first mediation. The weather was hot, her shop faced the street, the dead man was a neighbor, and she had already stopped sleeping.

Voluntary meant that she did not have to sign. It did not mean she could make the matter end. A form of voluntariness that can terminate a procedure but not the situation is a right on paper and an empty sentence at the scene. To know whether someone has a choice, do not ask whether they can say no within the procedure. Ask whether the matter stops after they do.

On the afternoon of August 26, the man's family returned the 19,000 yuan to Xiao's son's account. The transfer note read, “Return of humanitarian compensation.” That evening, with officials witnessing, both sides signed a new statement. The man's son said that the family had gained a deeper understanding of the law during the previous days. A person without fault need not pay compensation, he said, and they now accepted that principle.

It was the same rule Chen Xiaomao had explained in person on August 18. Eight days separated the statements. No new evidence appeared. What changed was that the whole country was watching.

If a rule takes effect only under public scrutiny, then in ordinary times it relies on something other than itself.

Public attention is not a cure. The other side of the same case makes that clear. One of the dead man's relatives said that after signing the agreement, they thought the matter was over. Now the whole country knew, and the family had become villains online. They reported online rumors to the police and requested an investigation.

The August 19 agreement stated that the family would raise no further objection to the dispute or its resolution. Both sides signed it, and neither could use it to end the matter, because the objections never came from either family.

Money traveled in a full circle. A demand for 100,000 yuan became 19,000. A local rice company brought Xiao 20,000 in assistance. Entrepreneur Luo Yonghao and several friends raised another 100,000 for her, explaining that compensation alone was insufficient and people who do good deserve emotional support from society. On August 26, the family returned the 19,000. Xiao's family then donated all 120,000 yuan they had received to the local charity federation.

After the additions and subtractions, the number returned roughly to zero. Other things did not. Xiao suffered insomnia, went to the hospital for an intravenous infusion after feeling ill, closed the shop for several days, and began considering whether to reopen. None of these fit within the 19,000 yuan or the 120,000.

Her decision to give away the 120,000 shows that the money could not set a price for the event. Compensation could not balance it; reward could not either. Every conversion felt wrong because the event never belonged to the category of convertible things.

Xiao said that if she encountered another person who needed help, she would still step forward.

That sentence appears in no mediation agreement, witness statement, transfer note, or trending topic. It is the only thing in the entire case that no procedure processed.

And it was the source of the decision made at 12:54:30. Xiao made that decision alone. No one authorized her during that minute, no one assumed the burden of proof on her behalf, and no document was present. Everything that followed, the agreements, witness statement, assistance payments, donations, returned payment, and online attention, dealt only with what came after that minute.

An institution can restore the losses of a person without fault. It can restore more than the loss or, as here, return the compensation afterward. It cannot make that decision for her. Only she can make it, and she can do so only while remaining someone who decides for herself.

Return to the question: Should we help? Should human beings still follow the rules of being human? The question tests not the human heart, but institutions. When institutions release human agency, following human rules is not difficult. When institutions consume agency, how difficult does it become to hold to them?

Part II: Knowledge and Consciousness

Knowing something is not merely storing the correct answer in the mind. Artificial intelligence, mathematics, philosophy, and the problem of consciousness repeatedly remind us that understanding also concerns who judges, why belief is warranted, and how knowledge changes action. Technology can perform increasingly complex tasks without automatically answering questions of happiness, meaning, or agency. When disputes ask whether a machine can understand, the essays in this part turn the question back upon human beings. What exactly is the understanding we cherish, and are we willing to accept its limits?

19. Why Is AI Producing So Many Mathematical Breakthroughs?

There had indeed been a great deal of mathematical news, and even mathematical drama, by September 2026. The explanation was not the sudden appearance of a universal mathematical algorithm. Rather, reasoning ability, sustained work, tool use, and proof checking had begun to reach research-supporting levels at the same time.

Previously, failure in any one link could destroy the entire task. Once the links began to hold together, results appeared to arrive in clusters.

Consider several recent breakthroughs. On May 20, an internal OpenAI model constructed a new planar point configuration that overturned an important conjecture in the unit-distance problem. External mathematicians checked the proof. It was a new mathematical discovery, though it did not settle every aspect of the unit-distance problem.

On August 10, Anthropic's Claude improved the lower bound on the proportion of zeros of the Riemann zeta function lying on the critical line to about 67.2 percent. By September 2, mathematicians had published a shorter proof. This was an important partial advance, not a proof of the Riemann hypothesis.

On September 4, Anthropic announced a fully machine-checked proof of Fermat's Last Theorem by Claude, reporting that the main work took eleven days. This was a breakthrough in proof formalization, not the first discovery that Fermat's Last Theorem is true.

On September 8, OpenAI announced a Navier-Stokes proof produced by an internal system. It claimed to resolve the relevant version of the official problem through a construction of finite-time singularities with smooth forcing and supplied a Lean formalization.

The dense sequence of headlines therefore included genuine discoveries, advances in verification technology, and results only just entering public scrutiny.

Models now know more and can pursue a line of reasoning farther. A crucial beginning of this shift was training directed at the reasoning process. OpenAI's technical report on o1 said that reinforcement learning improved not only answer accuracy but also the ability to detect errors, divide difficult steps, and change routes when one failed.

Research on DeepSeek-R1 likewise showed reinforcement learning improving checking, reflection, and strategic adjustment in verifiable tasks such as mathematics. This is fundamentally different from making a proof look like a proof. Research does not require dozens of elegant paragraphs of continuous derivation. It requires discovering a problem in step seven, withdrawing that step while preserving the first six, and finding another route.

Why did the change appear sudden? Consider a simplified workflow: propose an idea, calculate examples, find a flaw, repair a lemma, complete the argument. If any link is wholly unreliable, final output may be zero. As every link gradually improves, the success rate of the complete task can jump sharply at a certain point.

The mathematical systems in the headlines were not ordinary chat windows or workloads available through released models. Anthropic disclosed that the research version of Claude that produced the zeta-function result first tried 650 unsuccessful ideas. It then coordinated about sixty subagents for another day and a half. They ran code, performed numerical checks, reviewed one another's arguments, and searched the literature to determine whether the result had precedent.

OpenAI's Navier-Stokes work was larger still. The group that found the result involved about ten thousand concurrent agents and took roughly eighty-eight hours from launch to a candidate solution, followed by separate formalization and verification. These figures came from company process reports. Ordinary users could not deploy the same volume of work.

The better question is therefore not whether a chatbot is smarter than a mathematician. It is what research becomes possible when a sufficiently capable model can organize many parallel attempts, preserve intermediate results, and correct itself over long periods. The number of agents cannot be converted directly into a number of human mathematicians. Repeated exploration, shared blind spots, and coordination failures may render enormous amounts of computation useless.

Anthropic reported that early agents in its Fermat project lost control of project state and failed to collaborate. Progress came only after the team introduced a platform for managing dependencies among theorems. Organizing work effectively was itself part of the advance.

Mathematics is especially well suited to separating bold conjecture from strict acceptance. A language model can propose many ideas, but its own assurance that it has checked them is plainly insufficient. Proof assistants such as Lean offer another form of checking. A proposition and its derivation are written precisely, and the program checks the proof against stated definitions, axioms, and rules of inference. Lean is not another chatbot casting a vote in favor.

This permits a division of labor. Models explore, computational tools test, proof systems check formal derivations, and mathematicians examine the formulation of the problem, its relation to the literature, and its mathematical meaning.

Boundaries remain. Numerical tests cannot replace a general proof. Passing formal verification means only that the encoded proposition follows from the premises used. Mathematicians must still determine whether the encoded proposition accurately represents the original research problem and whether the assumptions smuggle in what was supposed to be proved. But once acceptance becomes reliable enough, exploration can tolerate more failure. Large-scale attempts become much more valuable. Each need not be right; the few genuinely correct routes must be identified and preserved.

The systems are connecting mathematical tools that were previously dispersed. This is closer to the substance of the recent results than mere speed of calculation. OpenAI's unit-distance result applied sophisticated tools from algebraic number theory to a planar geometry problem with an elementary statement. It did not simply scale up a traditional calculation. It found an unexpected route across fields. Claude's zeta-function result likewise depended heavily on existing mathematics, which it recombined into a stronger argument. Mathematicians then rewrote the result as a shorter and more transparent proof. A new research sequence had emerged: AI obtains a result, and human researchers deepen its understanding and simplify it.

Using existing human knowledge is therefore not sufficient reason to deny originality. More meaningful questions are whether the connection was previously unknown, whether the reasoning holds, and whether a genuinely new conclusion follows.

Why did both companies improve at the same time, and why did the number of results suddenly rise? I am inclined to see a shared technological stage rather than a unique mathematical secret possessed by one company. Reinforcement learning for reasoning, greater inference-time computation, code and literature tools, and the organization of long-running tasks were not confined to one laboratory.

Mathematicians were also embracing AI. The volume of experimentation by leading researchers continued to grow. A preprint posted on August 25 examined 330,000 mathematics-related arXiv submissions from March through August 20, 2026. It reported that the share explicitly disclosing a substantive mathematical contribution by AI rose from 1.39 percent in March to 14.09 percent by August 20. The classification relied on the papers' own disclosures and did not verify every result individually, but it suggested that actual adoption, not only capability, was increasing. These were real mathematicians using AI to intensify their work, not work AI could complete by itself.

Logically, even a partial improvement in the success rate of a single research attempt can produce many more results when the number and duration of attempts increase and verification becomes more effective. When the public simultaneously sees several different kinds of advance, the effect is a cascade of breakthroughs.

20. Is Human Consciousness Truly Beyond AI Simulation?

This is not merely Roger Penrose's personal view. He represents a philosophical school that believes human understanding contains a capacity no algorithm can exhaust. We do more than follow rules. We understand their meaning, judge whether they are reliable, and in some cases move beyond them. His claim is therefore not that present AI lacks sufficient computing power. It is that computation alone would still lack a crucial ingredient even if AI improved without limit.

The issue is not that there are mathematical theorems AI can never understand. It is how Penrose moves from limits on computation to the conclusion that human beings must be able to exceed computation.

We ordinarily distinguish a system's ability to perform a task from its possession of subjective experience. Describing pain accurately and feeling pain are not the same. Penrose's claim goes further. He explicitly distinguishes two positions: one says a computer might fully simulate human behavior without inner experience; the other says a computer could not fully simulate all the capacities produced by consciousness. He endorses the stronger second position.

To understand him as saying that AI can behave exactly like a human while remaining empty within would therefore weaken his view. He believes genuine understanding produces differences in capacity and behavior that pure computation cannot fully reproduce. That also explains his particular interest in mathematical understanding. It appears easier to discuss, compare, and test than the question of what redness feels like.

His main basis is Gödel's incompleteness theorem. The mathematically established part is this: for a formal system F that is sufficiently powerful, has mechanically enumerable rules, and is consistent, one can construct an arithmetic proposition G that is true but not provable within the system. Roughly speaking, the proposition says, “I cannot be proved in F.”

Here “cannot be proved” does not mean that a computer is too slow or that no one has yet found a clever method. It means that under the given rules, no such proof exists.

Penrose then asks a further question. We can understand this argument and, if we accept the system's reliability, see the truth of a proposition the system cannot prove. Does human mathematical understanding therefore always have a way to exceed any given algorithmic description? Penrose says yes. Any computational program that attempts to encompass mathematical understanding completely will encounter such a boundary. Understanding itself must therefore contain a noncomputational element.

As an analogy, a machine works within a rulebook. A human being can not only use the book but pick it up as a whole and discuss its limits. Penrose believes that this latter capacity cannot ultimately be contained in another, larger mechanically executable rulebook.

The idea is compelling. But this is precisely where the problem arises: have we proved that human beings can do this, or only that they can under additional assumptions?

Many people disagree with Penrose. The strongest objections focus on the phrase “human beings can.” The first problem is how a person knows that the system is reliable. David Chalmers puts the criticism directly. What a person may understand is only the conditional statement: if F is consistent, then G is true. A sufficiently strong formal system can also prove that conditional. The real difficulty is knowing whether the antecedent holds. For a system complex enough to describe the totality of human mathematical thought, what guarantees that we can identify it, understand it, and know that it is consistent?

Knowing that a given system has limits does not prove that we are free of the limits of every computational system. A person may move beyond one set of rules by using another, more powerful set, without having left computation itself.

The second problem is whether human beings can revise rules through reflection while machines cannot in principle. We should not caricature Penrose as knowing only old computers incapable of learning. In his replies, he explicitly considers artificial neural networks, genetic algorithms, random input, simulated environments, and related approaches. His point is that so long as the entire process of learning and revision remains computational, it still falls within the scope of his argument. But the reverse is also true. We cannot infer noncomputability simply from a human being's ability to alter their own rules. A program capable of revising its strategies remains a program. What must be proved is that the revision exceeds computation, not merely that it differs from rote execution.

The third problem concerns ideal ability. Is Penrose's idealization valid? He does not ignore human fallibility. He explicitly says that he is interested in the capacity, in principle, for correct understanding and error correction in mathematics, not every statement made by every mathematician each day. This prevents the argument from being defeated merely by pointing out that people make mistakes. But it creates another question. Is this ideal capacity a reasonable description of actual human beings, or has the premise already included the capacity to transcend systems correctly and without end?

Even among sympathizers there is disagreement. The logician Solomon Feferman was deeply skeptical that computational models could fully explain the mind, yet still believed that Penrose's Gödelian argument failed to support the skepticism.

Why, then, did Penrose later turn to quantum mechanics and microtubules in the brain? Because he still had to answer another question: if understanding is not computation, what in the brain produces it?

His answer is not a soul beyond physics, but the possibility that physics remains incomplete. In particular, he suspects that the transition from quantum states to the definite results we observe involves a noncomputational physical process not adequately described by current theory. He has also said explicitly that this does not make the brain a quantum computer in the ordinary sense. Quantum computation does not automatically provide the noncomputability his account requires.

Together with the anesthesiologist Stuart Hameroff, Penrose proposed the orchestrated objective reduction theory, or Orch OR. It locates the hypothesized process in the microtubule structures inside neurons. Quantum processes in microtubules are organized and regulated by the biological system and undergo gravity-related objective reduction, producing events of consciousness. This is their proposed mechanism, not an established account of how the brain works.

The proposal must cross several distinct thresholds. Do the relevant quantum processes exist in the required form? Are they noncomputable? Are they necessary for consciousness? How do they produce mathematical understanding? Proving one does not automatically prove the others.

Physical tests can indeed constrain the theory. A 2022 study used spontaneous-radiation experiments to place severe restrictions on versions of Orch OR based on the simplest gravitational-collapse models. The authors also explicitly discussed the scope of the result and more complex models. It cannot be summarized as a decisive refutation of every quantum theory of consciousness.

There is also an independent philosophical problem. Even if a special quantum process were discovered, why would it produce experience rather than merely another unusual physical event? Chalmers has noted that introducing quantum processes does not automatically remove the explanatory difficulty.

Will continued AI progress directly refute Penrose? At least for now, it would not. Suppose an AI independently discovered a new proof. That would be evidence of mathematical discovery, but would not by itself establish subjective experience. Conversely, we cannot define every new discovery made by a computer as something other than understanding simply because a computer made it. That would place the conclusion of the debate inside the definition.

Penrose claims not only that machines lack feelings, but also that pure computation cannot fully reproduce the capacities produced by understanding. As machines become more reliable across a wider range of behaviors, proposing concepts, repairing arguments, identifying premises, and explaining why claims hold, he bears a growing burden to explain what principled difference remains and how it can be recognized. It is not enough to say that humans truly understand while machines merely produce results.

Scientists have another line of research that does not use Gödel's theorem to rule out artificial consciousness in advance. It begins with theories of consciousness and derives structural and functional indicators that can be examined. A 2023 study by Patrick Butlin, Robert Long, and their colleagues, for example, discussed how theories such as recurrent processing and global workspace theory might be used to evaluate artificial systems. The approach does not resolve every dispute, but it turns the question toward mechanisms and evidence rather than judging only whether outputs resemble human behavior.

Penrose's answer therefore cannot be regarded as rigorously proved. Yet I personally find his judgment deeply persuasive. That computational systems possess principled limits is a mathematical fact. That human understanding must exceed every computational system is a further claim requiring additional argument, and perhaps some form of recognition or faith.

21. Why Has Technological Progress Not Made Us Happier?

This is a deep question, with a faintly melodramatic flavor: is the problem the corruption of human nature or the collapse of morality?

I think both human adaptation and institutional arrangements matter, but they are problems at different levels and should not receive an easy fifty-fifty division of blame. Psychological adaptation must explain why we do not remain perpetually excited after receiving a convenience. Institutional analysis must explain why increased productivity still leaves some people without more time, greater stability, or a larger right to decide how they live. The first cannot excuse the second. The second cannot eliminate the first.

Placed beside earlier questions from friends about AI, employment, and the economy, a more important question emerges: society can do more and more, so why has the individual's ability to direct their own life not grown accordingly?

Human beings do become accustomed to good things, but that does not make improved living conditions meaningless. We should distinguish three things: better material conditions, a more pleasant daily mood, and a judgment that one's life is worth living. They are related, but they are not the same.

Suppose a technology frees someone from two hours of exhausting labor every day. The person may be delighted at first and no longer think about the wonderful invention a year later. That does not make the two hours worthless. They may become sleep, time with family, or simply freedom from constant haste. Ceasing to feel excitement about a good life does not erase the difference between a good life and a bad one.

Psychologists do study hedonic adaptation, and emotional responses to some life changes weaken over time. But a meta-analysis combining 188 studies found that patterns differ among events and measures of well-being. Evidence of partial adaptation cannot be expanded into the claim that human beings adapt completely to everything and external conditions therefore do not matter.

Research on income requires the same caution. A joint reanalysis published in the Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences in 2023 found no single income plateau in the relationship between income and happiness that applied to every working adult in its U.S. sample. The authors also emphasized that the evidence was primarily correlational and that income explained only part of the variation in happiness. “Money cannot solve everything” may be true. It does not follow that improved income solves nothing important.

I certainly do not think a discussion of housing, income, or job pressure should begin with “people are simply never satisfied.” That can rewrite unresolved material problems as defects in the character of the people living through them. Especially when it comes from the wealthy, it is the familiar advice of someone speaking comfortably about a burden they do not carry.

The crucial transition is this: Who owns the time saved by technology? Consider a purely hypothetical case. A job once required eight hours and requires four after the adoption of AI. Several arrangements are possible. Employees may work fewer hours. They may work the same hours, produce more, and receive higher pay. Or the company may employ fewer people and use the savings to lower prices, invest, or increase profit.

The same technology can produce each result, with radically different meanings for different lives. “AI doubled productivity” does not entail that an employee gained four hours of free time. Pricing, ownership, employment contracts, bargaining power, and particular corporate choices stand between the two. Technical parameters alone cannot determine the outcome.

Nor is greater output necessarily bad. If a medical service is severely undersupplied, using efficiency gains to serve more people may be more valuable than immediately shortening everyone's hours. The question is not whether every additional unit of output is worthless, but who decides how the new capacity will be used and whether the people affected participate in that decision.

An empirical case is instructive. A study published in Nature Human Behaviour in 2025 followed 2,896 employees across 141 organizations during a six-month trial of a four-day workweek. Pay was maintained and workflows were reorganized. Employees reported improvements in burnout, job satisfaction, and physical and mental health. Twelve control companies did not show the same changes. The study does not prove that every industry can reproduce the result without cost, but it shows that converting efficiency gains into a different arrangement of time can improve life. It is evidence that organizational arrangements matter, not that AI automatically creates happiness.

Instead of asking only whether technology has advanced, we should also ask how much labor saved has become time that people can direct for themselves.

Institutions do not necessarily stand still. They may change continuously while pursuing another objective. Saying that institutions have failed to keep pace with technology is sometimes correct but incomplete. An organization may adopt new technologies very quickly to optimize schedules, track performance, and accelerate decisions, while improving grievance procedures, rest arrangements, and employee participation much more slowly. Institutional innovation is occurring, but the capacity to manage people is growing before the arrangements that protect and include them.

An OECD survey of more than 6,000 companies across six countries in 2025 displayed this double edge. Managers believed algorithmic management improved some decisions and their own job satisfaction, while also worrying about unclear responsibility, difficulty understanding the tools' judgments, and inadequate protection of employee well-being. The survey measured managerial observations and opinions and cannot be treated as a direct account of every employee's experience. It nevertheless shows that efficiency and the human condition do not align automatically.

Digital products present the same problem. If a product defines success primarily by time spent, then making it easier for you to stop using the product and return to your life may not be the most rewarded direction of design. A product's ability to capture you and its benefit to you are different objectives.

A randomized experiment published in the American Economic Review in 2020 found that asking participants to stop using Facebook for four weeks before the 2018 U.S. midterm elections increased their subjective well-being but reduced their knowledge of factual news. This does not prove that the internet makes everyone unhappy. It shows that a single technology can generate gains and losses at the same time, and that use cannot be treated directly as welfare.

The question is therefore not only whether the results are fairly distributed. An earlier question comes first: What are we developing and rewarding? Are we helping people complete lives of their own, or helping them complete system-assigned metrics more efficiently?

Human dissatisfaction and institutional pressure shape each other. They cannot be fully separated, because some forms of dissatisfaction do not arise spontaneously in an isolated individual.

Imagine a school with one hundred places and one thousand applicants, all of whom receive stronger learning tools. Their abilities may improve, but if the number of places and the selection method remain unchanged, competition will not automatically disappear. A person may continue investing time not from greed, but because everyone else is doing so and stopping means losing the opportunity.

There is a simple distinction between wanting more and having to obtain more continuously in order to preserve one's existing life. Likewise, wanting a decent life differs from wanting always to rank above others. Better basic security and public services can allow more people to realize the former simultaneously. If the latter is defined strictly as relative rank, it cannot be satisfied for everyone at once.

An institution can therefore do something important without abolishing ambition: reduce the punishment for falling a little behind, resting for a period, or choosing another way of life. A person should be able to decline the race for first place without losing basic security and dignity.

This also explains why contentment is sometimes genuine freedom and sometimes merely an injunction to endure. Choosing to want less when one has the conditions to leave a competition is different from having no choice and being told to accept one's lot.

Dissatisfaction itself need not be unhappiness. Wanting to continue learning, write another work, or improve a relationship may be part of a full life. The distinction is whether the effort is a willing exploration or a forced race whose costs of stopping are unbearable.

Institutions cannot and should not provide happiness in full, because people want more than care. Institutional explanation needs a boundary. Even major improvements in income, housing, and working hours cannot guarantee intimate relationships, creative passion, or a direction in life. This is not a retreat to “your attitude is the problem.” It is an acknowledgment that happiness concerns not only what one receives, but how one participates in living.

Self-determination theory identifies autonomy, competence, and relatedness as important psychological needs. A major review in the field of work analyzed the relation of these needs to psychological growth and well-being. This provides at least one research-based account of why greater convenience cannot fully replace autonomous action, learning and growth, and relations with other people.

Consider writing. If someone needs only an adequate instruction manual, a tool that drafts it quickly may be immensely valuable. If the person writes in order to form observations and a voice of their own, complete substitution may not constitute help. Some processes are burdens. Others are themselves the life someone wants to live. We cannot treat all of them as though faster replacement were always better.

A good institution should therefore neither promise to arrange happiness for everyone nor prescribe one way of being happy. It should provide conditions under which people can try, fail, rest, and say no to a highly efficient form of life.

Where should emphasis fall between the psychological and institutional explanations? In public questions, I think we should examine institutions and living conditions before asking whether individuals need to adjust their desires. This is not because psychology is unimportant. It is because a generalization about human nature should not allow us to skip matters that can be concretely investigated and changed.

After technology advances, does ordinary people's real income improve? Is saved time left to the person who saved it? Who bears the risk of unemployment or retraining? Do people subject to algorithmic management have opportunities for explanation, appeal, and exit? These questions are more open to verification than an abstract judgment about whether modern people have become greedier.

In individual life, however, we cannot postpone everything until institutions are perfected. Institutions can protect room for choice but cannot decide what is worth pursuing. Individuals can learn not to be ruled by comparison, but should not be asked to endure unreasonable conditions through attitude alone.

My answer is therefore this: technology expands the possible ways in which we can live; institutions determine to whom those possibilities are genuinely open; and each person must still decide how to use them. Without all three, progress may remain only a growth in capacity and numbers.

From the Kantian perspective that persons are ends in themselves, progress cannot be measured only by how much people accomplish for a system. We must also ask whether, as the system grows stronger, people gain better conditions for living in ways they endorse, including both the capacity to continue and the right to stop.

22. Why Does Academia Resist AI?

People may have seen a great deal of news about academic resistance to AI, but the situation is not quite so bleak. Consider a 2026 survey in Research Policy of more than 6,000 researchers at Germany's Max Planck Society and Fraunhofer Society. Of them, 25.9 percent used AI tools daily or more often, while only 22.2 percent did not use them at all.

By comparison, almost no one in Nature's 2023 survey of 1,600 researchers said they used AI frequently in research. The change over two years was substantial. The new survey covered two German organizations focused on science and engineering, so its findings cannot be generalized to every discipline, let alone the world. But one point is clear: using AI and criticizing it can occur at the same time.

Nor does the ivory tower speak with one voice. Fields Medalist Timothy Gowers attended the Leiden workshop in September 2025 that produced the Leiden Declaration. The declaration went online on June 2 and received more than one thousand signatures within twenty-four hours, as well as endorsement from the International Mathematical Union. Gowers himself did not sign. He did not identify a particular principle he opposed. He thought the declaration made several assertions with greater confidence than he possessed. Separately, he argued that the existing hierarchy of journals, appointments, prizes, and similar institutions urgently needed alternatives.

The question, then, is not whether academia resists AI, but where the resistance falls.

One fact is easily underestimated from outside academic life. Scholars may not face a more powerful competitor so much as a growing volume of material they must check. Suppose someone uses AI to generate fifty documents that look like papers and sends them to an expert for criticism. The generator experiences a tremendous gain in efficiency. The recipient must determine, one by one, which are worth reading, which citations do not exist, and which crucial derivations hide flaws. The cost of producing material has fallen; the cost of establishing its reliability has not fallen at the same rate, and the costs land on different people. When scholars say, “Please stop sending me AI-generated material,” they may not mean that outsiders should be excluded. They may mean that unfinished quality control cannot be transferred wholesale to others for free.

Up to this point, I think academia's reasons largely hold. Its real problem lies not in access to knowledge but in the ability to publish it.

People often say knowledge is already open: papers can be downloaded, theorems are public, and arXiv is free. But free downloads of an already filtered set of results do not constitute equality. What is actually allocated is the power to place something inside the set called knowledge.

arXiv itself is the best example. Despite its reputation as an open preprint repository, it has required endorsement since January 2004. Someone submitting to a category for the first time must first obtain endorsement from an author who already has submission privileges. In December 2025, the mathematics section tightened the rule: new submitters without a previous arXiv mathematics paper had to secure a personal endorsement. In January 2026, the requirement expanded across the site, and even an institutional email address was no longer sufficient evidence of minimum research competence. arXiv advised applicants to find an endorser “who knows you personally.”

Its reasons were real. Nonscientific submissions from automatically endorsed users had grown unsustainable, rejection rates were increasing, and moderation and staffing costs had become excessive. This is exactly the earlier problem: verification costs rise, and the system has one available response, narrowing the gate. No one needs a conscious desire to protect status. Once an institution responds according to its incentives, access contracts automatically because contraction is the tool at hand.

But notice arXiv's own statement: endorsement is not peer review. The endorser does not certify scientific correctness. They confirm only that the submission is appropriate to the category and that the author appears familiar with the field's basic conventions. The gate therefore judges not the content but the submitter's identity. The alternative offered to those denied endorsement is that they may still post the paper on their own websites or submit it to peer-reviewed journals. In plain language: you may write, but you cannot enter here.

This gives us a useful measure. Disclosure of tool use, accurate citations, verification material, human responsibility for correctness, and reviewer confidentiality are requirements concerning content. Anyone willing to satisfy them can do so. AI can even help people without academic lineages place their work in independently verifiable form. By contrast, questions such as “who is qualified to announce a result,” “who counts as a member of the field,” and “whether an activity should be permitted to occur” impose requirements of identity. They determine entry into the registry. A threshold may have reasons, but those reasons must be tested. The test is to ask which type of requirement it imposes. Most of the loudest resistance currently belongs to the second type.

Another comparison deserves thought. The same institutions tolerated decades of filler papers produced for evaluation, read and cited by no one, without calling them research misconduct. When AI began helping people produce similar material, the term appeared quickly. The judgment concerned not the quality of the thing, but who produced it.

We cannot expect AI companies to open the gate either. They have removed some barriers to entry and allowed more people to touch frontier problems, but they bypass the registry themselves. Results are announced in press releases; verification is left to the community; names and credit go to the company. They have not changed who counts, who is cited, or who may announce a result. They have constructed another road that only they can travel.

My view is this. Academia has good reason to require that results withstand scrutiny. It has no good reason to require that the same people produce them in the same way as before. We can judge a particular act of resistance by the same standard. If a critic identifies a testable problem and explains that acceptance will follow once it is resolved, that is quality control. If the critic merely repeats that “anything made by AI does not count” regardless of reliability, that is conservatism. The standard applies equally to the other side. If AI companies dismiss every criticism as an old establishment obstructing progress, they too are building a moat, only in different clothing.

23. Why We Live in the Dao Even If We Have Never Read Laozi or Zhuangzi

This is an interesting observation, though it would be more accurate to put it another way: we all use a language jointly shaped by the various schools of ancient thought. Using a language that a tradition helped shape is not the same thing as agreeing with that tradition.

Let us begin with the character dao. Laozi and Zhuangzi did not invent it. Its original meaning was simply a road people traveled. The Shuowen Jiezi glosses it as “the way one travels,” and the Book of Songs, in “Dadong” from the “Lesser Odes,” already says that “the Zhou road is like a whetstone, straight as an arrow,” using a whetstone and an arrow to describe how level and straight the road was. This is the word in its plainest sense.
