From a physical road, it naturally came to mean a route, a method, a standard of conduct, a school of thought, and finally the fundamental order of heaven, earth, and all things. To walk, we need a sense of direction; to act, we need a way of doing things; in arranging a life, we also ask where we ought to go. A path one can actually walk readily becomes an image through which to understand method and direction. The importance of Laozi and Zhuangzi lies not in inventing this everyday word, but in using it to unfold extraordinarily deep reflections.

More importantly, dao was never a term reserved for Daoists. Confucius said, “If I hear the Way in the morning, I can die content in the evening,” and also, “Those whose Ways differ do not make common plans.” The Doctrine of the Mean opens by saying, “Following our nature is called the Way.” Even Han Fei of the Legalist school wrote an entire essay, “Explaining Laozi,” to interpret the Laozi. A word used by all the schools, over whose interpretation they competed, could never be anyone’s private property.

Of the several words at issue, zhidao, “to know,” is most worth taking apart. The “Record of Learning” in the Book of Rites says: “Jade that is not carved does not become a vessel; a person who does not study does not know the Way.” Here, “know” and “Way” are still two separate words: through study, one comes to understand the principles one ought to follow. The fact that this sentence comes from a Confucian classic on education is enough to show that “knowing the Way” does not mean becoming a Daoist believer. In modern Chinese, zhidao later solidified into a two-syllable word whose function has changed. When we say, “I know where the keys are,” we are not claiming to have grasped the foundation of the cosmos. When we say, “I know there is a meeting tomorrow,” we do not first have to answer what the Dao is. A word can preserve traces of its history while acquiring a changed everyday function. We cannot force the meaning of two characters used together in ancient times, unchanged, into every use of the modern word.

Daolu, “road,” belongs to the same category. Dao and lu were near-synonyms combined into a word, one of the most common patterns of word formation in Chinese. Like words such as “morality,” “method,” and “route,” it bears no additional philosophical weight merely because it contains dao.

As for daoli, “reason” or “principle,” Han Fei’s “Explaining Laozi” does indeed say that “those who conduct their affairs according to the patterns of the Way can accomplish anything.” But one ancient usage is not enough to prove that the modern word derives entirely from a single thinker, much less that whenever we “talk reason,” the reason in question is the Way of Laozi and Zhuangzi.

Still, if we set etymology aside, the question is philosophically suggestive. We can distinguish three kinds of understanding: knowing that something is the case, understanding why it is so, and knowing how to respond to it. The three are related, but they are not the same. Someone may be able to recite a great many principles about intimate relationships and still not know how to listen until the other person has finished speaking. Someone else may be unable to offer a complete theory, yet know in an actual relationship when to explain and when first to stop defending himself. Here the metaphor of “finding a path” becomes powerful. Understanding does not merely mean adding one more correct sentence to the mind. It may also mean that, in a real situation, we are no longer quite so lost.

If we think carefully, there is even a certain irony here, because Laozi and Zhuangzi might not approve of the confidence with which we “talk reason” every day. The opening of the Laozi, “The Way that can be spoken is not the constant Way,” has been interpreted in many ways. On one common reading, it reminds us that a Way that can be put into words should not simply be treated as the unchanging Way. The “Discussion on Making All Things Equal” in the Zhuangzi says, “The Way is obscured by small accomplishments, and words are obscured by flowery display,” followed immediately by, “Thus there are the rights and wrongs of the Confucians and Mohists: each affirms what the other denies and denies what the other affirms.” Partial achievements in understanding and beautiful language can themselves conceal a wider understanding. What the passage questions is not only whether other people are wrong, but also our own tendency to take the correctness of our position for granted.

This reminder is quite useful in everyday disputes. When someone says, “I am only trying to reason with you,” is he offering reasons that both sides can examine, or announcing that you ought to accept his standards? When someone says, “This is the right path,” has he turned a method that works for him into a way of life that everyone must accept? Acknowledging limits does not mean abandoning judgment, nor does it mean that every opinion is equally right. It asks only that, even when we stand by a judgment, we explain our reasons and remain open to revision when confronted with counterexamples.

So I am happy to preserve the charm of the original remark, while keeping only this conclusion: we need not have read Laozi and Zhuangzi before we can encounter, within our own language, the questions they discussed.

In the end, dao remains alive today, and an ordinary person can casually use it three times, precisely because nobody ever registered it as private property. Genuine inheritance does not lie in having the same word on our lips, but in whether we seriously face the questions that word raises. Saying “I know” every day does not mean that we know the Way. Asking once more, “Do I really understand?” is where meeting them anew begins.

24. Why Creating Panic Is a Shortcut to Power

I do often say this, and many people before me have said it as well. But before discussing the issue broadly, I want to narrow the claim a little, so that it is not read as saying that every security warning is a fraud.

Pointing out a risk is not the same as creating panic. At the same time, the reality of a risk does not make every demand for power made in response to it reasonable. Both statements must be true. The real issue is a conversion: people’s legitimate need for safety can be converted into power that others exercise without sufficient constraint.

The first thing panic changes is not our intelligence, but the order in which we rank concerns. Ordinarily we care at the same time about income, freedom, fairness, dignity, and the future. Once we believe that we are about to lose our jobs, suffer violence, or even see the entire order of life collapse, all other demands are temporarily pushed aside.

A 2004 analysis of nationwide surveys conducted in the United States after September 11 found that respondents who felt more threatened were more willing to exchange some civil liberties for security. This exchange was also affected by how much they trusted the government.

Imagine that, under ordinary circumstances, someone demanded access to all our communications. We would certainly ask why, how broad the access would be, and who would oversee it. But if we were first made to believe that refusing would bring an imminent disaster, we might well answer: deal with the danger first; we can add the procedures later.

We make this choice not because we are stupid, but because genuine emergencies really do require swift action. The problem arises when the urgency is exaggerated and the extra power has no limits. To avoid one still-uncertain loss, we first accept another that occurs immediately. That is the shortcut. The person seeking authorization no longer has to prove fully that “my governance is better.” He only has to persuade people that “without me, the consequences will be worse.”

The statement that “fear shuts down reason” is too simple. When anxious, people may actually search more actively for information, read the news, and ask others. But searching for more information does not guarantee a more comprehensive judgment. In an earlier experiment about immigration, participants whose anxiety had been induced browsed a simulated news website. They were more likely to read, remember, and agree with threatening material. As a finding from a specific experiment, this cannot be generalized to everyone, but it points to a mechanism: people did not fail to look for information. They focused mainly on, and remembered, the part that frightened them more.

Creating panic therefore aims not only to make us believe a particular claim, but also to make a particular danger occupy the center of our judgment about the world. Someone who is repeatedly told that he is about to be eliminated will devote tremendous energy to learning how not to be eliminated. He will ask less and less whether the competition itself is reasonable, whether another way of life is possible, and whether the person claiming to know the way out also benefits from his anxiety. Whoever decides what must be feared first influences the allocation of resources, attention, and action.

The most important sleight of hand occurs in the middle of the reasoning. Several steps lie between “a danger exists” and “you should obey me,” and each must be established separately. The existence of a danger does not show that a particular solution will work. The effectiveness of a solution does not show that one person must have a monopoly on implementing it. The need for coordinated action does not show that oversight can be abolished. Panic-driven rhetoric compresses these steps into the sentence, “The situation is this serious, and you are still asking questions?” A workplace example makes this clearer. A manager announces that the company faces an existential crisis and asks everyone temporarily to put in more effort. That is entirely open to discussion. But when employees ask for business data, managerial accountability, and a condition for ending the emergency measures, they are told, “If you still care about such things now, you do not care whether the rest of us survive.” The nature of the discussion has changed. The employees began by asking about a plan; they ended up being asked to prove their loyalty. One school of thought in security studies sees the matter in exactly this way: defining something as a security issue is itself a political act. It determines how the issue enters decision-making, rather than merely adding one more item to an objective list of dangers.

More dangerous than temporary authorization is a crisis that is never allowed to end. Imagine an explanation that is almost impossible to test: if nothing happens, it is because I protected you well; if something happens, it is because you did not give me enough power; if someone proposes an alternative, it is because he underestimates the danger. Each explanation might be true in some circumstances. But if every possible outcome leads to the same conclusion, that the same person should receive still more power, then the judgment no longer contains any condition under which it can be tested and revised.

In terms of incentives, this creates a real conflict of interest. The public needs the danger to diminish, while the person who controls its interpretation may need the public to continue feeling endangered. This does not mean that everyone who issues a warning hopes for disaster, nor does it require a secret conspiracy. A practical question can help us tell whether protection has turned into control: is the arrangement gradually increasing people’s ability to cope with the risk, or continually proving that they can rely only on a particular protector?

Still, panic is not a button that works whenever it is pressed. More accurately, panic is a key that works only in certain locks, and the conditions of the lock can be listed: a single source of information, no viable alternative, the ability to circumvent oversight, and a direction of blame that points outward rather than toward the people in power. If people believe that the authorities created the crisis, magnifying the danger may simply remind them that those authorities ought to be held accountable.

For the same reason, it is not enough to tell ordinary people, “Do not be afraid.” Fear of unemployment, war, violence, or technological risk may be entirely justified. What helps is preserving independent sources of information, viable options for responding, and effective oversight, so that people are not forced into a position where they can trust only one person.

As for distinguishing a responsible warning from a bid for power through fear, I prefer to ask several verifiable questions rather than guess at the speaker’s inner motives. What is the basis for the risk? Has the speaker explained the evidence, the unknowns, and the conditions of the judgment, and does he permit independent verification? Or has he substituted a worst-case scenario for a probability, while treating questions as ignorance or malice? Why this measure? Has he explained how it reduces the risk, what it costs, and whether alternatives have been compared? Or has he leapt directly from “the risk is serious” to “you must accept my plan”? Who constrains the people implementing it? Do they accept scrutiny, correction, and a review of conflicts of interest, or do they demand exemption because they possess secrets and the situation is exceptional? And there is one question most easily overlooked: under what conditions does the arrangement end? Is there a review mechanism and a means of withdrawing the additional power, or does the danger have no standard by which it can ever be declared over, so that a temporary measure automatically becomes permanent?

These questions are not a universal test for whether someone is lying. Some risks genuinely are difficult to quantify, and some information genuinely should not be public. But suitable independent review can still be arranged. The unknown can be a reason for caution; it cannot automatically become a reason for unlimited authority.

I therefore do not believe that every security warning is a fraud. But pushing the public into a situation with no time, no alternative, and no one to rely on but a particular protector can sharply reduce the burden of proof required to obtain obedience. Resisting such power does not require us first to prove that the world contains no danger. We can acknowledge the danger and act, while still insisting on one thing: identifying a problem does not automatically qualify you to decide everything for everyone.

What is truly worth preserving is not the pose of fearlessness, but our capacity, even while afraid, to judge, supervise, and withdraw authorization. This standard applies equally to every essay I write.

25. Why Philosophy Is the Mother of All Sciences

I may have put this a little too briefly before, so let me expand. First, the source of the phrase. In a 1932 letter to Bruno Winawer, Einstein wrote that philosophy was like a mother who had given birth to and endowed all the other sciences. He immediately added that the mother should not be despised because she was now naked and poor, but that one should hope her Don Quixote-like ideal would live on in her children so that they would not sink into vulgarity. The second half is really the point. He was not ranking disciplines. He was warning science not to turn itself into a craft concerned only with practical results.

The metaphor of the mother is first of all a claim about intellectual history. Newton’s 1687 book was titled Mathematical Principles of Natural Philosophy. Research that we now classify as physics was still conducted under the name of natural philosophy. Science and philosophy were not separated into today’s independent disciplines from the outset. Before methods and divisions of labor had matured, people were already asking what nature was, how change occurred, and what kind of reason was sufficient for us to believe an explanation. Some of those questions later grew into specialized sciences.

But the metaphor should not be pushed too far. A relationship of descent and the evidentiary force of a claim today are two different things. A conclusion in physics does not escape testing because a philosopher approves of it, nor should science forever obey a philosophical system simply because philosophy participated in its formation.

His most persuasive example is how he himself handled “time.” Suppose you want to measure the speed of a train. You naturally need distance and time. But before you begin calculating, a question is hidden in the setup: when do two clocks in different places count as displaying the same time? It is easy to think there is nothing to ask. Just set them alike. But how do we do that? Transmitting a signal takes time, so how do we determine that the clocks in the two places have been synchronized?

The first section of Einstein’s 1905 paper on relativity was titled “Definition of Simultaneity.” He first discussed how clocks in different places could be synchronized using light signals and made the rule explicit. He then derived the famous conclusion: two distant events judged simultaneous in one inertial frame of reference need not remain simultaneous in another frame moving relative to it. This does not mean that time bends to anyone’s wishes, nor is it merely a matter of seeing distant things later.

What really deserves our attention is what he did. Instead of merely calculating the numbers in existing formulas more accurately, he went back and examined the words in the formulas that seemed to need no explanation at all. “What time is it?” is one question. “What exactly do we mean when we say that two places have the same time?” is another. The latter kind of question is clearly philosophical, yet it can enter directly into the construction of a physical theory. Of course, asking the question is not enough. Conceptual analysis must be joined to physical principles, mathematical argument, and empirical testing.

He took this so seriously because specialized knowledge can itself become habitual. In a 1916 essay commemorating Ernst Mach, he offered a sharp observation: because a concept has long been useful, it can acquire excessive authority. People forget how it was formed and treat it as an unalterable necessity. Analyzing the conditions under which the concept holds, or does not hold, can break that authority. In a 1944 letter to Robert Thornton, he said that the educational value of the history and philosophy of science lay in helping researchers free themselves from the prejudices of their own time and develop independent judgment.

What he admired, then, was not doubting everything and therefore doing nothing. It was the ability both to use a method skillfully and to recognize when one should stop using it in the old way. Knowing many conclusions does not mean knowing where their boundaries lie.

Yet he did not advocate choosing a philosophy first and then requiring science to accommodate it. In “Physics and Reality” from 1936, he put it directly: when the foundations of physics themselves become problematic, the physicist cannot hand critical reflection entirely over to the philosopher, because the physicist knows best where the shoe pinches. In 1949 he supplied the constraint in the other direction, in a sentence with two inseparable halves: epistemology without contact with science becomes an empty scheme, while science without epistemology is primitive and muddled. He added a rather impolite remark: to a systematic epistemologist, the scientist must appear an unscrupulous opportunist, a realist when realism is needed, an idealist when idealism is needed, and a positivist when positivism is needed.

Taken together, these observations show that his “mother” was not a disciplinary hierarchy. In his own practice, philosophy was not a profession to which one belonged, but something one personally had to do when the tools at hand stopped working. It could neither be outsourced to a philosophy department nor fenced off by any discipline as its exclusive territory. The point of his 1936 remark was precisely that no one else could do it for you, because the shoe pinching was your own.

This returns us to the second half of the 1932 letter. What concerned him was never that science was insufficiently precise. It was that, after achieving results, science might stop asking what it was doing. Have we mistaken a measurable result for the whole value associated with it? Being able to do more does not automatically tell us how much we understand or how we ought to use our abilities.

Of course, philosophy’s importance neither needs nor can be proved by Einstein’s praise. To do so would be to replace our own necessary judgment with the authority of a famous person yet again.

26. Why “Alienation” Is Everywhere in the Age of AI

This friend truly understands the issue. He went straight to the key term. Let us first narrow it down; otherwise it will explain everything and therefore explain nothing. Alienation does not mean simply that “work is exhausting” or that “life feels meaningless.” Marx described a specific relationship: a person’s own activity and its products become forces that he cannot control and to which he must instead submit.

In the Economic and Philosophic Manuscripts of 1844, Marx discussed the alienation of labor in four respects. The worker is separated from the product: it becomes someone else’s property and in turn dominates him. He is separated from the labor process: he does not decide what to do or how to do it. He is separated from his “species-being”: the free and conscious creative capacity proper to human beings is compressed into a means of survival. And he is separated from other people: relations among people are reshaped by these relations of production.

“Species-being” sounds abstract, but it simply means that human beings can do more than complete tasks. They can ask why something should be done and how to do it, and can develop their abilities through cooperation with others. Marx believed that when this capacity has no room at all to unfold in work, what is lost is more than wages.

There is a distinction easily missed, and once missed it renders the whole concept useless: creating something external to oneself is not in itself alienation. When we finish an essay, it becomes a work that others can read and criticize. In philosophy, this is called objectification, and it can be a way of realizing ourselves. Alienation arises only when the product turns back and dominates us, and when the activity becomes detached from our own purposes. Alienation does not ask whether “the world now contains many more things.” It asks whether, in making those things, people can still recognize the activity as their own.

Why, then, does alienation not necessarily diminish when life improves? Because receiving more compensation and gaining more power to decide are not the same improvement.

Imagine that the same technology reduces an eight-hour job to four hours. An organization can now let its employees work four fewer hours. It can maintain the eight-hour day while doubling output and share some of the gains with them. Or it can maintain the eight-hour day and simply double the workload. All three arrangements use the same technology, yet they produce three different lives for the people involved. Technology creates a new capacity; it does not itself determine to whom that capacity belongs.

Marx made the distinction quite clearly in the chapter on machinery in Volume I of Capital. Machinery can reduce labor, but under particular relations of production it can also be used to lengthen the working day and increase the intensity of labor. His criticism was never that machines made people degenerate. It concerned how machines were used and the relations of domination behind that use.

If Marx saw AI today, he would say the same thing about it. He would not criticize AI for making people degenerate. He would criticize the way AI is used and the relations of domination behind it.

Of course, we cannot reverse the argument and say that improvements in wages, housing, and rest are meaningless. They change a person’s situation in real and important ways. But “I live more comfortably as a result” does not automatically mean “I have more power to decide how I live.” The two also affect one another. When a person has no tolerable option of leaving, his “voluntary negotiation” is not the same as that of someone with somewhere else to go.

More importantly, this arrangement can continue without anyone deliberately designing it from behind the scenes. In his discussion of capital accumulation in Volume I of Capital, Marx described competition as a force that also constrains the owners of capital: an enterprise must keep accumulating and expanding production or risk losing its position. This is a structural explanation, not an accusation that particular bosses are greedy.

Consider a hypothetical example. A company wants to reduce the intensity of work, but fears that higher costs will lose it orders. Employees want to work less overtime, but fear that the first person who stops responding will lose a promotion. Each party therefore thinks, “I do not want this either, but I have no choice.” An arrangement can be sustained by everyone’s actions even when no one sincerely likes it.

That does not mean everyone has the same power and responsibility. The person who can decide contracts, budgets, and workloads has a far wider range of choices than someone who can only accept or leave. Competitive pressure may explain conduct, but it does not automatically absolve decision-makers of responsibility.

In his discussion of the “fetishism of commodities,” Marx added another layer: social relations among people appear as relations among things, as though prices and capital had grown legs of their own. This is related to alienation, but it is not the same concept. We can draw a limited analogy with claims we hear today: “No one decided this; the market requires it,” or “It is not that I refuse; the AI calculated it.” These statements may point to real constraints, but the questions they leave behind do not disappear. Who set the rules? Who can change them? Who bears responsibility when they go wrong? Why do the costs always fall on the same group of people? “No single person decides” is entirely different from “this is a natural law that cannot be discussed.”

What is new about AI today is that work can not only be managed, but continuously measured, predicted, and optimized. According to a survey published by the OECD in 2025, algorithmic management tools were already quite common in most of the countries surveyed. Managers believed they could improve decision-making, while also raising concerns about unclear responsibility, the difficulty of understanding the tools’ logic, and inadequate protections for employee well-being. This was a survey of managers, not a measure of workers’ alienation, and algorithmic management is not the same thing as generative AI.

Such tools cannot in themselves be classified as alienating. One scheduling system may reduce confusion and make people’s rest more predictable. Another may convert every minute into a performance demand and leave people without even an opportunity to explain their actual circumstances. The difference is whether the technology supports human judgment or makes it increasingly difficult for people to participate in, question, and change the rules that decide their lives.

The same structure exists in the writing of key opinion leaders. Someone begins writing to clarify a problem, then discovers that some kinds of content attract more traffic and adjusts his expression. There is nothing necessarily wrong with this; understanding one’s readers is part of writing. But if the process continues until a lack of traffic means that a question is not worth writing about, and an inability to monetize means that the effort has no value, then eventually even the question of what the writer wants to ask must await the platform’s approval. Feedback that originally helped the work reach readers has become the standard that determines what kind of person the author should be.

The alienation we see among many leading online influencers is not, in a strict sense, a mechanism Marx himself studied. There was no internet in his time, let alone key opinion leaders. It is an extension of his question into a contemporary case. The point is not that all metrics are bad, but that a metric can change from useful information into a tool that may not be questioned.

Another point is easily overlooked: people who appear from the outside to lack nothing can feel this deeply as well. They may have good incomes, stable work, and abundant recognition, yet find it increasingly hard to answer why they should continue down the same road. In The Holy Family, Marx and Engels even discussed how property owners, too, exist in a state of self-alienation, though they derive power from the relationship and therefore feel at ease within it. In their theory, a person’s place in a social relationship and whether he subjectively suffers can be discussed separately, though the power and consequences attached to different positions are by no means equal.

But we must put up a gate here, or the entire theory becomes impossible to refute. We cannot broadly and indiscriminately diagnose someone’s suffering as alienation. Nor can we respond to a person who is satisfied with life by declaring that he “simply has not awakened.” If suffering proves the theory and satisfaction proves it too, then it is no longer an explanation. It is a posture that remains correct whatever happens. To discuss an actual person, we must listen seriously to his experience and examine how much room he really has to choose, rather than first deciding for him what life he should truly want.

Nor should we describe alienation as an unalterable fate. In his “Comments on James Mill’s Elements of Political Economy,” Marx imagined a different relationship: people could see the realization of their abilities in their own works, and could gain confirmation from the fact that those works met the needs of others. Here, non-alienation does not mean living alone and needing no one. It means that one’s relationship with one’s activity, its products, and other people has changed as a whole. In the final chapters of Volume III of Capital, he discussed producers jointly bringing necessary productive activity under their control, placed great weight on the development of human capacities in free time, and treated the shortening of the working day as a basic condition. This was a direction he proposed, not a claim that the conditions already exist whenever a system adopts the corresponding name.

Marx’s own understanding of “self-realization” can likewise be debated. A person may willingly do clearly bounded, repetitive work and devote his main passion to family or writing. We cannot conclude that he has failed to live well simply because his job is “not creative enough.” Any theory of alienation must explain what conception of human nature and what value judgments it rests upon. It cannot prescribe one ideal life as the only answer for everyone.

The term continues to be used in the age of AI not because the world has made no progress, but because “growth in society’s overall capacities” and “whether concrete persons control their own activity” have never been the same thing.

If we want the word “alienation” to do real work, we should treat it as a set of answerable questions rather than a vague feeling: What have I helped to create? Who decides what it is used for? Can I influence the process? And am I able to understand myself only according to what it demands of me?

That last question is precisely the one Marx himself discussed least. His analysis was firmly rooted in relations of production: who owns and who controls the labor process. A more common form today begins when people voluntarily install an external standard inside themselves. They then use it to manage and assess themselves, translating what originally belonged to a structure entirely into the question, “Am I simply not working hard enough?” Marx gives us a starting point for this layer, but not the whole answer.

27. In the Age of AI, Can More Than a Decade of Education Be Compressed into Four Years?

I have indeed been saying this lately. But let me begin by explaining what I am not proposing, or people will refute it as if it were three other claims.

I am not saying that students should “learn faster.” Playing the same curriculum at four times the speed is not compression; it is cramming. Nor am I saying that everyone should earn a doctorate. Expanding opportunities for research and requiring everyone to obtain a research degree are two different things. Still less am I saying that, once we have AI, learning itself no longer takes time.

I mean something else: in the decade or more from high school through a doctorate, the time genuinely spent developing abilities is far shorter than the entire period. Much of the time is wasted on waiting, repetition, and satisfying procedural requirements. These happen to be exactly the things AI is best at cutting away.

Let us begin with “waiting.”

A student understands most of the material but gets stuck on one crucial concept. Without timely feedback, he may keep solving problems and repeating the same mistake. Another student mastered the concept long ago but must practice it again with the whole class. These two students do not need the same kind of acceleration at all. The first needs someone to identify his misunderstanding. The second needs permission to move on.

A traditional classroom cannot provide both at once, because it can advance at only one speed. In practice, the pace of a class is the slowest pace among all its students, multiplied by an administrative calendar.

In a randomized controlled trial published by Harvard researchers in 2025, 194 undergraduates took two introductory physics lessons. The group using an AI tutor created with deliberate instructional design outperformed the active-learning classroom group on post-lesson tests, while the median study time was 49 minutes, compared with about 60 minutes in the classroom. These were short-term results from only two topics, not an entire course, much less an experiment in doctoral education. We cannot extrapolate from them proportionally to a whole educational system. But they do at least reopen a question: does material that has traditionally taken a semester truly need a semester?

Next comes “repetition.”

High school, undergraduate education, a master’s program, and a doctorate are treated as four repositories of knowledge that must be emptied in sequence. From the standpoint of learning itself, however, the real constraints are prerequisite relationships, not status labels. If someone already understands and can use calculus and linear algebra, must he wait until he possesses the identity of an “undergraduate” before studying more advanced statistics? Conversely, if someone has enrolled in a master’s program but has gaps in the foundations, changing the label will not fill them automatically.

The prerequisite chain is in fact much shorter than the calendar. The number of genuinely necessary links between arithmetic and the ability to read a frontier paper in a field is limited. We stretch that chain over more than a decade largely because each level requires regrouping students, repeating admissions, and dividing study into semesters all over again. Nor must the boundaries between levels remain separate. MIT allows qualified undergraduates to pursue a master’s degree concurrently and, with approval, to use the same master’s thesis to satisfy their undergraduate thesis requirement. Progressing according to mastery rather than time spent in class is not an idea that arose only with AI. Western Governors University has long used a competency-based model, though chiefly for adult learners. AI makes such organization easier to scale; it did not make the logic possible for the first time.

At this point, we must pause and clarify what time cannot be compressed, or the proposal will turn into the claim that learning takes no time.

The hardest case is the doctorate. A doctorate certifies more than having “finished even the most difficult textbooks.” It also certifies whether someone can take responsibility for his own research judgments. Suppose AI helps formulate a question, write code, analyze data, and finally produce a correct new result. That result may have genuine scholarly value and should not be dismissed merely because a tool was used. But to judge whether the person has reached the doctoral level, we still need to ask: Does he understand the crucial assumptions of the research? Does he know under what conditions its conclusion would fail? When new evidence appears, can he explain why the original judgment should be revised? “Independence” should not mean turning off every tool and working with one’s bare hands. It should mean seeing clearly what the person actually understands, contributes, and can take responsibility for within human-AI collaboration.

An AI proving a mathematical theorem is good. Better still is the person using the AI understanding the proof and being able to advance the direction and methodology of mathematical research.

The second limit is time required by the object of study itself. If we want to know the actual consequences of a change three years later, AI can help design a study and process existing data, but it cannot turn three years of observations that have not yet occurred into evidence already obtained. A simulation can generate a prediction; prediction and observation remain two different things. For example, we might infer that gravitational waves are not themselves bent by gravity, but we must at least wait until an experiment can be conducted before confirming or falsifying that inference.

The third limit is the measurement trap most easily overlooked. An experiment published in the Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences studied mathematics instruction for nearly a thousand students at a Turkish secondary school. Students using ordinary GPT-style assistance performed about 48 percent better than the control group in practice. Once the assistance was removed, however, they scored about 17 percent lower than the control group on an independent examination. With a specially designed tutor that did not provide answers directly, gains during practice were even larger and the negative effect after removal largely disappeared. But independent test scores still were not significantly higher than those of the control group.

The same AI, then, can accelerate the formation of ability or help someone bypass that formation more quickly. Any proposal for compression must therefore measure more than how many courses were completed or papers submitted. It must examine long-term retention, transfer to unfamiliar problems, and whether the person can explain and revise his own judgments. We must measure separately what the person himself has mastered and what the human-AI system accomplishes jointly. The latter should not be passed off as the former.

Once we include these three constraints, “four years” is obviously not an established conclusion. No existing study proves that ordinary students can go from high school through a doctorate in four years.

But there is an asymmetry here that I find more noteworthy than the evidence itself.

The side that wants to move faster is always the one asked to provide proof. No one asks the current system to prove why education must take twenty-three years. Those numbers were never derived from the laws of learning. A bachelor’s degree takes three years in Britain and four in the United States. The same degree differs by a year. The duration of primary and secondary education also varies between countries. If twelve years, four years, two years, and five years truly followed from cognitive development, they should not differ so widely. They look more like the combined result of historical inheritance, administrative cycles, and admissions schedules, subsequently treated as natural constants.

The right question is therefore not, “On what grounds do you say four years are enough?” It is, “What exactly is being done in every one of these years? Explain them year by year.” An arrangement that has never had to provide evidence should not enjoy the authority to demand evidence from everyone else.

When I say four years, I mean less an exact number than a parameter designed to force a question. The real target is certification by years served. A degree should depend on whether a standard has been met, not on whether a pilot program has reached its fourth year and must now award everyone a credential. Three changes are needed. First, curricula should follow relationships of knowledge dependence, with permission to skip what has already been mastered. Second, ability should be assessed in layers: foundational understanding, professional application, and research capacity should be evaluated separately. Producing an excellent research paper does not automatically prove that every foundation is sound, and top marks in foundational courses do not automatically prove research ability. Third, completion time should vary from person to person. It may be shorter than the traditional course of study and, at some stages, longer. Moving from fixed duration to competency should not simply replace one uniform slow speed with one uniform fast speed.

Finally, let me explain why I care about this.

The years released by compression are not an abstract efficiency gain. They are a person’s twenties. Under the current path, someone who begins school at six finishes a doctorate at around twenty-nine. The additional seven or eight years happen to be years when a person has great physical strength and energy, is willing to take risks, and can afford to lose. If the system consumes them, it should justify each year.

Nor should compression merely send people earlier into the next competition for qualifications. If after four years someone can choose continued research, employment, creative work, a change of direction, or simply some rest, then the waiting eliminated has become room for choice. If instead it becomes, “You are eighteen or nineteen and still do not have a doctorate, so you have not worked hard enough,” we have only installed a harsher clock. AI determines neither outcome. Institutions do.

My proposal, then, is this: we should not deny a person’s actual abilities because he has not served the required number of years. Nor should we count an ability as formed merely because a tool has produced an impressive result. Whether four years are enough can be tested and revised. The supposed necessity of spending more than a decade has never been explained at all.

28. Why We Are Always Waiting for a Great Person

This is indeed a problem everyone has to some degree. The key is that it happens without our noticing. If we were merely deceived, things would be much simpler: stop believing. What is difficult to explain is that no one has deceived us. We slip into it on our own.

Let us begin with what we actually want. Most of the time, we do not merely hope for someone who has accomplished something important. Such people exist, and acknowledging them is perfectly ordinary. What we want is someone who can guarantee the direction: he has seen the situation clearly and knows where to go, so even if I am now completely lost, there is ultimately some way through the world.

But these two ideas are far apart. The first says, “Someone can help.” The second says, “Someone must be able to take care of everything.” We usually slide from the first to the second when we ourselves cannot say what should be done.

Suppose the problem before us has already exceeded our capacity to handle it. An industry is changing, and the relationship between effort and results has become increasingly hard to understand. At such a moment, “The problem is complicated; we will have to experiment slowly” feels very different from “I have seen it clearly; follow me.” The second statement may not offer a better plan, but it offers something more complete: even if we do not know what to do, at least someone knows.

One related line of leadership research is usually called the “romance of leadership”: the image of a leader is shaped not only by the decisions he makes, but also by followers’ interpretations and expectations. Some studies have also reported an association between self-uncertainty and support for forceful leaders. These are correlations found in particular samples, not a law that “whenever people feel weak, they worship authority.” In fact, recent replication studies have failed to reproduce some of the classic effects. We can therefore call this a recurring tendency, not an inevitable fate.

Now let us return to the fact that it happens without our noticing. Why are we so often unaware of the process?

Because at the moment we surrender judgment, the experience does not feel like loss. It feels like relief. Judgment has costs: gathering information, bearing uncertainty, and taking responsibility for a wrong choice. When we give these burdens to someone we trust, the immediate experience is one of ease. It is hard to notice what we have relinquished when that very thing has always made us tired.

This is the fundamental difference from deception. Someone who has been deceived later feels that he lost out. Someone who has surrendered judgment may for a long time feel that he gained.

This is why we are inclined to surrender our agency.

Next comes the deliberate construction of the great person.

Once we have a guarantor, he cannot be allowed to fail. His failure would falsify not only him, but the judgments on which we have based years of our lives. Protecting his image therefore also protects our explanation of our own life.

One point needs to be clear: this construction usually does not take the form of knowingly lying about what we know is false. It takes the form of applying two standards to evidence. Material that praises him is accepted at a glance. Material that criticizes him is examined word by word, with demands for sources and suspicions about motives. In one classic small-sample experiment, university students read studies supporting and challenging their prior positions. They were more likely to consider the material favoring their own side persuasive, even as they believed that they were carefully examining the evidence.

A cycle emerges. Success proves that he is great; failure proves that circumstances were too complicated. Clarity proves his depth; obscurity proves that others have not yet understood. When every possible result yields the same conclusion, what is being protected is no longer the person’s actual contribution, but an expectation that cannot be allowed to fail.

There is also a simpler form of causal attribution. A company produces an important product. The founder’s judgment may indeed have been crucial, but the achievement also depended on engineers, suppliers, existing technologies, capital, timing, and many corrections omitted from the story. Compress the entire system into “the CEO is a genius,” and the narrative becomes clean. Success has a name, and the future seems guaranteed. Psychology’s halo effect describes a neighboring mechanism: our overall favorable impression of a person affects our judgments of his particular traits, often without our awareness. “He did something I admire” quietly becomes “He must also possess the other virtues I hope exist.”

We therefore need to separate four claims. One person made an important contribution. Nothing could have happened without him. He exercised excellent judgment in this matter. He has superior judgment about everything. The second and fourth require far more evidence than the first and third.

Organizational studies also offer a harder version of the problem, one not entirely psychological.

Imagine an institution, though perhaps no imagination is necessary because we often inhabit such institutions, where ordinary feedback channels do not work. Problems pass upward through several levels without being resolved. Only the personal intervention of a particular leader produces a result. Under these conditions, employees are not irrational to hope for a just and capable leader. Their experience tells them that procedures may not help, but a particular person might.

The trouble begins in the next step. If only one person may speak, obtain information, and make decisions, others have no opportunity to demonstrate that they too can accomplish things. The claim that “only he can get anything done” is then used to justify keeping resources and power concentrated in his hands. A person may appear irreplaceable because of genuine ability, or because no one else was ever given the conditions under which they could become an alternative.

Yet we cannot explain the matter entirely through evasion and interest, or we will miss the part most worth preserving.

Suppose we have long watched people sacrifice what they once valued in order to succeed. We may then hope to see someone who is capable without needing to belittle others to prove himself, someone who accomplishes things without turning everyone around him into a means. What we need may not be a master who makes decisions for us. We may need only a concrete example showing that such a way of living need not remain confined to books.

That hope deserves to be cherished. A model can make an abstract possibility visible.

The problem begins when all our hope is placed in that person. Discovering his limitations may then make us feel not only that he is imperfect, but that the values we cherish have lost their basis. This does not follow logically. One person’s failure to realize a value completely does not make the value meaningless. A person worth learning from may commit a serious wrong, but that does not force us to choose between a “perfect great person” and “everyone is a hypocrite.”

Nor should the opposite of expecting greatness be trusting no one. That too is a way of avoiding concrete judgment, only more thorough. If we trust no one, we never have to distinguish among them.

We cannot independently verify all knowledge or solve every problem alone. Relying on experts, trusting teachers, and accepting help can all be choices made after judgment. Delegating some work is not the same as surrendering the entire right to decide how one should live.

The boundary can be stated clearly. Someone who helps me see what I could not see before does not thereby acquire the right to prescribe the ends of my life. The fact that he helped me does not mean I can never refuse or correct him, or take a road different from his. This boundary applies equally to thinkers, teachers, entrepreneurs, and to an AI writing for someone else. Help proves that help occurred. It does not prove that all the helper’s other judgments should be accepted automatically.

Returning to what happens without our noticing: no one exactly tricks us out of our judgment. We loosen our own grip because letting go feels good. The real danger is not any particular great person, but that feeling of comfort.

29. Why We Want an Extraordinary Life but Keep Repeating Other People

This is a sharp observation, and the question itself seems tinged with pessimism. My view, however, is that the contradiction it identifies is only apparent. Once we take it apart, what we find is more troublesome than the original contradiction. It arises only when an “extraordinary life” is treated as an autonomous choice. In everyday usage, the word almost always operates as a comparative term.

To be extraordinary usually means to be more worth telling a story about than the average person. The term does not describe what a life is like from within, but where it stands in other people’s eyes. Once someone makes “living an extraordinary life” his goal, he has already accepted a shared standard.

And if people share a standard, they must travel broadly similar roads. Otherwise comparison is impossible. Someone can run as far as he likes on a track where he is the only runner, but he will never appear on a ranking. Repeating what others have done is therefore a precondition of the very goal of being extraordinary.

This explains a common situation. Someone refuses an “ordinary life,” then lists the places he will travel, the positions he will obtain, the things he will own, and the degree of fame he will achieve. Each aim may be worthwhile on its own. But from beginning to end, the reason for judging them worthwhile may be, “If I achieve these things, other people will think I have lived well.” What the person wants is certified uniqueness: I want to be different, preferably in a direction everyone recognizes and approves.

Besides comparability, imitation offers something else: the feeling that one is unlikely to choose wrongly.

Choosing a new direction for oneself requires facing an uncomfortable fact: we do not yet know where it leads. Someone who has already succeeded appears to offer ready-made evidence. He lived this way and obtained wealth, respect, or influence, so following him is at least not groundless. Anthropological research on prestige and cultural learning likewise shows that people do use others’ success and social esteem to choose whom to learn from. This is neither inherently irrational nor ineffective.

Where, then, is the problem? In the easy extra step. We begin by learning “how to do something” from a person, then accept from him “what is worth doing” as well.

We admire a writer and learn how he observes people and revises sentences. This develops our abilities. But if we go on to believe that our own writing counts only if we possess his experiences, reputation, and manner of life, borrowing methods has become copying an identity. A successful person’s experience shows, at most, that this road once worked under his conditions. It cannot prove that we share those conditions, much less that upon reaching the same destination we would approve of the same life.

Perhaps the most dangerous and least visible development occurs when other people’s demands become “my own desires.”

From childhood, a person repeatedly hears that only certain achievements count as promise and only certain positions merit respect. Later, no one needs to command him. He still says, “No one is forcing me; I simply cannot tolerate mediocrity.” This may be true. It may also mean that the external standard has entered his self-evaluation, so that stopping makes him feel ashamed, unworthy, and left behind. Self-determination theory distinguishes this internalized pressure from autonomous motivation grounded in understanding and endorsement. The fact that a demand has entered the mind does not mean it has become a value the person endorses.

It is therefore sometimes insufficient to ask, “Is this what I myself want?” We must ask one more question: do I want the thing itself, or can I simply not bear other people’s judgment, and my own, if I fail to achieve it?

This is not a golden rule either. Someone may genuinely value income, career, family, and the approval of others. Motives are naturally mixed; they are neither wholly autonomous nor wholly coerced. Some people follow familiar roads not from a lack of imagination, but because they cannot bear the cost of experimentation. The same failed career change may cost one person only a year of lower earnings and another the basic livelihood of an entire family. We cannot blame both forms of caution by the same standard. “Why not live boldly for once?” cannot substitute for understanding someone’s actual circumstances.

Now we come to the point I most want to preserve from the question: repetition itself is not the problem.

Other people have already loved. Does that make my love unreal? Other people have raised children, written novels, and learned the piano. Does my doing these things amount only to repetition? Obviously not.

From an observer’s classification, it is the same activity. From the participant’s experience, it may be the first time he has truly learned to let another person finish speaking, the first time he has understood a sentence he read long ago, or the first time he is willing to bear the consequences of a relationship. Something having happened in cultural history does not mean it has happened in this person’s life.

Living one’s own life therefore does not require inventing a form of life never seen before. One person can autonomously choose stable work and a familiar daily routine. Another can constantly pursue novelty and difference only because he fears not being special enough.

Deliberate opposition is not freedom either. If I must hate whatever other people like, they still determine my direction; I have merely put a minus sign in front of it. This and the pursuit of an extraordinary life are two directions on the same axis.

One distinction is, I think, the key to the entire problem: originality asks how you differ from what already exists; agency asks how you participate in forming, endorsing, and revising your own direction. The first is comparative. The second is not.

The way to escape the shared standard is therefore not to run farther, act stranger, or become rarer along the same dimension. That is still comparison. The way out is to change dimensions. Once we do, the question that actually matters changes as well. It is no longer, “Has anyone walked this road before?” but: having understood what it will cost and what it may bring, do I still want to walk it? And if I discover that it does not suit me, can I adjust?

External results can be duplicated. Two people may achieve the same professional success. Through the process, one gradually develops abilities he values and endorses the responsibilities he has assumed. The other has merely crossed the last item off a qualifying checklist that burdened him for years. Their achievements look alike, but what they gained is entirely different. Psychological research on goal concordance has likewise found that goals more closely aligned with a person’s interests and core values are associated with more sustained effort and greater gains in well-being after achievement. This does not guarantee that following one’s heart will produce success. It does show that between “achieving a goal” and “what it feels like to live the resulting life” lies a crucial question: why is this goal worth pursuing?

Finally, the demand to “live an extraordinary life” should itself be examined. Why must everyone possess a life worth narrating and envying? May a person not cherish stability, intimacy, concentration, or even a period that for now yields no visible result? If even “being yourself” becomes a task that requires more stories and more style than other people have, it is only another form of comparison.

What deserves our real vigilance is not similarity, but gradually ceasing to ask whether this is the life we still want, because we are trying to make ourselves into a success others can recognize at a glance.

A life need not be unprecedented to belong truly to oneself. Nor must it be enviable to be worth living.

30. Why Death May Not Be the End of Everything

Good question. This is indeed my personal view, not a scientific conclusion. I do not base it on evidence, near-death experiences, what some great scientist believed, or the statement that “science cannot prove it does not exist.” It is a judgment of mine, or, one might say, a faith. But it is a faith with reasons, reasons that can travel as far as they are able. At the point where they can go no farther, the final step is one each person must leap for himself. Yes, Kierkegaard’s leap: the leap of faith.

My reason can be condensed into a single sentence: the source of negation is unknowable, yet reason is somehow able to perform negation, so reason and negation must come from the same source.

Let us begin with negation itself. I do not mean the kind of negation involved in instinctive avoidance. Suppose we are looking for our keys, glance at the table, and say, “The keys are not on the table.” What did we see? A tabletop, several books, and a cup. We did not see “not.” There is no object on the table called “not.” We brought that “not” with us. We first held the keys in mind, compared them with what lay before us, and only then did “not” appear. The world itself gives us only what is: this is here, that is there, this becomes that. “Not” appears only where someone judges.

Where, then, does this “not,” this negation, come from? I think the question cannot be answered, and not merely because we are temporarily unable to answer it. To answer, we would first have to distinguish negation from something else and say that it is this and not that. But making the distinction already uses negation. We try to go behind “not” to see where it comes from, yet every step must be taken by means of “not.” Its source is invisible in principle, as the eye sees everything except itself in the act of seeing.

Now consider reason. A person, like a table or a cup, is made of atoms. Yet a person somehow forms the concept of “not,” while tables and cups do not. A person can say of the facts before him, “It should not be this way”; of an arrangement others give him, “I do not accept it”; of his own conclusion yesterday, “I was wrong.” He can even negate his own existence and think that one day he will no longer be here.

Reason uses “not” completely and without remainder. Thinking distinguishes; judgment affirms or denies. Remove “not,” and reason can do nothing. This is what I mean when I say that reason and negation come from the same source. If the source of negation is not among the things we can know, then the source of reason does not lie entirely among them either.

Only now do we arrive at death.

Death is the greatest “not” a person will encounter. The body will stop. The brain will stop. Everything in me that belongs to the order of things will be negated. I accept all of this without qualification.

The next step, I admit, is not deduced. It is judged. Death itself is an act of negation. Negation can fall upon every thing, but it cannot fall upon its own source. What death takes is what belongs in me to the order of things. The part of me that can say “not” comes from the same place as “not” itself. My judgment is that it will not thereby become nothing at all.

I mark this step separately so that anyone who disagrees can see exactly where our paths divide.

What, then, is there after death? I cannot say. According to my own reasons, I should not be able to say. If the source of negation is unknowable, then what becomes, after leaving the body, of something that comes from the same place is likewise unknowable. I therefore do not describe an afterlife or promise that memory and personality remain unchanged. I say only as much as my reasons allow.

I know that many people who ask this question are really thinking of a particular person. I cannot tell you that you will certainly meet again. I can say only that the person has not simply become nothing.

Someone will naturally object that deep anesthesia extinguishes consciousness and brain injury changes a person. I accept all of this. It shows that the operation of reason in this life depends on the brain. But it concerns operation, not source. Neuroscience can describe with increasing precision what happens in the brain when someone judges or says “not.” Yet every step of that description uses negation: this region activated and that one did not; this signal crossed the threshold and that one did not. It can explain the mechanism of negation with increasing clarity, but it explains “not” by using “not.” It cannot pass behind negation itself.

To be fair to the objection, believing that nothing remains after death is also a judgment. Science can show that consciousness depends on the operation of the brain. To move from this to “when the brain stops, there is nothing at all” requires one further step. That step is the same kind as mine: a judgment made at the boundary of knowledge, only in the opposite direction. I therefore do not regard people who make the other judgment as unreasonable. We stand at the same place and judge differently.

My reasons are close to those of Socrates and Kant. I say that the reasons are close, not that I believe because they did.

Plato’s Phaedo records Socrates’ conversation on the day of his death. Socrates offers several arguments there. One says, roughly, that the soul can know things that are invisible and unchanging, so it belongs to the same kind as they rather than to the kind of the perishable body. My reason resembles this, except that I do not need to posit an invisible realm of Forms. I use something everyone uses every day: “not.”

Kant listed twelve categories of the understanding, and negation is one of them. To call it a category means that it is not learned from experience, but is a condition under which experience is possible for us. Yet Kant himself warned that the immortality of the soul cannot be deduced from “I think.” Such a deduction would carry reason beyond its limits. He therefore placed immortality within practical reason as a postulate that could not be proved but had to be assumed. In the preface to the second edition of the Critique of Pure Reason, he famously said, in effect, that he had to suspend knowledge to make room for faith. My judgment occupies the same place. He reached it through the moral law; I reach it through negation.

After telling a story about the world after death, Socrates added, roughly, that a sensible person would not insist that things were exactly as he had described them. But believing that they were something like that was a risk worth taking, a noble risk. If he were right, he might meet Homer. I love that sentence. It stops where it ought to stop.

So, to return to my friend’s question: “There is nothing after death” is itself a “not,” and the greatest one. The part of me that enables me to say that sentence, I judge and believe, does not lie within the reach of what the sentence can negate.

Part III: Education, Freedom, and Relationships

Education often prescribes a person’s direction in the name of “what is best for you.” Relationships, too, may use concern, competition, or shame to force someone into obedience. Having many options does not necessarily amount to freedom. If changing direction means losing safety, belonging, or the right to be respected, the choice may still be superficial. This part approaches the conditions of freedom through family, school, and public events. It also asks why bystanders protect the powerful and blame victims. A person’s boundaries do not sever relationships with others. They make it possible for relationships to stop erasing the person as their price.

31. What Fear-Based Education Leaves in a Child

My friend’s question seems to carry a wound. Of course, I heard this sentence many times as a child. Many people raised in East Asian environments will probably recognize it: if you do badly on this exam, you will not enter a good high school; without a good high school, you will not enter a good university; without a good job, your whole life will be over.

To be fair, the sentence worked. It frightened me very effectively. But it worked because it eliminated persuasion. Tell a child what abilities mathematics can develop, and the child may answer, “I do not want to study it now. What is the use?” Once life is compressed into a one-way road, however, the child need not understand why something is worth doing. He needs only to fear the consequences of not doing it. I have written before that creating panic is a shortcut to power. The structure here is the same, only moved into the home.

There is one difference between the home and an arena of power. Parents who say these things are often their first victims. The chain linking the college entrance examination, elite schools, employment, and class position appears real to many families, and some of its links do have grave consequences. Parents may not be deliberately frightening their children. Often they become afraid first, take fear for truth, and pass the fear down.

The problem lies in the leap from a real risk to the words “your life is over.” “This examination may affect the range of choices available to you later” might be true. But “therefore, if you fail it, you as a person have no value” does not follow. A limited institutional risk is enlarged into a verdict on the whole person.

High expectations are not themselves the problem. One study followed more than eight hundred Chinese and American middle-school students and distinguished two forms of parental control. Behavioral control, such as setting rules and supervising homework, was associated with better academic performance in both countries. Psychological control, such as humiliation, inducing guilt, and withdrawing affection after poor grades, predicted worse emotional well-being in both. “If you do not do this, your life is over” belongs to the latter category. It does not become harmless because it occurs in East Asia.

What happens after the child grows up? The deepest effect, I think, is not fear of examinations. It is learning to drive oneself with catastrophe.

The parents are no longer there, yet the person continues telling himself: if this project fails, my life is over; if I do not get promoted this time, my life is over; if I stop to rest, I will be left behind. The external threat has disappeared, but the person issuing it now lives inside. Such people may become highly successful and still find security almost impossible. Success does not feel like completion. It is only the temporary silencing of an alarm, before the next one sounds. Many capable people therefore cannot truly rest. To them, rest is not stopping; it is falling backward.

The irony is that this education is intended to make children more competitive, yet often leaves them less willing to try. Failure has never been merely an outcome in their minds. It is a verdict on identity. The safest strategy, then, is to attempt only what they already know how to do successfully.

Some parents will say, “Look, we were right to pressure you. You entered such-and-such university.” This too contains a fallacy. It may be true that coercion made the child study more. But that does not answer another question: could some approach that did not make the child feel for years that “one failure will end everything” also have led him to study seriously? Without making that comparison, a good outcome cannot prove that every threat used in the process was necessary.

Consequences should, of course, be discussed. Compare two statements: “If you do not prepare, you may do poorly this time, and that may affect some of your choices. Let us think together about what you want to do,” and “If you go on like this, your life is over.” Both discuss consequences. The first leaves a person with agency. The second produces a subject who obeys.

Those of us who grew up in such environments should do something as early as possible in adulthood: separate “this undertaking failed” from “I as a person am a failure.” They are not the same judgment. An examination can go badly. A career can collapse. Both deserve to be taken seriously, but neither is important enough to decide whether the person deserves to be treated well. If the inner voice saying “your life is over” has grown loud enough to affect sleep, daily life, or relationships, that is not a failure of willpower. Speaking directly with a professional is a proper response.

32. Why People Use AI to Make Games Instead of Simply Playing Them

The situation is not quite that extreme. People have not stopped playing games. According to this year’s report from the Entertainment Software Association, about two-thirds of Americans play video games for at least an hour each week. Last quarter, users spent a total of 29 billion hours on Roblox. But game-making really has changed. For the first time, making a game has itself become a game that ordinary people can play.

Playing and making used to be separated by a very high wall. Learning to play Mario takes ten minutes. Making a similar small game required first learning programming, engines, art, animation, and sound, and after several months one might still have nothing that ran. Roblox Build now allows someone to describe gameplay in a single sentence and have AI generate the game. Roblox said this month that about nine thousand games made with Build had already been published, and 71 percent of their creators had never used its development tools before. This is not a group of experienced developers switching tools. People who had previously been only players have crossed to the other side of the wall.

Why can “making” be even more fun than “playing”? Because making is itself a creative game. You tell an AI, “Make me a New York where, after it snows, every car turns into a crab.” A few dozen seconds later you enter and run around. The crabs seem too slow, so you say, “Make them faster, add a few police penguins, and cut gravity in half,” then enter again. Challenge, feedback, adjustment, and another challenge: the structure is exactly the same as playing a game, with one additional sensation. The world has changed immediately in response to something I just imagined.

I think the deeper layer lies in the word “game” itself. The philosopher Bernard Suits gave a famous definition: to play a game is voluntarily to overcome unnecessary obstacles. Playing someone else’s game means accepting obstacles and conditions of victory set for you. Making your own means deciding even what the obstacles are and what counts as winning. The pleasure moves from achieving someone else’s end to setting the end itself. When I wrote about Alpha School, I said that it gives children their own pace but not their own ends. AI game-making does the opposite. It gives ordinary people the step of setting the end, even if the end is only a snowy New York filled with crabs.

In the same book, Suits imagined a utopia where every need had been met. What would remain for people to do? His answer was games, because games alone are done for their own sake. AI is making more and more activities unnecessary. The answer sounds less like a joke all the time.

Of course, being able to generate a game is not the same as making one others want to play. AI lowers the cost of implementation. It does not automatically supply delight, pacing, aesthetic judgment, or an understanding of people. Roblox’s recommendation system looks at whether a game retains players for more than twenty-eight days. Over the past year, its ten highest-earning creators each averaged more than $65 million, while the median income among the more than forty thousand creators who shared revenue was only about $1,500. Making a game will become as ordinary as taking a photograph. Making a game someone wants to play for twenty hours will remain difficult, just as smartphones did not eliminate film directors.

The most interesting development, then, is not that everyone can become a developer. It is this: when anyone can build a world in a few dozen seconds, the scarce thing is no longer the ability to make something, but knowing what you want to exist. AI tears down the wall. It cannot answer that question, because the question properly belongs to human beings.

33. Why Variants of Egoism Are Flourishing

This question is sharp because contemporary egoism rarely says directly, “Care only about yourself.” It dresses itself up as self-help and quietly adopts a respectable vocabulary: boundaries, avoiding internal friction, personal growth, emotional value, personal branding, becoming your best self.

Each term may be perfectly reasonable on its own. Put them together, however, and they can become a closed system: “If I succeed, it is because I take responsibility for myself. If I fail, it is because I have not optimized enough. Other people’s needs violate my boundaries. The demands of a community restrict my freedom.” By this point, egoism no longer has to admit that it is egoism. It calls itself growing up and becoming mature.

Why does it spread so easily? Half the answer is that people do not feel secure. AI may replace their work. Housing grows more expensive. Retirement is uncertain. Institutions may not be reliable. Naturally, people protect themselves first. In the United States General Social Survey, the proportion of respondents who believed that most people could be trusted fell from more than 40 percent in the early 1970s to a little over 30 percent. Problems that once belonged partly to institutions have been translated into the individual’s responsibility to manage himself. Lose your job? Improve your skills. Anxious? Meditate. Lonely? Practice social skills. Each suggestion may be useful. The problem is that once they are available, the question “Is there something wrong with the system?” never has to appear.

The other half of the answer is that this sells well. Traditional consumerism tells you that you still lack an object and should hurry to buy it. Self-optimization tells you that you are the deficient object. Once you buy a car, the purchase is complete. You can go on upgrading yourself forever.

From the perspective of SAE, these variants share a name: colonization. SAE follows Kant, whose formula is clear: whether in your own person or the person of anyone else, humanity must always be treated at the same time as an end, never merely as a means. The sentence points in two directions. SAE’s opposition to colonization therefore also points outward and inward.

Outwardly, we turn other people into suppliers. Friends must provide emotional value. Acquaintances must be useful. “Low-energy people” must be avoided. Others still appear to be respected, but they have really been packaged as a more advanced service: you are not my instrument; you are my provider of emotional value. Real relationships necessarily contain times that cannot be monetized at once. A sick friend cannot give you positive energy. A child may consume your time for years. If every relationship must constantly prove itself worthy of my investment, the person on the other side, as a person, has already disappeared.

Inwardly, we turn ourselves into assets. In my essay on cultural deserts, I listed examples: reading to improve cognition, making friends to build a network, resting to restore performance. That essay described the logic of a place spilling over into human beings. Today I would add that the person applying the logic is often the self. This is colonization in its most hidden form. By the end, egoism does not even benefit the self. It serves a balance sheet, while the real person becomes an instrument for making the sheet look good.

But opposition to egoism easily falls into another trap. A family demands sacrifice. An organization demands devotion. Other people demand endless understanding, then tell you not to be selfish. This too is colonization, with a different colonizer.
