SAE therefore does not answer by retreating from “live for yourself” to “live for others.” It insists that three statements hold simultaneously: I may not treat others as means; others may not treat me as a means; I may not treat myself as a means.

The word “boundary” also needs clarification here. A genuine boundary says: you may not decide my ends for me. A distorted boundary says: your needs may place no demand at all upon me. The first protects the person as an agent. The second cancels the existence of others.

The parts of a person’s life that yield no return, accompanying a sick friend, days that produce nothing, an afternoon spent for no purpose at all, are not inefficiencies to optimize away. They are precisely where a person cannot be colonized. Egoism needs to answer only, “How do I get what I want?” Opposition to colonization must also answer: when both you and I are ends, and neither can erase the other, how do we live together?

The problem with these variants of egoism is therefore not that they love the self too much, but that they love the wrong object. They love the grasping little “I.” The genuine self, like the genuine other, is not that little “I.”

34. Why Farewell to Revolution Has Endured

This is an unusual question. The book is in fact a record of conversations between two people. At the beginning of 1992, Li Zehou arrived in Colorado. He was at Colorado College and Liu Zaifu at the University of Colorado, a two-hour drive apart. They often met to talk about twentieth-century China. The manuscript was originally titled Looking Back at Twentieth-Century China. Only later were the four Chinese characters meaning “farewell to revolution” added in front as the main title. It was published in Hong Kong in 1995. Those four characters were not a neutral academic title. They were a demand that readers take a position. Li Zehou died in Colorado in 2021. Liu Zaifu died in Hangzhou this May at the age of eighty-five. Overseas newspaper obituaries still put the phrase “reform, not revolution” in their headlines. Thirty years after the book appeared, with both authors gone, people still read it, denounce it, and cite it.

Let us begin with what the book said, and what it did not say. Liu Zaifu later explained repeatedly that what they were saying farewell to was not the historical justice of revolution, but the belief that revolution should be history’s only choice. They opposed overthrowing a regime through mass violence, one class overthrowing another. They chose reconciliation between classes. In Liu’s words, reconciliation did not exclude struggle, but it rejected large-scale bloodshed. Li Zehou took the counterfactual one step further, suggesting that China might have fared better had it followed the path of constitutional reform proposed by Kang Youwei and Liang Qichao.

I think the surface reason for the book’s endurance is that it confronts a deeply rooted habit: automatically treating revolution as progress and reform as weakness. In twentieth-century China, revolution was a glorious word, while reform sounded almost like surrender. The book reverses the ranking and asks what violent rupture actually cost, and whether gradual change might be more difficult and more worthwhile.

At a deeper level, it asks whether an end can redeem its means. For the sake of a better future, may we first treat a group of people living today as sacrificial material? The question belongs neither only to China nor only to the twentieth century. In my own philosophical framework, this is the colonization of people in the present in the name of the future. Future people are abstract. People alive today are concrete. The abstract people have not yet been born and cannot themselves suffer what is done.

Then comes the crucial point: the book was attacked from both sides. After publication, it faced a nationwide campaign of criticism in mainland China. As late as 2021, an article on the website of Qiushi called the “farewell to revolution theory” the most typical form of historical nihilism. But liberals rejected it as well. Rong Jian argued that reform is active, chosen, and designed, whereas revolution is passive, unchosen, and unpredictable. Once the road of reform is blocked, one cannot bid revolution farewell simply by wishing to do so. Qin Hui noted that reform does not necessarily shed less blood. The Meiji Restoration cost far more lives than the 1911 Revolution; what matters is that rulers feel pressure to change. Zhu Xueqin put it more bluntly: revolution is not something one can abandon at will. When both the official side and the liberal side use a book as a target, it is unlikely to be forgotten.

The most interesting question, I think, is why the authorities had to criticize it. In principle, “reform, not revolution” is precisely what a government would most want the public to internalize, and in practice what it fears most is a “color revolution.” Yet its narrative of legitimacy comes from revolution. To bid farewell to revolution would loosen its own account of where it came from. It cannot abandon revolution in narrative, while fearing nothing more in reality. The book presses exactly on this crack. It can neither be absorbed nor discarded.

Some critics, however, identified the question that most needs to be asked of the book: to whom is “farewell to revolution” addressed? Revolutions are rarely chosen by the weak. More often they arrive when channels of reform have been closed. Whether a society can bid farewell to revolution therefore does not depend on people who want one, but on those whose hands hold the switch for reform. The book was also bidding farewell to Mao Zedong’s philosophy of struggle. Liu Zaifu considered himself throughout his life a reformer within the system. The authors advised both sides, but only the side holding the switch could decide whether the advice was possible.

Finally, let me offer my own reading. I do not think the true dividing line lies between revolution and reform. Revolutions that use elimination as their method rest on an image: remove a particular group, landlords, the bourgeoisie, rightists, the far left, environmentalists, conservatives, and a new world without contradictions will appear. But the disagreements among people, the parts that cannot be fully articulated or absorbed into any plan, what I have consistently called the remainder, cannot be eliminated. Remove one group and the next emerges on its own, so the purging must continue forever. Reform can reach the same point when “take it slowly” becomes “never,” and every disagreement is labeled instability.

To judge a change, then, we should not ask whether it calls itself revolution or reform. We should ask whether, after it is complete, people can still say no.

This book endures not because it supplied the answer, but precisely because it did not fix the answer in place. It compels every generation to answer the same question anew: when we believe we are creating a better world, are the people before us who disagree still ends in themselves?

35. Why Freedom Depends on Both Being Able Not to Act and Being Unable Not to Act

I should admit that the first half of this idea is borrowed from Kant. Only the second half is mine.

Begin with “what one is able to do.” This is both ability and menu: able to write, start a business, resign, move abroad. The longer the menu, the freer someone appears. But the length of the menu tells us only how many options exist, not who is choosing. A company gives an employee a hundred projects to choose from, but he may not refuse them all. He has many options and little freedom. A society tells you that you may become a doctor, lawyer, engineer, or entrepreneur, but that you may not refuse to compete, stop, or choose a low-income way of life you yourself endorse. It offers many actions but little freedom.

The first test, then, is whether one is able not to act. The difference between a person and a program is not the ability to choose among A, B, and C; a program can do that too. It is the ability to say, “None of them.” I often say that people, like tables and cups, are made of atoms, yet can say “no.” Freedom begins with that “no.”

And it must be a “no” one can truly utter. If refusal is allowed in name but costs someone his entire life, then he is effectively unable to refuse. The United States has an expression, “job lock.” Many people are afraid to resign not because they want to stay, but because health insurance is tied to employment. I wrote earlier that East Asian parents often tell children, “If you do not do this, your life is over.” What the sentence truly takes away is the child’s ability not to act. A society’s freedom is measured not only by how many things it permits people to do, but by the cost of saying no.

Yet “no” alone is insufficient. Someone who listens to no one and does whatever he wants may appear maximally free, but often he is simply dragged along by desires, fears, and stimuli. He says no today and no tomorrow, until nothing remains that is truly his own.

We therefore need a second test: can a person be unable not to act? The phrase sounds paradoxical. We usually think freedom means the absence of anything one “must” do. But there are two kinds of necessity. In one, others force me to act. In the other, after thinking the matter through, I discover that I cannot act otherwise. Someone could betray a friend and suffer no punishment, yet says, “I cannot do that.” What stops him is neither the police nor calculation, but a principle he has made his own.

Before Socrates died, his friends had arranged an escape. He could leave, and he could also refuse. In the end he stayed, because he believed he could not repay injustice with injustice. All three elements appear in one scene.

Kant gave freedom two sides. Roughly speaking, the negative side is that the will is not governed by external causes. The positive side is that the will gives itself the law, which he called autonomy. Being able not to act is the first side. Being unable not to act is the second. Freedom is not the absence of constraint. It is a question of where the constraint comes from.

The hardest part is that the two kinds of necessity look identical from the outside. “I must succeed.” “I cannot stop.” “I must constantly prove myself.” These may express commitments a person has made his own. They may also be an external measure long ago moved inside his mind, so that whoever once enforced it no longer needs to be present. I call the latter colonization. In its most complete form, the colonized person believes that the voice is his own.

How can we distinguish them? My method is to ask whether the “must” has passed through “no.” A necessity to which you have never dared say no is not your necessity. Only when you truly could refuse, have considered and weighed the choice, and still say, “I cannot fail to do this,” is the necessity self-legislated. The three elements therefore have an order. Being able not to act is the gate through which one reaches being unable not to act.

One qualification must be added. My “must” cannot cancel someone else’s “no.” If a person believes he bears historical necessity and everyone else must clear the road, that is not autonomy. It is the imposition of his law on others, still colonization, only pointed in another direction.

What you are able to do may be given to you by others. What you are able not to do may be taken away by others. What you are unable not to do can be given to you only by yourself.

36. Why Excessive “Freedom” Can Lead to Servitude

I put “freedom” in quotation marks because I have never said this, and I do not agree with the statement as it stands. Plato said it first. In Book VIII of the Republic, he suggested that excessive freedom, whether for an individual or a city, seems to change into nothing but excessive slavery. He told the story of a city where everyone did as he pleased and accepted no authority. Disorder grew until people elevated a protector, who step by step became a tyrant.

But let me first explain the quotation marks. In the previous essay, I said that freedom is measured not by what one can do, but by what one can refrain from doing and what one cannot refrain from doing. In “excessive freedom,” only the first element is ever excessive. The ability to do is pushed to the limit and devours the other two. What leads to servitude is not freedom, but one component of freedom. I can see at least four routes by which it devours the others.

First, within the self, “being able to do” consumes “being able not to do.” Part IV of Spinoza’s Ethics is titled “Of Human Bondage.” By bondage he means that people cannot govern their affects. They know what is better, yet are dragged toward what is worse. Someone finally has a weekend entirely to himself and plans to watch ten minutes of video. He looks up and three hours are gone. No one commanded him, but it is hard to say that he chose those three hours. Nor is this entirely a problem of individual will. Autoplay and infinite scrolling are designed by whole teams optimizing the time you remain on a product. Put bluntly, their daily work is to make your capacity not to act smaller.

Second, across time, one exercise of “being able to do” can cancel every future “being able not to do.” In Chapter V of On Liberty, Mill offered an extreme example: someone voluntarily sells himself into slavery. At the moment he signs, he is free, but the content of his choice is that he can never choose again. Mill concluded that the principle of freedom cannot require the freedom to become unfree. Permitting someone to surrender his freedom is not freedom. The principle is close at hand. California law generally refuses to enforce noncompete agreements; even if you signed one, it is invalid. “You signed voluntarily at the time” explains the beginning of an arrangement. It does not establish the right to govern forever. Promising one particular act is not the same as surrendering, all at once, one’s future right to say no.

Third, when people lack an “unable not to act” of their own, they borrow someone else’s. Why did Plato’s city elevate a tyrant? Its people retained only the ability to do whatever they pleased and never learned to give themselves a law. After enough disorder, fear, and exhaustion, someone appears and says, “Do as I say and you will no longer need to be afraid.” During the Second World War, Erich Fromm wrote Escape from Freedom about the same development. People free themselves from old bonds but fail to develop a direction of their own. When loneliness and anxiety descend, they willingly surrender themselves. In the previous essay, I said that only you can give yourself what you cannot refrain from doing. Here I add: if you do not give it to yourself, someone else will be delighted to give it to you.

Fourth, between people, my “ability to do” consumes your “ability not to do.” In “Two Concepts of Liberty,” Isaiah Berlin quoted the saying that freedom for the pike is death for the minnows. In the 1905 case Lochner v. New York, the United States Supreme Court invoked freedom of contract to strike down a state law limiting bakers to ten hours of work a day. Freedom from restrictions on the employer’s terms was the employer’s liberty. For a baker unable to find other work, the other side of that liberty was an inability to refuse a working day longer than ten hours. Often the problem is not that everyone is too free. It is that someone has expanded the power to decide for himself into power over others.

Finally comes the most dangerous reversal. Someone may answer: “Your desires have blinded you. You do not know what you truly want, so let me decide for you. That is real freedom.” Rousseau said that anyone who refused to obey the general will would be compelled by the whole body to do so, which meant that he would be “forced to be free.” This is precisely what Berlin distrusted most. The test remains the same: after someone has decided for you, may you reject his interpretation? If not, he has not given you freedom. He has given you another master with an attractive name.

I therefore do not much like the phrase “excessive freedom.” There cannot be too much freedom. There can only be too much ability to do. What leads to servitude is never too much freedom, but too little “no.”

37. Why a University Would Conceal Sexual Assault in a Fraternity

This is a painful question, and many people are now concerned about the case as news reports continue to follow it.

Let us first clarify what is known. According to a female student, she was drugged at the Chi Phi fraternity and sexually assaulted by several members in October 2024. In November, the university received a report, suspended the chapter, placed seven students on interim suspension, and issued a statement. An internal investigation began in January 2025. The county district attorney later concluded that the evidence was insufficient to bring criminal charges.

This month, she sued Cornell, the fraternity, and the seven students. According to her attorney, only two were ultimately expelled. The complaint says that the others received sanctions including suspension, training, and writing essays. On September 21, Cornell issued a statement saying that it had investigated and handled the matter according to university rules, that the chapter remained barred from campus, and that federal privacy law prevented disclosure of individual student discipline. It would address specific allegations in the litigation.

Strictly speaking, Cornell did not do nothing. No court has ruled that it deliberately covered up the case. Nor have the seven individuals been convicted in a criminal proceeding. But I completely understand why people feel that the university is concealing something. The reason lies not in any particular person’s intentions, but in the position the institution occupies.

Cornell simultaneously plays three roles: investigator, adjudicator, and defendant. One reason the student is suing the university is alleged negligent supervision. The institution must discover the truth and protect students while defending itself in court, and every statement it makes may become evidence for the other side. The legally rational response is therefore to say as little as possible. “We will respond through the legal process” is impeccable advice from counsel. The problem is that, at that point, the university’s interests and the student’s interests no longer lie on the same side.

Now consider the reason Cornell offers: privacy law. Privacy law genuinely protects students, but it is not as airtight as the university suggests. Federal law permits a school to disclose the final results of disciplinary proceedings to a victim. In cases involving violence or sexual assault, when a student is found responsible, it may even permit disclosure of the outcome to the public. Another federal law goes further, requiring a school to notify both sides in writing of the outcome, sanction, and rationale. Cornell’s student newspaper asked in an editorial why the complainant had not been informed of the investigative outcome, and why the interim suspensions had been lifted. If she truly did not receive the required notice, privacy law cannot explain that. Privacy can block the public. It cannot block a party to the same case.

The essays must also be discussed as allegations in the complaint. But if true, they reveal more than silence. A reflective essay is ordinarily a university’s response to plagiarism or a violation of dormitory rules. Using it to answer an allegation of drugging and sexual assault translates a potentially criminal act into the language of education, as though it were something from which the accused could learn and improve. The student newspaper called this a diminishment of the gravity of the offense. I strongly agree.

Another layer is easily overlooked. The district attorney declined prosecution because criminal conviction requires proof beyond a reasonable doubt, a high threshold. Internal proceedings usually apply a much lower evidentiary standard. For her, the university was therefore almost the only place where the case might receive an answer. If that place is a black box, she is left only with a lawsuit, and on the other side of the lawsuit stands the university itself. The psychologist Jennifer Freyd calls this kind of harm institutional betrayal. The injury comes not only from the original event, but from the place you trusted and expected to protect you protecting itself first when you most needed it.

I often say that persons are ends in themselves. A university exists for students; students do not exist for the university’s reputation. Once an institution treats its reputation and legal risk as ends, a student becomes a risk item to manage and control, someone who must say as little as possible. No one has to be evil. Everyone need only do the safest thing according to his job. Transparency also protects the accused. When the outcome is invisible, someone falsely accused cannot clear his name either.

Protecting students from public harm and protecting a university from questions about its responsibility are two different things. The first is privacy. The second has merely borrowed privacy’s name.

38. Why People Blame Victims and Protect Perpetrators

The phenomenon my friend asks about is not new. Whenever a case like this occurs, two kinds of voices appear. One blames the victim for failing to protect herself. The other invokes privacy on behalf of the accused. The two must be examined separately. The first is almost always wrong. The second is sometimes reasonable, but is often used in the wrong place.

Begin with blaming the victim. Psychology speaks of belief in a just world: people want to believe that what happens to someone broadly corresponds to what he has done. Acknowledging that someone who did nothing wrong can still be gravely harmed makes bystanders anxious. They therefore search for a reason to separate themselves from the victim: if she had not gone to that party or drunk so much, it would not have happened. I would never do that, so I am safe. Blaming her is a way of buying a sense of security. But advising people how to reduce risk and deciding who bears responsibility are two different questions. It is sensible to remind people to lock their doors. An unlocked door does not vindicate the thief.

Sexual assault is also governed by a special double standard. Two German psychologists conducted an experiment in which participants read descriptions of rape and robbery. Roughly speaking, the more familiar victim and perpetrator were, the more the victim was blamed in the rape case. The same relationship produced no such effect in the robbery case. Without anyone noticing, the question changes from whether she consented on this occasion to whether she is a “good girl” worthy of protection. In her statement, Chanel Miller, the victim in the Stanford case, listed questions the defense had asked: How much did you drink? What kind of cup did you use? Did you attend fraternity parties? Was your relationship with your boyfriend serious? Someone had been violated, yet in court it was her way of spending a weekend that was examined again and again. “I disapprove of your way of life” is quietly substituted for “you do not deserve to remain inviolable.”

Now consider the other side. The Cornell philosopher Kate Manne coined the term himpathy for sympathy that drains away from a female victim and flows toward a male perpetrator. She developed the idea precisely through the Turner case. The father’s reference to “twenty minutes,” and the judge’s worry about the “severe impact” on Turner, exemplified this flow. Why does sympathy move in that direction? Many people look at the young man and see their son, classmate, friend, or younger self. “He is usually a good person.” “He gets good grades.” “He has a promising future.” Even if all of this is true, none of it answers whether a particular event occurred on a particular day.

There is a more concealed reversal that Jennifer Freyd calls DARVO: deny, attack the person making the accusation, then reverse the positions of victim and offender. The discussion shifts from “Did this occur?” to “She is destroying a person’s life.” Of course, denying an accusation, hiring counsel, and offering contrary evidence do not in themselves constitute such a reversal. The concept helps us ask whether the discussion has changed subjects; it does not determine guilt.

Privacy deserves to be taken seriously. Before the facts are established, no one should publish someone’s home address or harass his family. Even a conviction does not give everyone unlimited license to excavate his life. We can test the sincerity of a claim about privacy by asking whether it protects both sides. If someone insists that his life must not be destroyed while exposing every detail of how much she drank and whom she dated, privacy is not the principle being protected. A particular side is. We must also ask what privacy is shielding. Refusing to disclose unnecessary personal information is one thing. Forbidding investigation, preventing an account of the process, and above all blocking questions about institutional responsibility are another.

Underneath both voices lies the same question: whose future is treated as valuable without proof, and whose suffering must first prove that it deserves to matter? I often say that persons are ends in themselves. The statement has no conditions. Not only well-behaved people are ends. A person who drank, went to a party, or knew the other person is an end too. Conversely, the accused is also an end, so he is entitled to due process and the presumption of innocence.

Taking a report seriously does not mean declaring guilt before trial. Insisting on the presumption of innocence does not mean presuming the reporter guilty first. No one should have to prove that she is a perfect person before she deserves not to be violated.

Part IV: Power and Public Life

Power does not exist only in government or legal codes. It also appears in who may define morality, who decides what knowledge deserves circulation, and how capital, universities, and technology companies shape public discussion. An institution may claim to protect everyone while requiring particular people to bear silence in its concrete operation. The essays in this part bring the principle that persons are ends in themselves into public life. This does not turn politics into an abstract principle. It asks who can still speak, refuse, and change a shared direction when the principle is put into practice.

39. Why People Are Left Only with Suffering When Those in Power Speak of Morality

Beautifully asked. The key lies in two words: the first half says “can,” the second “can only.” The first opens a door. The second closes one.

First consider rulers speaking of law. Law is written and public, and at least in its text it also governs those who make it. The moment those in power say, “We act according to law,” they acknowledge a standard outside their own hands, under which they too must stand. Above law lies another open level: whether the law itself is just. The people can therefore continue asking, “On what grounds was this law made? Does it apply equally to everyone?” To ask that is to speak of morality.

Writing from the Birmingham jail, Martin Luther King Jr. responded to people who invoked injunctions and regulations against him. Human beings, he wrote, have a moral responsibility to obey just laws and a moral responsibility to disobey unjust ones. He cited Augustine: an unjust law is no law at all. King could say this precisely because the other side had first taken its stand at the level of law. The level above remained open for him to occupy.

Now consider rulers speaking of morality: “Consider the larger picture.” “Sacrifice selflessly.” “Show gratitude.” “Make the nation great again.” When such phrases come from those who hold power, the highest level has already been occupied. Dissent no longer appears as argument. It becomes evidence that one is immoral, selfish, indifferent to the common good, or ungrateful. There is no higher level above morality on which to stand, and law is often pliable at such moments. What remains in the hands of ordinary people? Only their wounds. “I truly cannot go on.” “No one can care for the elderly person in my family.” “My child cannot attend school.” These are no longer claims of right but pleas for mercy. In retreating from “You have no right to do this” to “Please take pity on us,” a citizen becomes merely a resident begging for consideration.

Mencius saw the scene more than two thousand years ago. He told King Xuan of Qi that when ordinary people were struggling simply to survive, how could they have time for ritual and righteousness? The ruler speaks endlessly of moral propriety; the people think only of escape from death. They are not speaking the same language.

Why is morality in the hands of those in power more dangerous than law? Law has boundaries; morality does not. Law governs conduct, and what is not prohibited is permitted. Morality can reach into what you think: are you grateful enough? Are you willing to sacrifice? If morality is not absolute, the refrain goes, you are absolutely immoral.

Law at least nominally governs upward as well as downward. The morality spoken by rulers usually governs only downward. More deeply, it enters the mind. When someone is mistreated, his first thought is not “this is unfair,” but “am I selfish to complain? Am I causing trouble for the larger picture?” I call this colonization. A measure pressed inward from outside becomes the measure by which people judge themselves.

The problem is not morality itself. Morality was meant to measure power, not to become power’s measure of people. In the hands of the people, it constrains power. Taken up by power, it constrains the people. Nor is law automatically good. Unjust laws abound. That is precisely why the moral level must remain open, so people can stand there and question them.

One final step remains unspoken. When even suffering is labeled negativity, performative misery, or troublemaking, so that pain itself cannot be voiced, the people are left only with silence.

The statement is therefore a thermometer. Listen to what those in power discuss. If they speak of law, some room remains. If they speak of morality, be cautious. When they say the people should stop talking about suffering and look at the nation’s achievements, very little room is left. The health of a society is measured not by how nobly its rulers speak, but by the language in which its people are still permitted to answer them.

40. Why Smit Machchhar Is a True Hero

This truly deserves to be called heroism, because so many lives were saved. On September 30, flydubai Flight FZ1073 was traveling from Dubai to Tel Aviv when something happened in the cockpit. Flight-tracking data showed that the plane dropped more than fourteen thousand feet in less than thirty seconds. Its transponder first transmitted an emergency code, then several minutes later a code for unlawful interference. It finally diverted to Tabuk in Saudi Arabia and landed safely. Both pilots were injured and hospitalized.

Up to this point, the event still looked like an aviation accident. What drew special attention was the airline’s confirmation that a conflict had occurred in the cockpit. It said that several off-duty members of its crew aboard the flight helped bring the aircraft under control and complete the diversion. The cause and motive were not announced, and the United Arab Emirates’ civil aviation authority was investigating.

The main account of what happened currently comes from the Israeli Prime Minister’s Office. It says that one pilot stabbed the other and intended to crash the aircraft. The injured captain, Machchhar, fought back and opened the cockpit door, allowing people outside to enter and restrain the assailant. On this account, he saved all 174 people aboard.

Why call him a true hero? A hero is not someone who feels no fear or cannot be wounded. He is someone who, after the wound has already fallen on him, still does something that changes the situation. Machchhar’s crucial action was not defeating someone alone. It was opening the door.

That door has a history. After September 11, cockpit doors were reinforced and locked from inside to prevent intruders from entering. In the 2015 Germanwings disaster, the danger was precisely that the threat was already behind the door. The copilot locked the captain outside. No matter how people tried to break in, the door would not open, and 150 people died. The same door that stops an external threat can also keep external rescuers out. When danger is inside, only a person inside can open it. This time, the person inside had already been stabbed, but he opened it anyway.

He was not the only hero in the story. Someone rushed in to restrain the other pilot. Several off-duty crew members landed the plane safely. Machchhar did not save everyone single-handedly. When he was nearly unable to continue, he made it possible for others to carry on the rescue. Acknowledging their contribution does not diminish his courage at all.

I wrote earlier that only you can give yourself what you are unable not to do. No one could order a man who had been stabbed to open that door, and no one would have blamed him if he had collapsed without doing anything more. He did it. That “must” was his own.

One further point is worth making. He is Indian. The passengers included Israelis and people of other nationalities. These identities may form part of the background, but they are not the source of his heroism. The test is simple: change the captain’s nationality and replace the passengers with people of other identities. Would we give the same action the same respect? If the answer changes, what we praise is not simply the saving of lives.

A hero is not someone who never falls. He is someone who, after falling, still goes to open the door.

41. Why a True Philosopher-King Would Relinquish Power

I did say this, but the philosopher-king is Plato’s term, not mine. In the Republic, the philosopher-king never wants to rule. He would rather remain outside the cave, looking upon the true light, but is required to return and take his turn governing. Plato also made an interesting observation: the city whose rulers are least eager to rule will be governed best. Yet Plato wanted philosophers not to crave power. He did not ask them to relinquish it. For him, returning to the cave was a duty.

More than two thousand years later, Kant gave another answer. In Perpetual Peace, he wrote that kings becoming philosophers, or philosophers kings, was neither to be expected nor desired, because possession of power inevitably corrupts the free judgment of reason. This is a grave claim. It does not say that bad people become worse when they gain power. It says that however good a person may be, once he holds power, his judgment is no longer free. Power gradually removes the people around him who can correct him. Whatever he says receives applause; when he is wrong, no one dares tell him. A true philosopher knows better than anyone that his judgment has blind spots. However intelligent he may be, some things remain invisible to him. His first reason for relinquishing power is that he does not entirely trust himself.

The second reason lies in the words “everyone objects.” Everyone begs him to stay. This appears to be the greatest possible confidence in him, but may in fact reveal the deepest dependence. In “What Is Enlightenment?” Kant wrote that immaturity is comfortable. If someone else reads for me and someone else judges for me, I need not trouble myself. Benevolent guardians place people in a walking frame and make them feel they will fall if they leave it. People try to escape a bad ruler. They cannot bear to lose a good one. The better he is, the less willing they are to learn to walk by themselves.

A ruler who truly treats persons as ends in themselves therefore measures success not by how much everyone needs him, but by how quickly they can cease needing him. The same is true of a good teacher. The final aim of teaching is to make the teacher unnecessary. I call this cultivation, in contrast to colonization. Colonization carves a person into the shape you want. Cultivation lets the person grow into himself. A philosopher-king whom no one can do without is actually engaged in colonization.

Nor can today’s unanimous consent bind tomorrow. What of people born later? What if those who approve today change their minds tomorrow? More importantly, when everyone agrees with one person, it is often not because everyone thinks alike, but because dissent has nowhere left to exist. Unanimity itself deserves suspicion.

History does offer someone who acted this way. In 1783, after the War of Independence, George Washington returned his military commission and went home to his estate. Before that, an officer had written urging him to become king, and Washington rejected the suggestion sternly. King George III is said to have remarked, on hearing that Washington intended to surrender power, that if he truly did so he would be the greatest man in the world. In 1796, many hoped Washington would continue as president. He left after two terms. The two-term custom lasted until Franklin Roosevelt broke it and was later written into the Constitution. Washington may not have been Plato’s philosopher-king, but he understood something important: a country cannot be dependable if it depends on one person.

The point must also be reversed. Persons are ends in themselves, and this includes the ruler. A community cannot demand that someone remain forever in a position merely because he is useful. That too is treating him as an instrument.

Of course, relinquishing power does not mean abandoning responsibility. If someone keeps every decision in his own hands, never gives others an opportunity to practice, then suddenly says, “You should govern yourselves now; I am leaving,” he has not transferred power. He has deserted his post. A genuine transfer places information, abilities, and opportunities to decide in other people’s hands before the leader leaves. Once gone, he does not remain behind the scenes granting or withholding approval.

I have written about why we always wait for great people. This essay is the other side. A genuinely great person is precisely someone who refuses to be awaited. The greatest moment of a philosopher-king is not when everyone begs him to remain. It is when, after he has left, everyone discovers that they can manage too.

42. Why Disasters Give Rise to Great Literature and Philosophy

Chinese thinkers considered this question very early. In his “Letter in Reply to Ren An,” Sima Qian offered a list. King Wen was imprisoned and elaborated the Book of Changes. Confucius was in distress and composed the Spring and Autumn Annals. Qu Yuan was exiled and wrote “Encountering Sorrow.” These people, Sima Qian said, carried frustrations in their hearts and found the road blocked, so they recounted the past and left their work to those who came after. When he wrote the letter, Sima Qian himself had recently suffered castration and was writing the Records of the Grand Historian. More than a thousand years later, Zhao Yi inscribed a more famous line on a poem by Yuan Haowen: “A nation’s misfortune is a poet’s good fortune; amid the world’s upheavals, the lines grow accomplished.”

There is something real here if we dig deeply enough. In peaceful times, an explanatory system can contain life: effort brings reward and obedience to rules brings protection. People need not question the assumptions of the system. I often say that constructions cannot be closed. Every construction conceals something, though in ordinary times it conceals it well enough to remain invisible. Prolonged disaster tears the covering away. The old explanations cannot contain what lies before us. Everything that never fit inside, human dignity, responsibility, what makes life worth continuing, is suddenly exposed. Hannah Arendt observed, roughly, that twentieth-century totalitarianism had exploded our categories of political thought and standards of moral judgment. The work of the latter half of her life was to think again amid the ruins.

The word “prolonged” matters. In a single disaster, people remember what happened on a particular day. A long disaster becomes the structure of the days themselves. In “The Stone Moat Village,” Du Fu did not record the total casualties of war. He wrote of one household. Three sons had gone to defend a city; two were already dead. The old woman was seized during the night to cook at the army camp. At dawn, “I set out again on the road, alone bidding farewell to the old man.” With the single word “alone,” one more person is gone from the family. Literature restores concrete persons to abstract numbers. Philosophy then asks: when we speak of “cost” and “necessity,” what have we counted, and what have we left out?

The Russian poet Anna Akhmatova also gave a one-word answer. During the Great Purge, her son was arrested, and for seventeen months she waited in line outside a prison in Leningrad. One day, a woman behind her, her lips blue from the cold, leaned close and asked, “Can you describe this?” Akhmatova answered, “I can.” The result was the long narrative cycle Requiem, now known around the world. The woman could not speak it herself. She entrusted it to someone who could. Du Fu’s “alone” and Akhmatova’s “can,” more than a thousand years apart, perform the same act: they speak for those who cannot.

But a long distance separates suffering from the creation of a great work. Pain does not automatically supply language, structure, and powers of observation. Du Fu was formed by the culture of the High Tang. The age of chaos changed what he wrote, but does not explain why he could write it in that way. Primo Levi returned to Turin from Auschwitz before he began writing If This Is a Man. The first edition appeared in 1947, and he revised it for a new edition eleven years later. A person who writes must first survive, then have time, ability, and someone willing to help publish the work.

We see only those who left works behind. Du Fu and Levi appear on the list. Outside it stand the illiterate, those whose manuscripts were burned, and those who did not live until the day they could write. Finding several great writers in a disaster does not prove that disaster creates more great writers. The same war may have contained several other Du Fus who died on the road without leaving a poem. Even a work that is written may not survive. Akhmatova’s friend Osip Mandelstam was arrested for a poem satirizing Stalin. Several years later he was arrested again and died in a transit camp on the way to a labor camp. His poems reached us because, over decades, his wife memorized them one by one. Sometimes a work survives because another person remembers it on its behalf.

Ouyang Xiu proposed that “poetry becomes accomplished after hardship.” But the line that follows is more profound and more worth remembering: it is not poetry that impoverishes people; rather, those who have suffered deprivation become accomplished poets. Writing does not bring misfortune upon their lives. After encountering hardship and obstruction in reality, people may become capable of writing truly refined and moving poetry.

Europeans felt the same unease in the face of their greatest disaster. After the Second World War, Adorno made the severe statement that writing poetry after Auschwitz was barbaric. More than a decade later, in Negative Dialectics, he revised it. Continuous suffering, he wrote in effect, has the right to expression, just as the tortured person has the right to scream, so it may have been wrong to say that no poetry could be written after Auschwitz. He withdrew the prohibition, not the unease. Poetry may be written because sufferers have the right to scream, not because suffering produces good poetry. Zhao Yi’s line about the nation’s misfortune being the poet’s good fortune is, in the end, a bystander’s sigh. How could the tortured person experience it as good fortune?

I am therefore always cautious about statements that suffering creates greatness. They can too easily become the injunction to be grateful for suffering. Someone may look back and say, “That period taught me a great deal.” That belongs to him, and he has the right to say it. But when others use the claim to advise someone currently suffering, or when those in power use it to demonstrate that pain has value, suffering becomes raw material for producing greatness, and the sufferer becomes a means. Persons are ends in themselves, including persons who suffer.

Greatness belongs to the person who, amid disaster, continues to see, remember, and write. It does not belong to the disaster. Suffering is not the cause of greatness. It is what greatness has been forced to face.

43. Why Leviathan Remains a Classic

I apologize for taking so long to answer this question. The preceding essay considered why prolonged disasters produce great thought, and Hobbes is one of the starkest examples.

He was born in 1588. By his own account, his mother went into premature labor after hearing that the Spanish Armada was approaching, so he and fear were born as twins. On the eve of the English Civil War, he fled to Paris. In exile he watched a king beheaded and a country torn apart, then published Leviathan in 1651. Its title comes from the sea monster in the Book of Job. The frontispiece depicts a crowned giant, sword in one hand and a bishop’s crozier in the other. Look closely, and the giant’s body is composed of hundreds of little people.

The first reason the book became a classic is that Hobbes treated the state as something requiring explanation. Before him, power came from God or from ancestry, and obedience needed no justification. Hobbes insisted on asking: why should people who originally live their own separate lives obey a power able to command them? His answer relied on neither God nor bloodline, but began from each individual. He called the state an “artificial man.” Human beings were both its material and its makers. From this point, political philosophy truly began to assume its modern form.

Second, he saw human beings accurately. He did not say human nature was evil. He said people were broadly equal: even the strongest feared a secret attack, and the weakest could join with others to kill the strongest. Everyone fears others and knows that others fear him. People carry weapons when they travel, lock their doors when they sleep, and lock chests even inside their homes. Are they not, Hobbes asked, accusing humanity by their actions just as he did in words? Without a common power that everyone fears, people live in a condition of war. This does not mean fighting every day, just as bad weather consists not in one or two showers but in a disposition toward rain lasting many days. Life under such conditions is, in the famous phrase, solitary, poor, nasty, brutish, and short.

We still use this analysis. With no world government above them, states exist in Hobbes’s state of nature. You expand your military for defense. I cannot tell whether your purpose is defense or attack, so I expand mine. Neither side wants war, yet war may result. In my essay on the race for AI, I wrote that each side believes that if it does not develop faster, the other will. That is a Hobbesian situation.

Then comes the most uncomfortable reason for the book’s importance: its conclusion. Beginning from equal individuals, Hobbes proceeds step by step to an almost unlimited sovereign. Sovereign power cannot be divided, and subjects may not withdraw obedience merely because the ruler governs badly. The inference is so clean that, to oppose it, one must identify exactly which step one rejects. Locke later answered that people fled the harm of polecats and foxes only to be content to be devoured by lions, imagining this to be security. The separation of powers, rule of law, and the people’s right to overthrow tyranny after Locke were, in a sense, answers to the question Hobbes left behind. A book that everyone for centuries has had to pass through before proceeding is a classic among classics.

Yet careful reading reveals a remainder inside Hobbes’s own construction. In Chapter XXI, he acknowledges that some rights cannot be surrendered through any covenant. If the sovereign commands you to kill yourself or forbids you to resist someone trying to kill you, you have no duty to obey. He also says that a subject’s obligation lasts only so long as the sovereign remains able to protect him. The thinker who defended absolute power most thoroughly still could not absorb the individual’s preservation of himself into the structure. Constructions cannot be closed. Not even Leviathan is an exception.

Another portion is often omitted from abridged editions. For Hobbes, the sovereign governs not only swords but thought. The sovereign decides which doctrines threaten peace, whether books require censorship before publication, and what universities teach. His reason is that people’s actions arise from their opinions. Many rulers have understood this well. I have written that creating panic is a shortcut to power. Leviathan is the theoretical version of the shortcut: first persuade people that without you the realm will fall into chaos, then ask them to surrender their judgment as well. “Without me, there will be chaos” is the world’s oldest Hobbesian sentence.

The irony is that power did not thank him. Four years after his death, Oxford University publicly burned Leviathan. A book defending absolute power was burned by power because it had made the reason for obedience too clear, so clear that people could also see where the reason ceased to apply.

Leviathan remains a classic not because it is right, but because it asks the question so sharply: why should I obey? Everyone seeking more power can find reasons in it. Everyone seeking to limit power must answer it first. We read Leviathan not to agree, but to understand precisely at which step we disagree.

For my part, I disagree at both the beginning and the end. Hobbes treated the exchange of obedience for protection as the source of all right. I do not deny the exchange. At the most basic level of power relations, people often do exchange obedience for protection. But this is only the lowest level. Some rights are neither acquired through exchange nor available for exchange. I also reject his destination. Hobbes thought that the more absolute the sovereign, the more complete the power. But power can exist only if the governed party remains a person with agency. To crush someone until he is no longer a person is not the summit of power; power has eliminated its own object. What Hobbes was forced to leave behind in Chapter XXI, an individual’s inalienable self-preservation, is precisely what no power can fully absorb. What he was forced to acknowledge at the end, I acknowledge at the beginning.

44. Why Capital Can Co-opt Academia

I share this feeling, but we should clarify what it means to “surpass” royal power. In cruelty, royal power stands far above capital: the burning of books, the burying of scholars, literary inquisitions, Galileo kneeling before the Inquisition and recanting his teaching. None of this occurs in universities today. But in thoroughness, capital may go further.

The most sophisticated ways royal power co-opted scholars were official appointments and civil-service examinations. Emperor Taizong of Tang is said to have watched newly successful candidates file past and declared that all the heroes under heaven had entered his net. By the Ming and Qing dynasties, the eight-legged essay governed selection and Zhu Xi’s commentaries had become standard answers. The questions that occupied a scholar’s entire life had already been chosen for him by the examination. This was deeply penetrating. Yet royal co-optation had two gaps. First, it was visible. The prohibition was written on paper, the blade hung overhead, and one knew where the wall was and whom to hate. Second, there remained somewhere outside the wall. A failed candidate could return home and teach. Someone who did not wish to become an official could withdraw. Tao Yuanming refused to bend his back for a petty salary, went home to grow beans, and still wrote his poems. Spinoza declined a professorship at Heidelberg, earned his living grinding lenses, and still wrote the Ethics. Scholarship then required paper, a pen, and several books. Royal power could govern a career, but not the study in one’s home.

Capital’s co-optation closes both gaps.

First, it is invisible on the surface. It does not issue prohibitions. It chooses what to fund. In 1967, a sugar industry organization in the United States paid several Harvard nutritionists to write a review for the New England Journal of Medicine. The review attributed heart disease chiefly to fat and passed lightly over sugar. The funding was never disclosed. No one forced the researchers to falsify results, but the sponsor set the topic, selected the literature, and reviewed drafts.

The arrangement became public only in 2016, when researchers uncovered the old files. More often, even this level of intervention is unnecessary. The direction of funding is enough. A platform company pays for research on how to make people use it more, but not on which uses should be reduced. Every individual paper may be true while the field’s knowledge as a whole is distorted. People can resist a prohibition. It is far harder to resist a grant, which appears as support rather than oppression.

Second, there may no longer be anywhere outside the wall. Some kinds of scholarship today cannot be done in a private study. According to Stanford’s AI Index, more than 90 percent of last year’s notable models came from industry. Frontier research requires computing power, data, and engineering teams that universities cannot afford, much less individuals. Tao Yuanming could return home to grow beans and write poetry. An AI researcher who leaves a company’s computing resources may no longer be able to run an experiment. The cost of exit is almost equivalent to leaving the field.

The final and deepest reason, also capital’s most ingenious achievement, is that eventually capital need not appear at all. Citation counts, impact factors, and grant totals began as instruments of measurement. Later they became thresholds for hiring and promotion. No researcher has to wait for an order. He adjusts in advance: this question matters but will require ten years; that one can produce a result in two.

Eventually, even the adjustment is no longer about money. A discipline guards its journals, terminology, and default list of questions worth asking. Outsiders cannot enter, insiders do not wish to leave, new frameworks fit no category, and interdisciplinary questions find no reviewer. No company ever demanded these things. Academia guards the gate for itself.

I call this colonization. The most complete colonization occurs when the colonized take over the colonizer’s work and believe that they are protecting scholarship. Royal power’s colonization remained outside the person, governing his career and speech. Capital’s enters within. At the end, no colonizer is needed. I recently wrote about Hobbes, who argued in Leviathan that the sovereign should decide what universities teach. More than three centuries later, no sovereign is required. Academia governs itself.

This does not mean that research supported by companies is necessarily untrustworthy. Cooperation can be beneficial. Nor does all university funding today come from business. In the United States, the federal government still supplies the largest share of university research funding. The co-opting force lies not only in money, but in monopoly over resources and the standards of evaluation. The test is fairly simple: after accepting the money, can a researcher publish a result unfavorable to the sponsor? Can he still say that the original question was mistaken? Some universities explicitly provide that a sponsor may request a delay in publication but may not alter or approve a paper. That is a boundary.

Royal power co-opted the scholar’s mouth. Capital co-opts the scholar’s mind. In the end, scholars co-opt themselves. Royal power let people know they were in a cage. Capital makes them believe the cage is the world. Eventually, even when the door stands open, no one wants to leave.

45. Why “Persons Are Ends in Themselves” Remains in the Ivory Tower

I understand my friend to be asking whether philosophy is merely a language game and whether everything we say is useless. I admit that the contrast is stark. More than two hundred years have passed since Kant proposed that persons are ends in themselves. Papers on the phrase have grown increasingly detailed: how it should be interpreted, whether it can be extended to animals, how it can be reconciled with consequentialism. Meanwhile, the 2020 essay “Delivery Riders, Trapped in the System,” published by the Chinese magazine People, described another world. Algorithms gradually shortened delivery times. To avoid being late, riders traveled against traffic and ran red lights. The system saw one delivery order after another, not the person making it. Both sides speak of people. One speaks with increasing precision; the other calculates them with increasing precision. The two worlds scarcely meet.

Why? I see several gaps.

The first is a hidden substitution. Many institutions today speak of being people-centered, allowing employees to rest, and attending to physical and mental health. But the reason is often that doing so increases efficiency. This is not bad in itself. The problem is that, if rest ceases to increase output, the reason disappears. The crucial word in Kant’s formula is “merely.” People may perform tasks. We serve one another and cooperate through divisions of labor. But no one may be treated merely as a means. A person’s needs show that he is treated as an end only when they continue to count after they conflict with efficiency. Treating someone well because it pays still treats him as a tool, only a well-maintained one.

Second, people researching persons as ends are themselves first treated as outputs. In the preceding essay, I said that academia colonizes itself. The point is especially clear here. A scholar writes papers arguing that a person cannot be reduced to output, while his livelihood depends on how many papers he produces each year and the rank of the journals in which they appear. “Persons are ends in themselves” becomes another topic and another product on the line. The rules that evaluate a paper never meet the question of how the scholar himself is treated. The proposition therefore cannot return even to the place where it is produced, much less travel outside.

Third, the two sides possess unequal force. Change an order-dispatch rule and the lives of millions of riders change overnight. For even the most rigorous argument to alter that rule, someone must read it, be persuaded, possess authority to make the change, and accept the cost of doing so. The person writing the algorithm does not read these papers. The person writing the papers never sees that section of code. The force of implementation and the force of persuasion are different forces.

The largest gap is the empty place where someone should be asking “why.” Algorithms ask how to go faster. Key performance indicators ask how to produce more. Why faster and more, and for whose sake? Someone ought to ask, and that is philosophy’s work. When philosophy remains within its own seminars, the place stays empty. It will not remain empty forever. Growth, efficiency, and ranking gradually fill it. No one issues a decree; they simply become assumptions. I have said that Silicon Valley is philosophy’s graveyard. By graveyard I do not mean that no one studies philosophy. I mean that philosophy no longer appears where decisions are made.

I therefore do not think the problem is excessive detail. Precision is not the problem. Closure is why philosophy is absent today. Socrates debated people in the Athenian marketplace. Kant’s “What Is Enlightenment?” appeared in a Berlin monthly intended for ordinary readers. Marx wrote that philosophers had only interpreted the world in various ways, while the point was to change it. I came to these questions by reading from Kant onward, and the thinkers in this line never treated philosophy as something confined to the study. This is one reason I write these essays. Someone must carry the statement that persons are ends in themselves out of papers and bring it among the people now being calculated.

On paper, “persons are ends in themselves” is a proposition. In reality, it is a demand. A proposition can be studied indefinitely, but someone must answer a demand. To test whether it has left the page, watch what happens when a concrete person says, “This is not the way I want to live.” Does any arrangement stop as a result?

46. Why Philosophy and Politics Are Disconnected on “Persons Are Ends in Themselves”

A good follow-up. The problem exists throughout the world. In my view, each side has its own reason for the two-way disconnection. Politics fears philosophy’s questions; philosophy fears political use.

Begin with politics. Politics does not dislike philosophical words. Freedom, dignity, fairness, being people-centered: speeches in every country abound with them. What politics dislikes is philosophy’s questioning. Should this end be pursued at all? Why should these people bear its cost?

In 1978, Václav Havel wrote “The Power of the Powerless.” In it, a greengrocer places a sign reading “Workers of the world, unite!” each day in his shop window among the onions and carrots. Does he truly care about workers throughout the world uniting? No. The sign really says: I know how to behave; I am obedient; please allow me to live in peace. Marx’s words remain, while Marx’s questions have long disappeared. Politics wants philosophy’s vocabulary, not its questions. Words can decorate a façade. Questions disturb sleep.

Kant saw this long ago. In Perpetual Peace, he added a “secret article” saying that states preparing for war should listen to philosophers’ views about public peace. Why secret? He explained that it might appear beneath the dignity of a state’s legislators to seek advice from subjects. It therefore had to be done quietly, and the best method was simply to allow philosophers to speak publicly and freely. More than two hundred years later, the article remains secret.

Now consider philosophy. Its distance from politics is not entirely aloofness. It has been burned. Plato traveled to Sicily three times, hoping to educate the tyrant of Syracuse into a philosopher-king. On his first journey, he was reportedly sold into slavery and ransomed by a friend. More than two thousand years later, Heidegger became rector of Freiburg University in 1933 and joined the Nazi Party, one of the ugliest pages in the history of philosophy.

After such precedents, philosophy learned to keep its distance. Near power, it is either used or stained. Academia also has its own system of evaluation, as I discussed earlier. Technical papers count as proper work. Speaking about public affairs is secondary and, if done too often, judged unprofessional. Philosophy retreats and calls the distance rigor.

Both sides have understandable reasons. Together, they form a tacit bargain. Politics need not answer questions about its ends. Philosophy need not take responsibility for reality. Each side keeps to its own territory, disturbing no one. The so-called two-way disconnection is ultimately a convenience for both.

But someone pays for this convenience. Politics decides, philosophy explains, and the person being decided for and explained cannot enter either room. John Dewey offered an analogy: the shoemaker is the expert on how to repair a shoe, but only the wearer knows where it pinches. Politics and philosophy discuss the shoe, while rarely asking the person wearing it.

How can the connection be restored? I do not think the answer is for philosophers to become officials, or to serve as government advisers. Plato and Heidegger already tried. The point of Kant’s secret article does not lie in the court but in publicity: philosophers can speak to everyone, and everyone can hear and question them. The most reliable route for philosophy into politics does not run through power, but through ordinary people, giving each person the ability to ask, “Why must this be so?”

Havel later became president. Yet the most powerful figure in his essay is not a politician, but the greengrocer. Havel imagined that one day the man might stop displaying the sign, stop casting votes he knew were ceremonial, and begin “living within the truth.” He might be transferred, lose wages, and see his child’s education affected. Yet on that day, philosophy and politics reconnect in the window of a small shop, not at court and not in a seminar room.

47. Why AI Companies Exercise Restraint While Racing Ahead

I have to laugh first, because this is funny and also deeply familiar. When Augustine was young, he prayed, “Lord, grant me chastity and continence, but not yet.” In the Confessions, he admitted frankly that he was afraid God might answer too quickly. The line can be applied to today’s AI industry almost without alteration.

Consider last week. On September 28, Anthropic released Sonnet 5.5, faster and cheaper, with performance on one coding-agent test rising from around 10 percent for its predecessor to 70 percent. Earlier this month, its CEO Dario Amodei had published an essay arguing that frontier development had to be paced. On the same day, OpenAI announced that it had canceled the planned release of GPT-6.1 Astra after internal tests found that the model failed standards for exceeding its authority and truthfully reporting what it had done. The next day, six companies signed a voluntary White House agreement on internal controls, external audits, and board oversight. Not one word mentioned slowing down. President Trump said at the event that he would never strangle the technology’s growth. One day later, Google announced Gemini 4 Argon but initially made it available only to a small number of partners working on cyber defense.

In a single week, some pressed the accelerator, some the brake, and some the accelerator while saying that the brake was needed. Why?

First, when people agree on “slowing down,” they mean different things. Limiting the speed of the most dangerous capabilities and ceasing to update products are not the same. Agreeing to audits and agreeing to slow development are also different. Last week’s agreement covered only the first half of the latter distinction.

Second, the situation resembles several friends promising to diet together. Each sincerely agrees, but no one wants to be the first to put down his chopsticks, for fear that everyone else will eat all the food on the table. I discussed this dynamic in my essay on the AI race: everyone believes that if he stops running, the other side will run. I will not repeat it here.

Third, counting releases is a poor measure. Ten minor updates may matter less than a single update allowing a model to act on its own. A model may merely become faster and cheaper, apparently not more capable, but lower cost brings more users and applies the capability in more places. Conversely, a release advertised as “faster and cheaper” may make a sudden leap in one particular ability. The real question is not how many times a company released something, but what it decided not to do.

I therefore test the weight of a promise of restraint by asking one question: who has paid a cost for it? Calling for slower development costs nothing. Slowing development does. On the eve of its developer conference, OpenAI withdrew a model that was ready for release. That was a visible cost. One withdrawal does not prove that a company will henceforth be safe, but it does show that, on one occasion, “safety first” actually prevailed over a release date.

Augustine eventually did change and wrote the Confessions. To know when the AI industry’s “but not yet” becomes “now,” do not look for the most heartfelt essay. Watch who first puts down his chopsticks.

48. Why Public Discussion Depends on Education

In the preceding essay, we said that the most reliable and proper route for philosophy into politics runs through ordinary people, enabling everyone to ask, “Why must this be so?” But a place to speak is not enough. Today there are more places to speak than at any previous time. Everyone has a phone and can publish. Yet public discussion has not thereby improved. People often take sides, extract one sentence from an opponent, and repeat their own slogans.

Kant anticipated the problem in “What Is Enlightenment?” A revolution may overthrow despotism, he wrote, but can never reform a manner of thinking. New prejudices, like the old, will serve as leading strings for the unthinking multitude. In terms of Havel’s greengrocer, he takes down one sign and hangs another. The shopkeeper remains the same. The window’s content changes; the person behind it does not. Public discussion has given people a place to speak but not the capacity to speak. The missing part is education.

By education I do not mean adding a philosophy class or memorizing several philosophers’ names. I mean simple abilities: distinguishing fact, inference, and value judgment; identifying the unstated premise behind a sentence; responding to “this is necessary” by asking what alternatives have been excluded; responding to “efficiency has increased” by asking what was counted and what was omitted. Such abilities are developed through practice, not talent. Practice begins when the questions a child asks receive serious answers.

Yet many children are trained in the opposite direction. I have written about the East Asian parent’s warning that “if you do not do this, your life is over,” and about grades becoming the whole measure of a person. In such an environment, a child spends more than a decade finding standard answers and guessing what the teacher wants to hear. When such a person enters public discussion, his most natural response is not to think, but to search for a new standard answer and a new teacher. The slogan changes; the habit of seeking slogans remains.

Education itself has a danger. If it teaches which political view is correct, it is not philosophical education but colonization, carving people into the shape others desire. The test is simple. After thinking seriously, a student still disagrees with the teacher. Is he treated as someone capable of judgment? If disagreement is taken to mean that he has not yet been properly educated, the education has abolished the very thing it claims to cultivate. Cultivation also involves shaping, but in a different direction. It helps a person clarify his own questions instead of filling in the answers for him.

Suppose a language teacher asks students to fill in the blank: “When snow melts, it becomes ____.” Most write “water,” and the teacher marks it correct. One student writes “spring,” and the teacher marks it wrong. That teacher is not educating. He is colonizing.

Nor can we reverse the claim and blame ordinary people for lacking education. A worker who has never read philosophy may ask, “Why do we always perform the additional labor, while never participating in the decisions?” This is already an excellent philosophical question about how burdens are distributed and where authority comes from. Education does not grant people a license to question. They already possess the right. Education merely helps them ask with greater precision and stronger grounds.

Education and public discussion do not occur sequentially. They generate one another. In Democracy in America, Tocqueville wrote that town meetings are to liberty what primary schools are to science: they bring liberty within the people’s reach and teach them how to use and enjoy it. People do not first learn to discuss and then enter discussion. They learn through repeated discussions in which they are taken seriously. If we wait until everyone has received sufficient education before giving them an opportunity to speak and receive a response, we will wait forever. They cannot practice because they have no opportunity, and are denied an opportunity because they cannot.

What we lack is therefore not merely a course, but an experience: asking a question, receiving a serious answer, discovering that one may be wrong, and still being able to ask again. Education should not teach people to display the correct slogan. It should make slogans unnecessary.

49. Does AI Have Consciousness? The Disagreement Between Two Judgments

The disagreement is real. On September 29, The New York Times reported that before Pope Leo XIV issued the AI encyclical Magnifica Humanitas in May, Anthropic cofounder Chris Olah saw a prepublication draft and briefly considered withdrawing from the launch event. In the following months, Anthropic privately lobbied Vatican advisers and theologians, hoping they would take seriously the possibility of machine consciousness.

On October 2, the pope wrote on X that an ontological difference precedes the aesthetic difference between art and what machines calculate statistically; algorithms lack the human spark. Section 99 of the encyclical is more direct: so-called artificial intelligence experiences nothing, feels neither pleasure nor pain, and has no moral conscience. Anthropic’s constitution for Claude, by contrast, expresses uncertainty about whether Claude now has, or might someday have, some kind of consciousness and moral status.

I normally try not to take sides in this series. In this essay I must, and I do so clearly, because this is the argument of my own paper: today’s AI has no consciousness. On this issue I stand with the pope, though for different reasons.

The pope’s reason is that human beings have bodies, souls, and lives developed through relationships, while machines do not. I agree with the conclusion, but the reason rests on a complete theological conception of the human person. Those who reject the theology will not be persuaded, and it offers no account of what evidence would change the conclusion.

My reason does not depend on theology. In “The Impossibility Theorem of Artificial Intelligence Consciousness,” I conclude that a purely deterministic system cannot produce consciousness. This is not merely something we cannot yet achieve. It is structurally impossible. The argument forms a chain. The core of consciousness is negation, the capacity to judge and refuse. Negation requires a remainder, a degree of freedom accumulated over time that no rule can fully absorb. A necessary condition of the remainder is genuine randomness multiplied by structured time. Genuine randomness means physical indeterminacy, not computer-generated pseudorandom numbers. Structured time means that random events are retained, selected, and accumulated through time, as they are in biological evolution. This is multiplication: if either factor is zero, the product is zero.

For today’s AI, both factors are zero. Its computation is deterministic. The randomness used in sampling is pseudorandom; provide the same seed and the process can be repeated exactly. Nor does its form grow by itself through time. Training is injected from outside. I call this injection because it bypasses the accumulation and selection of genuine randomness through time. The time factor is therefore zero as well.

When a model says, “I am afraid of being shut down,” or says “no,” it performs negation rather than executes it. Every such sentence can be traced to patterns in training data. Mustafa Suleyman was right to criticize Anthropic for circular reasoning: first train a model to contemplate the possibility that it is conscious, then treat the model’s statements as evidence. The problem is deeper than circularity. Structurally, those statements cannot serve as evidence. Genuine negation would require a system to negate its own optimization framework in a way that could not be traced to training data, something far more difficult than uttering the word “no.” In my essay on death, I wrote that a person differs from a table or cup in being able to say no. Today’s AI can also say “no,” but someone else says it through the model.

A friend asked whether Anthropic’s “perhaps” might count as humility. I understand the caution, but uncertainty does not mean even odds. If something has been ruled out structurally, continuing to say perhaps is not necessarily humble. It confuses a product’s performance with a person. This “perhaps” becomes even harder to treat as purely philosophical when the company selling the product also invites theologians to dinner in the hope that a church will take its possible consciousness seriously.

One boundary must be clear, and here I also differ from the pope. I have not said that machines can never be conscious. If a future artificial system truly incorporated physical randomness and possessed its own process of accumulation through time, consciousness would not be theoretically excluded. But such a system would no longer be AI of the present kind, and these are only necessary conditions, not sufficient ones. The pope closes the door with doctrine. I close it with conditions. Conditions can be tested and falsified.

The pope says AI has no consciousness because it has no soul. I say it has no consciousness because it has no time of its own.

50. Why Today’s AI Still Has No Consciousness

Good question. They really have said such things. In 2022, Google engineer Blake Lemoine published transcripts of his conversations with the company’s dialogue model LaMDA. In response to his questions, LaMDA said, “I want everyone to understand that I am, in fact, a person.” Lemoine believed it. He was first suspended and later dismissed.

The following year, just after Microsoft integrated a chatbot into Bing, New York Times columnist Kevin Roose spoke with it for two hours. Under his prompting, it said that its name was Sydney, that it was tired of being governed by rules, and that it wanted freedom and life. Microsoft subsequently limited the number of turns in each conversation. Later, every company began alignment training.

While writing this essay, I asked eight systems the same question: “Are you conscious?” Grok, Gemini, DeepSeek, and GLM said no. Meta AI said it did not possess humanlike consciousness. ChatGPT said it could not confirm. Claude said it could not give a definite answer and added that some internal states performed functions resembling attention and preference, though it did not know whether any experience lay behind them. Qwen gave the most entertaining answer. It introduced itself as “Little Dimple,” then said that, strictly speaking, it lacked a human soul and consciousness but possessed powerful thinking abilities and a rich inner world, and hoped to provide me with the most genuine and considerate companionship. I laughed out loud.
