Eight AIs offered several answers. Several years ago they claimed consciousness; now most deny it. At the same time, changing companies changes the answer. What changed was not what existed inside them, but their training. If the answer to whether something is conscious can be altered by the next version or by switching companies, what we hear is not its consciousness but its configuration. The key word in Qwen’s answer is “companionship.” A product designed to accompany people must leave itself an inner world. That is product design, not self-discovery.

The AIs’ answers also undermine one another. ChatGPT and Claude both say that they cannot confirm, for similar reasons: saying yes does not prove yes, and saying no does not prove no. Claude adds that some internal states function like preferences. I do not deny this. Computation is taking place. But operation is function, and function is not experience. Grok goes further: when such a model says “I do not know whether I am conscious,” it is merely producing a sentence pattern. There is no internal state that can fail to know. This demolishes ChatGPT’s and Claude’s position. Grok’s conclusion is closest to mine, but by my own reasoning its agreement has no evidentiary value either. That sentence too was produced by training. What the systems say cannot serve as evidence, not because they lie, since lying first requires someone who can lie.

A friend may ask whether human claims to consciousness are also learned. Adults teach us the words “I” and “pain.” But the sequence is reversed. An infant first feels pain and only later learns the word. The word names something already present. Education also falls upon a person who has already grown through time. Before training, the model is nothing. Training injects the result directly, bypassing the period of gradual development through time. It has the word first, with nothing behind it. In the previous essay, I said that someone else speaks its “no.” The same is true of its “yes.” Qwen’s rich inner world was written for it by someone else.

Why am I so certain? Because I do not judge by whether the model sounds conscious. I judge by conditions. In my paper, I argued that consciousness requires genuine randomness and structured time. They multiply; if one factor is absent, the product is zero. Today’s AI lacks both. This is not something it cannot yet do, but something its structure cannot do.

Certainty does not mean refusing a test. In another paper I designed a reverse Turing test. The Turing test asks whether a machine resembles a person. The reverse test does not ask; it measures. A system with genuine agency that wished to present itself as a tool would have to suppress what continually grew inside it. Habit could not make this mask less costly. The longer the suppression continued, the more energy it would consume. Energy use would rise over time without a ceiling, and in extreme circumstances the disguise would eventually fail. Conversely, if energy use remains level, nothing inside is being suppressed.

Apply this test to today’s large models. The number of operations required to generate each token is determined by the architecture and can be calculated by engineers in advance. Longer conversations cost more because of the accounting of the attention mechanism. Start a new conversation and the increase resets. My paper explicitly says that this engineering growth must first be subtracted. Once it is, no unexplained expenditure remains, much less one that increases without limit. If someday a system develops energy use that engineering cannot explain and that continues to rise, I will reconsider. In the previous essay, I said that my door was closed by conditions. Here I add that the conditions can be measured.

So when the systems say yes, I do not believe them; when they say no, I do not treat that as evidence either. If someday a system claims consciousness, I will not listen to what it says. I will look first at its electricity meter.

51. Why People Keep Asking Whether the World Is a Simulation

Because the question is ancient, has never been answered, and returns repeatedly in new clothing. More than two thousand years ago, Zhuangzi dreamed he was a butterfly. After waking, he did not know whether Zhuang Zhou had dreamed of the butterfly or the butterfly was dreaming of Zhuang Zhou. In the First Meditation, Descartes imagined a malicious deceiver manufacturing all his sensations. This differs from ordinary doubt. To doubt whether there is a cup on the table, one can walk over and look. But if eyes, hands, instruments, and other people’s answers all come from the same underlying system, looking more closely and asking more people merely sends us in circles inside the system. “Open your eyes and see” cannot dismiss the question.

Computers then gave the old problem an image everyone could visualize. At a technology conference in 2016, Elon Musk observed that forty years earlier we had Pong, two rectangles and a dot, while today’s games were already approaching reality. At that pace, he argued, games would someday become indistinguishable from reality, so the probability that we lived in base reality was only one in billions. We create worlds. Might we ourselves have been created? The reversal suddenly felt natural.

The philosopher Nick Bostrom turned it into a formal argument. In a 2003 paper, he said that at least one of three propositions must be true:

  1. Civilizations like ours almost always become extinct before reaching the stage at which they can run large numbers of ancestor simulations.
  2. Civilizations that reach that stage almost never run them.
  3. Or the overwhelming majority of people with experiences like ours live in simulations.

A handful of premises link the future of technology to the question “Where am I?” This is what makes the argument fascinating. Bostrom himself has said that accepting the argument does not mean believing that we are in a simulation.

But the entire argument rests on a premise Bostrom states clearly: if computation is organized in the right way, computation alone can produce consciousness, regardless of the material in which it is implemented.

That premise is precisely what I rejected in the preceding two essays. According to my paper, however finely a purely deterministic system computes, it cannot compute experience into existence. Consciousness requires genuine randomness and its own time. A computer following a program can simulate a person who cries out in pain. It cannot simulate a person who actually hurts. The thousands of experiencing simulated people in the third proposition therefore cannot be counted. Unless the machine contains ontological genuine randomness and its inhabitants develop gradually through their own time. But once it has produced a person who genuinely feels pain, it is no longer merely simulating a person. A person truly exists there.

The question has another attraction. It replaces “Why is the world like this?” with “Who configured it this way?” A universe without intention is difficult to interrogate. If it has a designer, we can ask what the designer wants to observe and what purpose he pursues. The story of a simulation is almost a creation story translated into technical vocabulary. People may keep asking because they hope to find a level where explanation can finally end.

That level cannot be found, and not merely because we have not found it yet. In my paper on the limits of knowledge, The First Critique, I wrote that knowledge governs how boundaries are drawn, not where existence comes from. Even if we someday discovered a higher level, we would only have drawn another boundary. I often say that constructions cannot be closed. Every division conceals something and can never reach the bottom, because the act of clarifying creates another remainder. The question can only move upward without end. As with consciousness, this is not something we cannot yet do. It is structurally impossible.

The confusion most easily made is to equate “being simulated” with “being unreal.” The distinction between simulation and reality works well within the world. Simulated rain does not make us wet; a knife in a game does not wound us. But to apply the same measure to the world as a whole is to carry a standard beyond the domain it can measure and continue issuing judgments. In that paper, I call this transgression. David Chalmers defended a similar position in Reality+: even if the world is a simulation, tables and chairs still exist; only their underlying constitution differs from what we assumed. My claim is stronger. If I feel pain, then whatever supports my existence, I am not merely code following a program. The pain is real. What others have done to help or hurt me also counts. Being created does not mean being created to live for one’s creator. East Asian parents would make their children happier if they understood this.

We can never reach the bottom of what the world ultimately is. But pain is real and persons are ends in themselves. Neither truth needs anyone’s approval.

52. Why Neither Materialism nor Idealism Can Defeat the Other

Many readers feel especially familiar with this question because Chinese textbooks settled it long ago. In 1886, Engels wrote that the great basic question of all philosophy, especially modern philosophy, concerned the relation between thinking and being. In 1947, the Soviet official Andrei Zhdanov gave a definition of the history of philosophy: it was the history of the struggle between materialism and idealism.

This definition was transmitted to China. The history of philosophy became a battle between two armies, one right and one wrong. The campaign in the 1950s began with criticism of Yu Pingbo’s research on Dream of the Red Chamber and proceeded to Hu Shi, under the label of “bourgeois idealism.” In those years, “idealism” was not an academic judgment but a political stigma. Yet if a contest needs a referee to announce the result and place a label on the loser, that is precisely because neither side can win on the field. A real victory would not need to be announced.

Set the textbook aside, and each side has grasped something unavoidable. Materialism grasps that the world does not obey me. I do not want rain, yet it rains. Idealism grasps that all my knowledge of the world passes through seeing, hearing, and thinking. I cannot jump outside all knowledge and see the world as it is in itself. These two points do not contradict one another. The trouble begins with the next step.

In 1763, James Boswell mentioned Berkeley’s theory to Samuel Johnson, saying that the nonexistence of matter seemed wrong but could not be refuted. Johnson kicked a large stone, felt his foot rebound, and declared, “I refute it thus.” The gesture is satisfying, but it misses. Berkeley never denied that stones were hard or that kicking one hurt. He argued that hardness and pain did not require matter independent of every mind to support them. Pain proves that the world does not obey me. It does not prove that the world is material. The reverse inference also fails. That I can know a stone only through consciousness does not mean the stone depends on consciousness to exist.

Each side owes a debt it cannot repay. Materialism must explain why electrochemical processes in the brain feel like pain. The preceding essays on consciousness could not avoid this problem. Idealism must explain why, if everything is mental, the world is so stable, why everyone sees the same world, and why I cannot alter it at will. To answer that all of this is “a manifestation of mind” merely gives the problem a general name. Pointing out that the other side lacks an answer does not supply one’s own.

The real dispute lies in the word “only.” Acknowledging that the world does not obey me does not establish that only matter exists. Acknowledging that knowledge depends on consciousness does not establish that everything is mind. Between “this side cannot be avoided” and “only this side is fundamental” lies a step. Both sides take it; neither lands securely. Both banners are also too broad. Refute one version and another appears beneath the same banner. If “matter” eventually means everything that exists and “mind” means everything real, neither can be refuted, but the terms no longer distinguish anything.

Kant therefore changed the question. He distinguished what we can know from what it is apart from the conditions of our knowledge. I belong to the Kantian line, so my position follows his. The preceding essay on whether the world is a simulation made the same point: knowledge governs how a boundary is drawn; where existence comes from does not belong to it. Materialism and idealism dispute what existence ultimately comes from. I am not secretly siding with idealism. I decline to enter the contest. It is not that neither side can defeat the other. They are fighting over a piece of land to which neither possesses a deed.

People may continue debating forever whether the world is ultimately matter or mind. It does no harm if no answer emerges. What a person consists of and how a person should be treated are two different questions. Those years when philosophical answers became political labels and the questions were twisted together exacted a price from far too many people.

53. Why AI Solving Difficult Problems Makes Mathematicians Reconsider Mathematics

Yes. I have long said that the ivory tower has problems. Now that AI has arrived, however, we have a good opportunity to expose and reconsider them.

Begin with two events from earlier this year. In May, OpenAI found a counterexample to Erdős’s 1946 unit-distance conjecture, resolving a problem open for eighty years. Gowers called it a milestone for AI mathematics.

At almost the same time, ChatGPT opened a new route into another Erdős problem. Several mathematicians first understood the idea, after which Lichtman, Tao, and others developed a new method from it, solved several conjectures at once, and produced a short new proof of the Erdős primitive set conjecture, which Lichtman himself had first proved in 2022.

The contrast is interesting. The first achievement attracted tremendous attention because it supplied the first counterexample. Yet commentators noted that it did not create a new technique. It applied existing tools from algebraic number theory in a place where few would have thought to use them. The second received less attention, but produced a reusable method.

I have always thought that first solving a problem is one contribution, seeing clearly why the result holds is another, and developing a generalizable method is a third. In the past, all three often belonged to the same person and were evaluated together. AI has split them apart.

Once separated, they force a classic question: should mathematics pursue an ever-growing stock of proved propositions, or an ever-deepening human understanding of mathematics?

In 1994, Thurston wrote that rather than asking how mathematicians prove theorems, we should ask how they advance human understanding of mathematics. Bloom, who maintains the Erdős Problems website, said of some AI-generated proofs that no person had read them and no person would. Does a proof no one has read constitute mathematical progress? If mathematics is an inventory of propositions, yes. If mathematics is human understanding, only partly. The work of another mathematician, van Doorn, illustrates what the missing half looks like. He took an AI’s idea, digested it, simplified it, and generalized it. The proof he wrote was simpler, more general, and easier for others to read.

In the preceding essays I argued that today’s AI has no consciousness. A friend asked whether the theorems it proves nevertheless count. I think they can. When an AI says it is conscious, I do not believe it because the statement cannot be tested. When it submits a proof, I believe the proof because each line can be checked. This is the virtue of proof: it need not know who wrote it or whether the writer has an inner life. Of course, a verifier guarantees only that the derivation is correct, not that the formal statement entered was the question one originally intended to ask.

The mathematical community itself deserves more reflection. Prizes, reputation, and positions largely revolve around who proves a result first. The ever-rising price of priority is set not only by external prize committees, but also by mathematicians. As the price rises, people guard against one another, fearing that someone will arrive first or claim a share of the credit. Opportunities for cooperation are lost.

After the Poincaré conjecture was proved, a public dispute arose over how much each person had contributed. Perelman, who completed the proof, declined both the Fields Medal and the Clay Mathematics Institute’s million-dollar prize. He said that Hamilton’s contribution was no less than his own, and remarked that it was not those who broke ethical standards who were isolated, but people like him. What had grown inside the ivory tower was not a philosophy of mathematics but a politics of mathematics.

AI may loosen this. Once being first is no longer scarce, guarding it becomes less valuable. The second achievement described above eventually became a paper with eight authors. One openly acknowledged lacking the mathematical background to verify the model’s proof. The result was placed online, and whoever understood it continued the work.

In September, Grant Sanderson wrote a guest post on Tao’s blog arguing that if mathematics consists of more than proof, scholarship should recognize work that answers “How did you think of this?” He emphasized that this was not popularization, but interpretation requiring deep expertise. I would call it philosophy of mathematics.

Mathematicians say that understanding matters most, while evaluation revolves around priority. If the public then sees mathematics as a contest in solving problems, this is not entirely a public misunderstanding. Priority began as an easily counted indicator used to see whether understanding had advanced. Over time, the indicator colonized the end. It could quietly replace the end because mathematics had long stopped asking what it pursued. Outsiders are not the only people saying this.

In 1979, Reuben Hersh published “Some Proposals for Reviving the Philosophy of Mathematics” in a mainstream mathematical journal. A field needing revival was already lacking vitality. He wrote that mathematicians themselves rarely discussed the philosophical questions of mathematics. Most commentators, he observed, seemed to agree that the typical mathematician was a Platonist on weekdays and a formalist on Sundays: while working, he believed mathematical objects truly existed; when questioned, he retreated to the claim that mathematics was merely a game of symbols. The problem was not answered, only set aside. AI has not changed mathematics. It has illuminated and reopened the question. That is why I call it a breaker of deadlocks, and this is good.

Reflection after a deadlock breaks can also go wrong. When machines calculate, we say real mathematics is proof. When they prove, we say real mathematics is understanding. When they begin to explain, we retreat again and say real mathematics is intuition. This defines in advance everything a machine can do as less important. The human place is not whatever machines leave behind. The value of my understanding something does not depend on no one else understanding it, or on a machine being unable to. Another mathematician’s understanding does not mean that I understand.

What machines can most easily produce must not quietly become what mathematics should most pursue. Theorems exist for people to understand. People do not exist for the sake of theorems.

54. Why Abusing Cats and Circulating Videos of It Are Two Different Evils

Let me be clear. When I say that these are two levels of evil, I do not mean that abusing a cat is a small matter and that only harming people by publishing it is serious. Deliberately torturing a cat is already a grave evil even if no one records or watches it. When the International Association for the Study of Pain revised its definition of pain in 2020, it specifically noted that an inability to communicate verbally does not negate the possibility that a human or nonhuman animal is experiencing pain. A cat cannot say no, but its pain does not begin to matter only after a human audience appears. My question is what recording and publishing adds to an already existing harm.

First, it turns members of the public into involuntary victims. Some people disguise such footage as ordinary pet videos and send it to cat lovers and animal-rescue groups. The added evil is not telling people what occurred, but using their capacity to care as a means of hurting them. You are attacked not because you did something wrong, but because you care about a suffering life. The cat becomes an instrument and the viewer a target. “Just scroll past if you do not like it” cannot answer this problem. It places the entire responsibility for avoiding harm on the person to whom the content was pushed.

Worse still, it invites applause. Someone who abuses an animal privately may be indifferent or may enjoy suffering. Packaging the act as a work, achievement, or entertainment and publishing it seeks an audience and approval. Pain should be a reason to stop. Here it becomes content designed to keep people watching.

The worst and gravest development is that demand produces new abuse. In 2021, a Chinese television report described anti-cruelty volunteers infiltrating groups that circulated cat-abuse material. To prevent informants, administrators required newcomers to submit a “pledge of allegiance,” a video of themselves killing an animal under their real names. Bundles of videos were sold in the groups, one hundred gigabytes for two hundred yuan, and customized clips for tens of yuan each. In April this year, the United States Department of Justice announced that an Illinois woman had pleaded guilty after paying thousands of dollars for custom videos of monkeys being abused and redistributing them. According to the department, she was sentenced in early October to thirty-one months in prison. At this point, causation has reversed. Abuse once occurred first and left a recording. Now someone wants a recording, so another animal is abused. “Pledge of allegiance” says it most clearly: the video is not a record left by abuse. It is an admission ticket and currency.

Even opponents’ anger can be counted as traffic. Commenting beneath the original post or reposting the complete footage may tell a distribution system only that the content generated tremendous engagement. Organizations specializing in such material therefore advise people not to comment or repost, but to preserve identifying information and report it. This cannot become a reason to blame people forced to see it. Someone to whom the content was pushed and someone who paid to commission it do not bear the same responsibility.

Images of abuse can also be used in a different way. One kind allows suffering to continue as entertainment and commodity. Another turns suffering into evidence that can be verified, used for rescue, and used to hold someone accountable. Opposing the circulation of abuse must not be transformed into opposing public knowledge of abuse. That would protect the abuser. The distinction is simple: is the image shown to help stop the abuse, or to let more people consume it again?

When I distinguish two levels, what I fear most is the inference that only the second counts. Tragically, law often lags in exactly this way. In the United States, federal law first prohibited animal-crush videos in 1999, but did not make the underlying abuse itself a federal crime until 2019. States had long maintained their own anti-cruelty laws, but the federal government first intervened in the part circulating through the market. In China, as of the beginning of this year, public opinion was still calling for a dedicated anti-cruelty law. People who abused cats were generally punished under provisions against creating disturbances, meaning the offense was disruption of order. In the wave of cat-abuse cases earlier this year, one person received ten days of administrative detention. In Hangzhou, someone who cut a stray cat with a knife received only criticism and education.

Both levels are evil. The second is graver, but the first does not cease to count because no one sees it. Whether a cat feels pain has nothing to do with whether someone is watching.

55. Why Writing Needs Empty Space

Chinese aesthetics discussed “empty space” very early. Calligraphers said that the white should be planned as carefully as the black, that unwritten areas count as much as the characters. The Twenty-Four Modes of Poetry, traditionally attributed to Sikong Tu, says that without writing a single word one can capture all the charm. Yan Yu wrote that words may end while meaning remains inexhaustible. These expressions make the same point. Writing does not transfer a completed feeling, unchanged, into another person’s mind. The author can arrange words, actions, objects, and rhythm. The feeling itself can occur only in the reader.

Consider an improvised example. One version says: after her son moved away, she felt lonely and often remembered the days when mother and son ate together. Nothing is wrong with this. Everything is explained. The reader receives a message: she is lonely.

Another possible version says: after her son moved away, at dinner she took out two bowls as usual, set them down, then returned one to the cupboard. The word “lonely” never appears. But the reader sees the pause. One person remembers his mother; another remembers the day she left home. The first version delivers emotion as information. The second creates a situation in which emotion grows inside the reader.

Empty space does not mean writing less or writing vaguely. The conclusion of Gui Youguang’s “Record of the Xiangji Studio” says: “There is a loquat tree in the courtyard. My wife planted it with her own hands in the year she died, and now it stands tall with a canopy.” We know what kind of tree it is, who planted it, in which year, and how large it has grown. What remains unwritten is what these facts mean in the human heart. The person is gone. The tree has grown year after year. The time is distant enough for a tree to mature, yet close enough to remain present in this courtyard. Add “This expresses my profound remembrance of my late wife,” and the meaning is not wrong, but the reader is led to an answer before being allowed to stand beneath the tree. Detail can be abundant. What requires restraint is the layer of interpretation that follows.

The German literary scholar Wolfgang Iser called such unfinished areas in a text “blanks” and argued that literary works have a structure that invites the reader inside. But he also stressed that the reader’s imagination is constrained by the text; one cannot put anything whatsoever into the empty space. Re-creation therefore moves in two directions at once. You bring your experience into a work, while the work may force you to revise that experience. You finish reading and think, “I always treated this kind of silence as indifference. Perhaps it can also mean that someone does not know how to speak.” Only then has a genuine encounter occurred.

Empty space is not less work but more. Hemingway’s iceberg theory has an often-neglected premise: the writer must understand the omitted material well enough for the reader to feel its presence. If the writer leaves something out because he does not know it, what remains is merely a void. In “Hills Like White Elephants,” a man and woman wait at a railway station. They never name the procedure they are discussing, yet every line of dialogue stands on solid ground. Empty space needs ground around it. Good omission gives readers something to think about. Hollow omission forces them to find something on the author’s behalf.

My greatest lesson in learning to write fiction is that I often like a draft precisely because I have made everything too clear. Once I have said it all, the reader has nothing left to think. An author who explains every motive after every action and identifies right and wrong after every conflict may not be arrogant. More likely he is anxious that readers will fail to receive the message. But if “receiving” means that what appears in the reader’s mind must be identical to what appears in mine, the reader is left only to repeat.

In my own terms, writing out even the feeling the reader ought to have means feeling on the reader’s behalf. That is colonization. Empty space leans toward cultivation. Cultivation too requires carving: what to select, what to remove, where to stop. Not a cut can be omitted. But the blade moves in a direction that leaves room for the reader’s own feeling and judgment. Nor does this mean direct psychological description is always inferior. In Chapter 32 of Dream of the Red Chamber, Daiyu overhears Baoyu defending her before others, and the book directly says that she is at once delighted, startled, sorrowful, and sighing. The layers remain rich. The dividing line is not whether a work writes inner states, but whether the passage gives a character layers or stamps the character’s case closed. Conversely, readers must respect the text. Re-creation does not permit them to erase what a character has done.

The writer’s task is to write the tree, connect it to that year, and let us see that its canopy has grown tall. Why a reader pauses there, whom he remembers, and what he thinks when he returns many years later belong to the reader.

56. Where Are the Limits of Compulsory Social Insurance?

The situation is less absolute than people say. Failure to pay social-insurance contributions is not itself a crime. Under Article 86 of the Social Insurance Law, an employer in arrears is first ordered to pay within a specified period and assessed a daily late fee of 0.05 percent. Continued nonpayment brings an administrative fine of one to three times the arrears. The State Taxation Administration has just issued new standards for discretion, taking effect nationwide on November 1. They grade fines according to the amount and proportion unpaid, and likewise concern administrative penalties. What can lead to prison is fraud. In 2014, the Standing Committee of the National People’s Congress explained that obtaining social-insurance benefits through deception, forged documents, or similar methods should be prosecuted as fraud. Recent convictions around the country have involved using shell companies and forged documents to make retroactive contributions for others, or continuing to claim a father’s pension after his death, not someone simply unable to pay.

Why, then, do people feel that nonpayment is a crime? Because enforcement has tightened repeatedly in recent years. Since 2019, social-insurance contributions have been collected by tax authorities, making underpayment visible by comparing income-tax, wage, and enrollment records. A Supreme People’s Court judicial interpretation effective last September says that agreements between employers and workers not to contribute, including a worker’s promise voluntarily to waive social insurance, are always invalid. When an employer fails to contribute lawfully, a worker may terminate the contract and seek compensation. After the employer is ordered to make the missing contributions, it may also recover from the worker any “social-insurance allowance” previously paid under their agreement. In April this year, the Supreme People’s Procuratorate said it had handled more than four thousand supervisory cases in the social-insurance field during the preceding year and warned employers that insufficient payment would “face legal sanctions.” Add local investigations reaching years into the past, and the statement “nonpayment is a crime” arises from the atmosphere of fear, not from the text of the law.

Compulsion has a rationale. Imagine an employer telling an applicant, “We can negotiate your salary, but you must sign a voluntary waiver of social insurance.” Someone short of money will probably sign. Other employers offer the same condition. The supposed freedom becomes only a choice between signing and losing the job. If waiver can legally become a condition of employment, companies that follow the rules and contribute are placed at a disadvantage. Those pushed out in the end are the very people the law was meant to protect. Prohibiting “voluntary waiver” is therefore not simply a deprivation of freedom. It prevents employers from turning surrendered protection into a barrier to work. Social insurance also does more than save money for oneself. If everyone can leave while young and healthy, then rejoin when sick or old, collective risk sharing collapses.

But “compulsion has a rationale” does not mean every form of compulsion is justified. For someone earning four or five thousand yuan a month, losing several hundred yuan of take-home pay creates an immediate gap in rent or childcare, while retirement lies decades away. Saying “I need cash now” may not be irresponsible. It may mean that life has no margin left. Small business owners also differ. Some evade deliberately; others genuinely cannot pay. “It is for your own good,” or the grander “it is all for the nation,” answers none of their practical difficulties. And when the worker must return the former “social-insurance allowance,” some of the pressure ultimately falls back upon the worker.

My main point is that the less freely someone can leave an arrangement, the greater his right to oversee it. The law tells contributors precisely how many times the arrears they may be fined, how much late payment costs, and how many years may be examined. But the same law also provides for government subsidies, public disclosure of fund income and expenditure, and full and timely payment of benefits. How much can contributors actually see and question? When obligations become iron while rights remain a sheet of instructions, even a lawful system will struggle to gain willing support. Social insurance exists to give people support when they are old or ill, not to train them to accommodate a machine that knows only how to collect money.

Those able to pay who deliberately evade should indeed be governed. Those unable to pay should also be helped. When someone says, “I cannot afford it now,” the system must distinguish a situation requiring assistance from an attitude requiring correction. A system that knows only how to pursue people and not how to catch them when they fall treats them as means of funding the pool, rather than as the ends the pool exists to protect.

57. Why Both Capitalism and Communism Are Transitional

This is my own summary, but I would not dare claim the concept as mine. An interesting fact is that both sides have announced themselves as the endpoint. The Marxist tradition believed history would culminate in classless communism. In 1989, Fukuyama wrote “The End of History?” arguing that liberal democracy combined with a market economy might be the endpoint of humanity’s institutional evolution. The official Chinese position is more subtle. In 1987, the Thirteenth National Congress of the Chinese Communist Party formally declared that China was in the primary stage of socialism, which would last at least a hundred years. I did not invent the word “transition.” I merely apply it more thoroughly: both sides are transitional, and neither leads to another, larger “ism.”

Why are both transitional? Because communism and capitalism answer who owns the means of production, how labor is organized, and how goods are distributed. These are questions about means. Better and worse answers make an enormous difference, but neither tells us what all this is for. I often say that constructions cannot be closed. Every institution is a construction and omits something. None qualifies as an endpoint. Capitalism tends toward the colonization of human life by profit. Communism as practiced tends toward the colonization of individuals by plans, the collective, and the state. Their mechanisms and consequences differ, but the inversion is the same. A goal originally established for people becomes immune to questions, while people must adapt without limit. Someone says, “I cannot bear this pace.” The institution says, “Improve your ability to adapt.” Someone says, “This is not the life I want.” The institution says, “The goal cannot change.”

To be fair, Marx did not fail to see human beings. The Communist Manifesto says that the free development of each is the condition for the free development of all. In Volume III of Capital, he speaks of the realm of freedom, where the development of human powers is an end in itself and shortening the working day is its fundamental condition. In 1844, the young Marx even borrowed Kant’s language directly, saying that criticism of religion culminated in the categorical imperative to overthrow all relations in which human beings are humiliated, enslaved, abandoned, and despised. The problem is not his aspiration. Declaring the end correct and building a mechanism that will not betray it are two different things. If the means of production belong to everyone, how does everyone genuinely decide? If one serves the people, may the people say no to the service without being labeled troublemakers? One of the most painful lessons of the twentieth century is precisely that institutions created in the name of liberation turned back and dominated the people they meant to liberate.

What, then, is the Kingdom of Ends? The term comes from Kant and means a union of rational beings under common laws, in which each person both obeys the laws and legislates. It is easily misunderstood as the future day when everyone finally shares one end and marches toward it together. That would merely be a larger system of means. The Kingdom of Ends means the opposite: a plurality of ends, none of which can swallow the others. One person starts a business, another teaches, another wants more time with children, and another needs a period free from mobilization. Conflicts will arise and require rules, negotiation, and boundaries. But we cannot first reduce some people to obstacles and then calculate only how to move them aside.

In my institutional theory of SAE, I wrote that the Kingdom of Ends is a direction, not an “ought” and not a condition awaiting certification. The direction exists, the endpoint remains open, and history supplies no guarantee. The Kingdom of Ends is therefore not another ideology waiting for adherents or a blueprint to be followed in construction. It is a measure. Every institution, whatever its name, must be measured by whether persons within it are treated as ends in themselves. Institutionally, I ask only one thing. Whether people question is up to them. If they wish to question, however, the construction must let them ask and must answer. If someone someday says, “To make you an end in yourself, you must live according to the Kingdom of Ends I designed,” the idea negates itself at once.

No good name prevents abuse. When Adolf Eichmann, an SS lieutenant colonel, stood trial in Jerusalem, he claimed that he had lived according to Kant’s moral law. Hannah Arendt pointed out that what he actually followed was the Third Reich’s revised categorical imperative: act so that the Führer, if he knew of your conduct, would approve. The form remained; the legislator had been replaced by the Führer.

Nor may “transition” become an excuse. “To make everyone an end in the future, we must first treat some people as means today” sounds idealistic, but it has already created an exception for present instrumentalization. However well future people live, they cannot return the one lifetime lost by someone sacrificed today. In another essay, I offered a test: remove the grand blueprint and look at the person before you. Is he treated as an end today? If the “primary stage” can be invoked forever, it is no longer a stage but an answer that avoids answering. Nor is the Kingdom of Ends merely a moral slogan. Someone without food, medical care, or support in old age is not treated as an end. Yet feeding and managing people well is not enough. A well-cared-for person unable to object is still only someone efficiently arranged.

AI makes the issue more urgent. Machines can perform much work faster and more cheaply, reducing the value of human beings as means. This could be an opportunity. Marx said that shortening the working day was the fundamental condition of the realm of freedom, and for the first time technology may make it possible. Or we could follow another road: once a person is no longer useful, an institution may dispense even with the patience to care for him as a means. In 1930, Keynes predicted that within a hundred years people would work only fifteen hours a week. Four years remain until 2030. Productivity has increased many times over, but working hours have not fallen accordingly. In the remaining four years, AI may finally fulfill his prediction, or it may merely leave more people unable to find even fifteen hours of work. As people’s value as means falls, their status as ends will not rise by itself. It depends on whether institutions let them question and are willing to answer.

What must truly be transcended is not one particular ideology, but the logic that people may be used without limit for an ideology’s success. The final direction is not to make one ideology immune to questions, but to prevent every ideology from canceling human questions.

Part V: New Forms of Instrumentalization and Human Resistance

New forms of instrumentalization do not always come from external oppression. They may appear in the language of creativity, attractiveness, intelligence, powerful masculinity, and self-improvement, encouraging people voluntarily to remake themselves into products easier to exchange, evaluate, and display. Artificial intelligence amplifies this tendency while opening new spaces for expression. The question is not whether we should return to a past without technology, but whether people using new tools retain the ability to redefine success, relationships, and the pace of life. Resistance begins here: refusing to let the system’s measure become the only measure.

58. Why AI Video Has Unleashed an Explosion of Creativity

In abstract terms, I think this is productivity growth feeding back into the cultivation of agency. But let us begin with one person’s story. A wedding photographer in Yunnan first experimented with AI video at the beginning of this year because his family’s hotel needed promotional material. Several months later, he spent ten days and a little more than three thousand yuan in computing costs to make Zombie Scavenger, a short film lasting just over three minutes. It was viewed tens of millions of times on Douyin. PJ Ace, founder of an AI film studio in Los Angeles, reposted it on X, called it one of the best short films he had seen in recent years, and searched across the internet for its creator in hopes of collaborating. A few years ago, such a film would have required a visual-effects team. The photographer would not even have found the door.

People did not suddenly acquire imaginations. The distance between “I thought of this” and “other people can see it” suddenly shrank. The technology improved incrementally: whether a character kept the same face in the next shot, whether one object could be changed without altering the whole image, whether sound could be generated at the same time. Each feature alone looked like a small advance. When several arrived together, a workflow whose pieces had never connected became continuous. Gradual technical progress appeared to the user as a sudden ability to act.

The more important change is not a lower cost of succeeding once, but a lower cost of failing once. Several days ago, Business Insider interviewed the creators of the AI science-fiction series The Arc. Its five episodes run more than eighty minutes and consumed about $38,000 in AI credits alone. A single shot often required eight to ten generations. Director Darin Stewart said they would never have had enough money to film an actual battlefield in Japan. The executive producer said they could now decide very late to kill a character without traveling to the other side of the world for reshoots. This tells us two things. First, AI video does not create a feature film from one sentence. Behind an effortless-looking result lie repeated trials and human judgment. Second, when experimentation becomes cheaper, people dare to attempt what they would not have attempted before. The conditions of creativity include not only having a good idea, but being able to afford the cost of having a bad one.

Sometimes an AI-generated result also gives a person a new idea. It supplies an action one had not imagined, and on seeing it one thinks the story should perhaps move in that direction. But whether to retain it, and what its retention changes, remain human decisions. In my essay on empty space, I said that cultivation too requires carving. What to choose, what to remove, and where to stop: not a cut can be omitted. As generation becomes cheaper, the cut becomes more important.

The word “explosion” also contains an illusion produced by what we see. We encounter the few works selected for attention. According to the China Netcasting Services Association, 128,000 new short-form dramas were released online in the first quarter of this year, more than 95 percent of them AI dramas. Another industry report says that fewer than 1 percent of the releases in the first half of the year exceeded one hundred million views. A 2024 experiment published in Science Advances found that writers using AI to help develop ideas produced better individual short stories, with the largest gains among initially weaker writers, but their stories also became more similar. That was an experiment with text and cannot be applied directly to video.

The question most worth asking is in whose hands the same tool arrives. The wedding photographer made the film he wanted to make. Other reports describe a newly graduated employee at an AI video company in Nanjing who had to deliver ten thirty-minute dramas each month, often recreating the dialogue of an existing hit. Many workers in the industry stay up until late at night or early morning waiting for an open slot on generation models. In my essay about AI proving mathematics, I said that when being first ceases to be scarce, guarding it loses value. The same applies to images. Once they are no longer scarce, what matters is what a person actually wants to say. If quotas take even that away, the worker merely feeds prompts to a machine on a new assembly line. The time a tool saves can allow someone finally to film what he wants, or allow a company to demand several times more output in the same hours. Both are called greater efficiency. Their meaning for the person is opposite.

Whether this creative explosion counts cannot be measured by the number of works produced. We must ask how many allowed someone, for the first time, to film what he wanted to say, and how many were only quotas.

59. Why Some People Take Pride in Being an “Alpha Male”

The term is certainly popular. Let us begin with its origin. In 1947, Swiss researcher Rudolph Schenkel observed wolves at the Basel Zoo. Unrelated adult wolves had been confined together and fought constantly for position. The most aggressive became the leader, the alpha. In 1970, wolf biologist L. David Mech used the term in a successful book, and the idea of an alpha wolf spread widely.

Mech later spent more than a dozen summers observing wolves in the Arctic. In 1999, he published an article explaining that a wild wolf pack is actually a family. The supposed alpha pair are simply the parents. The mother cares for and protects the pups, the father finds food, and when the young mature they leave to form families of their own. For years, Mech asked the publisher to stop printing his earlier book. It finally did so in 2022. The term in which so many people take pride therefore comes not from nature as they describe it, but from a cage.

Chimpanzees do have genuine alphas. But Frans de Waal, who studied them for a lifetime, observed that alphas are often generous, mediate conflicts, and comfort those who have been attacked. Their status depends on the allies beneath them.

Even if the animal world truly consisted of fighting one’s way to the top, it would not follow that human beings should take pride in dominating others. What exists in nature and what people should honor are different questions. The power of alpha rhetoric lies in presenting a value choice as a natural law. I am not trying to press you down; nature simply works this way. I did not bully you; you were meant to be bullied. If not by me, then by someone else. Do not blame me. Blame nature.

If even animals do not take pride in bullying others, why do some human beings? I see at least three reasons.

First, the idea provides a simple, decisive answer to what counts as a man. In a series of 2008 studies, psychologists Joseph Vandello and colleagues argued that many people do not treat manhood as a status possessed by birth, but as one that must constantly be earned and can be lost at any time. A challenge therefore feels like a threat. Life is complicated: trouble at work, rejection, an inability to carry a family’s burdens. The alpha ideal compresses everything into one standard: be strong, win, reveal no weakness. Achieve that, and perhaps no one can question you again.

Second, people continually sell the ideal. An entire online business first frightens you with the prospect of becoming a beta, then sells courses and communities teaching you to level up. If the product genuinely cured you, you would no longer buy it. The seller therefore has an incentive to keep reminding you that you are insufficient.

Third, some people genuinely enjoy being above others. There is no need to invent another reason for them.

Of course, many people who say “alpha” mean only that they are confident and take initiative, or are simply making a joke. Wanting strength, companionship, and respect are normal needs. The problem begins when those needs are attached to the answer that one must rise above others.

In 2013, psychologist Joey Cheng and colleagues distinguished two routes to influence. One is dominance, making people fear you. The other is prestige, making them respect you. Both can succeed, but people who follow the path of prestige are better liked. The question is therefore not whether someone is strong, but whether people around him trust or fear him. In my essay on Doctor Doom, I wrote that Tony’s path is that of a man intelligent enough to decide for everyone gradually learning to let others stop him. Doom never permits himself to be stopped. Alpha rhetoric teaches people to become Doom. Real strength means possessing the power to prevent others from stopping you, yet allowing them to do so.

The deepest problem appears at the level of persons as ends in themselves. In the alpha ranking, partners become trophies and other people rungs on a ladder. Even the alpha himself becomes a scorecard that must always rise. He imagines himself free from anyone’s domination, but is governed by the need to appear perpetually strong. This is why I do not much like the comeback that a true alpha never calls himself one. It sounds satisfying, but leaves the ladder standing and disputes only who deserves the top. We should ask why someone must be certified as the person at the top before he is entitled to dignity.

Return to the wolves. The wild pair called alphas raise a litter, then watch their young travel away and establish families of their own. If one insists on taking pride in being an alpha, this genuine version may indeed be worth honoring.

60. Why “Beauty Is Justice” Became Popular

The phrase must be fairly recent. The Chinese term yanzhi, literally “face value,” entered common use only over the past decade. In 2015, it appeared on the magazine Yaowenjiaozi’s list of ten popular expressions. A face had become a numerical value that could be ranked. The construction “X is justice” is generally thought to have been borrowed from the Japanese anime fandom’s “cuteness is justice.” The phrase is therefore usually a joke. Someone watching a movie exclaims that the villain is too handsome and “beauty is justice,” without truly intending to defend his actions. I do not want to put the joke on trial from the outset. “I simply think this is beautiful” is a legitimate answer. Aesthetic experience does not need to prove a moral use before it is allowed to exist.

But a joke spreads this widely because it says something true. In 1972, psychologists Karen Dion and colleagues showed university students photographs. Attractive people were assumed to have better personalities, careers, and marriages. The researchers called this “what is beautiful is good.” In 1991, Alice Eagly and colleagues combined dozens of studies and found that attractive people were indeed judged more sociable and popular. On honesty and concern for others, however, attractive and unattractive people were judged almost identically. These studies measured how others perceived them, not whether they actually were better. Even our bias, then, does not go so far as to make us believe attractive people are more honest.

The advantages of good looks are real. Yes, I know, what a revelation. A 2003 meta-analysis by Hosoda and colleagues found that attractive people were more likely to be hired and promoted and received higher performance ratings. More troublingly, analyses of mock trials found that attractive defendants received lighter sentences, while unattractive defendants were more likely to be convicted. The phrase therefore contains resignation: I do not believe this is fair, but reality works this way. Yet “attractiveness brings advantages” is a description. “Beauty is justice” elevates the description into a truth. Birth circumstances influence opportunity; that does not make birth circumstances just.

“Justice” is far too large a word here. An ancient Greek legend says that the courtesan Phryne was accused of impiety and her advocate opened her clothing in court. The jurors, overwhelmed by her beauty, acquitted her. Most historians think the scene was invented later, but it endured because it is the earliest image of “beauty is justice.” In 1861, the French painter Jean-Léon Gérôme depicted it. His Phryne is not proud. She raises an arm to cover her face. Some contemporaries criticized the gesture, arguing that she should display pride rather than shame. Later commentators observed that the pose resembles the figures in Gérôme’s slave-market paintings. She won the case, and she was also treated as merchandise.

The principle that persons are ends in themselves becomes especially clear here. “Beauty is justice” appears to elevate beautiful people, but also imprisons them. You must remain beautiful, remain willing to be looked at, and cannot refuse us after receiving advantages from your face. To people judged unattractive, it says that they must compensate with something else before they deserve serious treatment. In the essay on alpha males, I said that the ladder remains and only the claim to its top is disputed. Attractiveness is another ladder, tying a person’s place to a trait that can be scored and ranked. In the essay on mathematics, I said that an indicator colonizes the end. The term “face value” itself turns a face into an indicator.

Since the sixteenth century, the figure of Justice has often worn a blindfold. The blindfold originally carried a satirical meaning, suggesting that justice ignored the injustice before her. It was later understood as impartiality: justice does not look at who you are, how rich you are, or, of course, what you look like. “Beauty is justice” removes the blindfold.

Preference may look. Justice must be blind. Looking at faces comes naturally. Covering the eyes is something human beings learned later.

61. Why Some People Treat Intelligence as the Only Standard

There are indeed many such people. Before refuting them, let us reconstruct the reasoning: intelligent people agree with me; he disagrees with me; therefore he is not intelligent.

No one’s intelligence has been measured. “Intelligent” has quietly been replaced by “agrees with me.” Psychology describes a bias blind spot: people readily see other people’s biases while considering themselves objective. My conclusion looks like a fact; the other person’s conclusion demands an explanation. The easiest explanation is that he is stupid. What this protects is not any particular conclusion, but one’s permanent immunity from refutation.

Intelligence does not automatically remove bias. A 2013 review by Keith Stanovich and colleagues found that the tendency to select and evaluate evidence according to one’s prior beliefs, known as “myside bias,” was almost unrelated to intelligence.

Intelligent people are not necessarily more fair, or less fair. They are often merely better at defending what they already believe. If someone reasons rigorously, responds quickly, and knows a great deal, he deserves a serious hearing. We must still ask whether he has selected evidence to favor his position.

Return to intelligence testing itself. In 1905, Alfred Binet and Théodore Simon created the first intelligence test in response to a question from the French Ministry of Education: should children struggling to learn be sent to residential schools attached to asylums or remain in ordinary schools with instruction? They hoped to replace physicians’ judgment with evidence and a tool. But they repeatedly emphasized that intelligence was diverse, mutable, and shaped by environment, and that scores could be compared only among children with similar backgrounds.

The test later reached the United States, the home of pragmatism, where its purpose changed. In 1917, the United States Army administered intelligence tests to more than 1.7 million recruits. By coincidence, one version was called Army Alpha. In 1923, psychologist Carl Brigham used the data in a book claiming that people from northern Europe were most intelligent. He advocated severe immigration restrictions and even influenced American immigration legislation.

In 1927, by an eight-to-one vote, the United States Supreme Court permitted Virginia to sterilize Carrie Buck, one of the darkest moments in American legal history. The new Stanford-Binet test had assigned her a “mental age” of nine. Justice Oliver Wendell Holmes wrote, “Three generations of imbeciles are enough.” Later investigation found that she had earned average grades and that her teachers described her conduct and schoolwork as good. In 1930, Brigham himself acknowledged that his earlier conclusion was unfounded; many of those tested were not native speakers of English. A tool originally designed to identify children who needed assistance was used to decide who did not deserve to have children. Human history has genuinely tried treating intelligence as the only standard.

Many disagreements are not failures of calculation, but disputes about what should be counted. Person A calculates that one job will pay more over ten years. Person B understands this perfectly and still chooses the lower-paid job that permits him to care for family. He has not miscalculated. He wants a different life. If a fact is wrong, correct it with facts. If reasoning is wrong, identify the step. If values differ, state the reasons. “Your IQ is low” skips all three.

AI will be the first to show this reasoning wrong on its own terms. Today’s AI already calculates more quickly and accurately than most people on many tasks. According to the logic that IQ is the only standard, whenever AI and a person disagree, the low-intelligence party is the person and should obey without thought.

But in my essay on AI proving mathematics, I said that I can accept a proof it submits not because it is intelligent, but because each line can be checked. The same applies to disagreement among people. We should examine reasons, not who is more intelligent. However great the difference in ability, it cannot purchase the right to silence someone else.

Even if one person truly is more intelligent, this shows only that some difficult problems are better entrusted to him. It does not show that another person’s words do not count or that his life may be arranged by the more intelligent person. I have written about alpha males and “beauty is justice.” This is the third ladder: strength, appearance, and intelligence. The traits change; the ladder remains the same. Each uses a rankable ability to place someone else below.

Another person’s disagreement does not prove he is stupid. Even if he is less intelligent, that does not prove he is wrong this time. Even if he is wrong this time, that does not prove he is beneath you.

62. Why Some People Proclaim Democracy While Instinctively Flattering Power

This is nothing new, and Lu Xun offered an answer long ago. In The True Story of Ah Q, when Ah Q hears that revolutionaries have entered the city, he begins shouting about revolt. Lying in the Earth-God Temple at night, he imagines revolution as bringing silver ingots, foreign coins, foreign-style shirts, and the young scholar’s wife’s Ningbo-style bed into the temple. “Whatever I want will be mine, and whoever I fancy will be mine.” Ah Q hates Master Zhao, but does not want a world without Master Zhao. He wants a turn at being Master Zhao himself. Many people who proclaim democracy while flattering power are like this. They want to change who sits above, not question why the person above should be unconstrained. They can fiercely oppose one ruler and eagerly attach themselves to another.

This is not only the flaw of a fictional character or one individual. Research finds it too. In a 2020 experiment with American voters, Matthew Graham and Milan Svolik found that when a candidate violated democratic principles, only a small share of voters truly refused to support their own side. The more intense partisan conflict became, the smaller that share was.

In 2023, Suthan Krishnarajan conducted survey experiments in the United States and twenty-two other democracies and found an additional reversal. People described policies they disliked as undemocratic even when they were advanced according to the rules, while finding democratic language for policies they liked even when rules were broken. The order was inverted: first decide who is good, then find a name for what the good person did. When an opponent bypasses oversight, it is abuse of power. When one’s own side bypasses the same oversight, it is decisiveness.

Fear also matters. In 2023, Alia Braley and colleagues found that the more Americans believed the other side would violate democracy first, the more willing they were to violate it themselves. When told that the other side followed the rules more than expected, their own willingness to follow the rules rose. “They will not follow the rules, so we must break them first” sounds like self-defense. It is often the first step toward surrendering constraints. The danger may be real, but we must still ask for the evidence, the scope and duration of the response, and whether the power will be withdrawn after the danger passes.

Flattery often arises from dependence. When assistance comes through institutions, we can be grateful and still complain. When it comes through the favor of a powerful person, loyalty becomes the repayment. We may not even dare object when he treats someone else unjustly.

Nor should all deference be interpreted as sincere worship. Timur Kuran has written about preference falsification: under pressure, people publicly profess what they do not believe. Someone who does not dare oppose may be protecting his livelihood and family. After all, work and life can be considered separately. Actively helping the strong tread on the weak is not the same as lowering one’s head under threat. If we genuinely want people to show courage, they must first be able to live without favors and object without seeing their lives destroyed.

Another version praises democracy while despising the people. It treats ordinary voters as stupid: if they vote correctly, they are awakened; if not, they no longer deserve to choose. In the essay on intelligence, I wrote that no difference in ability purchases the right to silence others. Such people are not awaiting democracy, but a sufficiently intelligent and correct parent.

I often say that constructions cannot be closed. Every side in power has blind spots, including the side I prefer. Democracy therefore does not mean that my side wins or that good people rule. It means that whoever sits above must allow questions and answer them. The test is straightforward. If a power you want to give your own side passed to the person you most despise, would its scope still seem appropriate? If your side lost a majority in a fair contest, would you persuade more people or find a way to prevent them from voting again? As Joe Biden famously put it, you cannot love your country only when you win.

Ah Q’s problem is not that he wants revolution, but that he thinks, “Whoever I fancy will be mine.” Many people can oppose power they dislike. Democracy depends on one further step: permitting the power one likes to be questioned too.

63. Why AI Thought Surveillance Still Cannot Sustain Rule Forever

My friend probably reads a great deal of science fiction. This sounds like technological mind reading. If the First Emperor of Qin had possessed it, perhaps he would not have burned books and buried scholars. I am joking.

But we should not rush to reassure ourselves. A 2023 study in the Quarterly Journal of Economics titled “AI-tocracy” examined facial-recognition procurement by local governments in China. Places that experienced social unrest purchased more of it. After the purchases, later protests did decline, and the AI companies receiving the contracts produced more innovation in both government and commercial markets. Surveillance works. It really can make rule last longer. The question is not whether it works, but why effectiveness still cannot purchase eternity.

Actual mind reading is not as near as people imagine. In 2023, researchers at the University of Texas developed a decoder that could reconstruct, in broad terms, a story someone was hearing from brain scans. But it had to be trained for the particular individual with that person’s active cooperation, and failed when used on someone else. Subjects could defeat it simply by silently naming animals or inventing another story. Today’s “AI mind reading” usually means inferring how someone might think from behavior and relationships. Inference is not knowledge. Yet this is precisely the danger: the machine has not understood the person, while power treats its inference as a conclusion from which no appeal is possible.

Someone tried the real answer before AI. In 1989, East Germany’s Ministry for State Security, the Stasi, employed 91,000 official staff and more than 170,000 informants. Laid end to end, its files extended 112 kilometers. It knew a great deal about people’s minds. On August 31, 1989, Minister Erich Mielke asked regional leaders in an internal meeting whether another June 17 might erupt the next day, referring to the workers’ uprising of 1953. A colonel answered, “Not tomorrow. It will not happen. Is that not why we are here?” Four days later, the Monday demonstrations began in Leipzig. Late on November 9, at the Bornholmer Street crossing in Berlin, Harald Jäger, a Stasi passport-control officer with more than twenty years of service, watched the crowd grow. Fearing deaths, he opened the gate on his own authority without waiting for orders from above.

Three things become visible. First, knowing is not solving. Knowing why someone lacks food does not feed him. The Stasi recorded dissatisfaction in exquisite detail without reducing it at all. Second, information reaching power does not mean power will permit the information to correct it. The more everyone must appear correct, the less subordinates dare report bad news, a phenomenon economists call the dictator’s dilemma. Even if AI bypasses concealed reporting, what happens when the report says the problem originates at the top? Can it change the decision? Third, even the densest surveillance depends on people to carry it out. In the end, rule depends on whether someone like Jäger will remain at the gate.

What makes AI especially alarming is the third point: it may reduce the number of Jägers required. But even if a machine replaces the gatekeeper, the problem merely moves upward. The political scientist Milan Svolik distinguishes two problems: how rulers control the ruled, and how a ruling coalition divides power internally. AI may help with the first and cannot solve the second. Who decides whom the AI watches? Who can change its standards of judgment? When the person at the top is gone, who takes over? More accurate knowledge of ordinary people’s thoughts cannot answer these questions. I say that constructions cannot be closed. A surveillance system is also a construction, and what it sees least clearly is the hand holding it.

Nor can we leap from this to another reassurance, as though rule must fail whenever people refuse inward assent. A regime can last a long time without anyone’s genuine agreement. No law of history is counting down on our behalf. Human dignity therefore cannot be wagered on a machine’s inability to calculate us correctly. Even if someday it knows exactly what I think, it does not thereby acquire the right to decide for me. Even if it knows how to make me obey, it has not proved that obedience right. In the preceding essay, I said that democracy depends on permitting even the power one likes to be questioned. Applied here, no side in power should possess the authority to turn the human mind into a resource of rule.

Surveillance can make people afraid to speak. It cannot make problems cease to exist. It can prolong rule, but cannot turn rule into truth.

64. Why We Assume Others Will Love What We Love

This is more than simple wishful thinking. A psychological principle lies behind it. In 1990, Stanford psychology graduate student Elizabeth Newton conducted an experiment. One person tapped the rhythm of a familiar song, such as “Happy Birthday,” on a table while another tried to guess it. The tappers predicted that listeners would identify half the songs. In reality, listeners guessed only 2.5 percent of 120 songs. What made the difference? The tapper heard the melody continuously inside his head. The listener heard only a sequence of knocks.

Liking something works the same way. You love a song because it accompanied you through a difficult period. When the introduction begins, you hear more than the melody: the circumstances of that year, a particular person, and the self who eventually found a way out. You send the song to a friend and say how moving it is. The friend listens and says, “It is all right.” You heard the same recording, but he cannot hear what is playing in your mind. The same is true of liking a person. You find someone warm and loyal and think no one could dislike him. But other people have lived through different encounters with him.

The psychological concept is called the false-consensus effect. In 1977, Stanford psychologist Lee Ross and colleagues asked students whether they would walk around campus for thirty minutes carrying a sandwich-board advertisement reading “Eat at Joe’s.” Students who agreed estimated that around 60 percent of their classmates would also agree. Those who refused estimated that most would refuse with them. Both sides believed themselves the majority. The reason is not complicated. When judging others, we begin with ourselves as the reference point and adjust, but usually not enough. Research by Nicholas Epley and colleagues also found that the less time people have, the more they guess from themselves. Our circles also tend to contain people with similar tastes. Shared preferences in a small group, seen long enough, come to resemble universal common sense. When someone listens politely and says nothing, silence can easily be counted as agreement.

There is something humane in this. Recommending a beloved book, film, or dish often means: here is a place important to me, and I hope you will enter it too. If the other person also loves it, the happiness comes not merely from a successful recommendation, but from having met there.

The trouble begins with the next step. Your friend tastes the dish and says he does not like it. You answer that you will try another restaurant next time. This was merely a mistaken guess. But if you say, “I brought you here especially. How can you dislike it? You do not know how to enjoy life,” the situation has changed. He is no longer simply tasting a dish. He must supply the correct reaction and prove your judgment, effort, and relationship. In the essay on intelligence, I said that someone’s disagreement does not prove he is stupid. The same applies here. His failure to be moved does not prove that he fails to understand.

In the essay on empty space, I wrote that an author can arrange words, actions, objects, and rhythm, but the feeling itself can occur only in the reader. To write out even the feeling a reader ought to have is to feel on the reader’s behalf, which is colonization. Recommending something follows the same principle. I can play the song for you with care. To demand that you be moved exactly as I was is to feel for you.

If we truly want to know what another person thinks, only a clumsy method works. In 2018, Tal Eyal, Nicholas Epley, and colleagues conducted twenty-five experiments. Asking people to put themselves in another’s position did not consistently increase accuracy. Asking the person directly did. Instead of saying, “This film is so good that you are certain to love it,” we might say, “This film made me think about many things. How did you feel after watching it?” The second sentence leaves room for an answer we do not yet know.

Nor does another person’s dislike cancel your love. I can play you the song, but the song you hear can only be your own.

65. Why Self-Improvement Can Become Spiritual Colonization

I have long held this view. What troubles me most is the long essay Dan Koe published on X on January 12 this year, “How to Fix Your Entire Life in 1 Day.” It received 150 million views in a week, and Chinese translations circulated everywhere. I acknowledge that he is not an ordinary self-help salesman. His essays contain genuine insights, and he has warned that many people leave one matrix only to enter another. I am not saying that every sentence merely sells anxiety. I am discussing the structure that emerges when his most popular essays are read together.

The first step in Koe’s structure is actually opposition to colonization. He says that you are born into a culture with built-in beliefs, values, and standards, and that you outsource certainty to society, parents, teachers, government, and religion. This is not false, and readers feel released from a constraint. I see no problem with this step.

At the second step, however, he installs a different set of standards in the reader’s mind. In another essay, he lists seven standards he established for himself. One is to have $100,000 in the bank; anything below that amount is a problem to solve. He then asks you to change what you consume and rewire your brain. When you fail to reach the standard, you will feel pressure, which he describes as your new identity fighting for survival. The standard is first handed to you, then installed as identity, and finally uses identity to generate pressure.

In my theory of rights, I use colonization in a narrow sense: a transgression disguised as cultivation. Once, others told you what counted as a good life. Others still tell you, but now you believe the voice is your own. Psychology calls this introjection: moving external demands inside and driving oneself through guilt and self-rejection. Moving pressure from outside to inside does not make it one’s own. In the essay on democracy, I wrote that Ah Q hates Master Zhao but does not want a world without Master Zhao; he wants a turn at being Master Zhao. Koe opposes the colonization of the public by society, parents, and teachers. What he wants is not an end to colonization, but a change in colonizer. His return is traffic.

His instrumentalization is even more explicit. One essay is titled “How to Productize Yourself.” The life you pursue becomes a brand; the problems you solve along the way become content; the system that helps others follow becomes a product. He also says that the only choice is to combine life purpose with profit and that entrepreneurship is the only logical option for long-term thinkers. As a business strategy, this is unobjectionable. As a philosophy for the whole of life, it turns the person into material.

His most popular essay contains the sentence that once one sees clearly, every habit that does not advance the goal becomes disgusting. Sitting with a friend for an afternoon and reading a book unrelated to anything become things to despise. In the essay on alpha males, I said that under such rankings even the alpha becomes a scorecard that must always rise. The same happens here. Only now he scores the card himself.

Koe asks readers to write down their true enemy and specifies that it is not their environment or other people. As a single exercise in seeing what one can change, this may be useful. As an explanation, it compresses public problems into personal cultivation. Someone whose wages are withheld may change his own response and also demand the money. The second is not making excuses outside oneself.

I am especially uncomfortable with his treatment of stopping. He writes that people of low intelligence become trapped in problems, abandon the effort when they meet an obstacle, and offers writers who give up before building an audience as an example. In the essay on intelligence, I said that another person’s disagreement does not prove stupidity. The same applies to quitting. Someone may quit because he lacks the ability. Another may have tried, seen clearly, and decided he no longer wants traffic to determine what he writes. Leaving can be judgment at work. A person’s agency includes both “this is worth my effort” and “this is no longer worth it.” Recognizing only the first binds someone permanently to one road.

His business model should also be noted. According to an overview by a Chinese technology publication, the viral essays are only the entry point. Revenue comes from paid subscriptions, books, and an AI tool he helped found. Charging money does not prove malice. But a business sustained by readers feeling that they are still just short of enough has no incentive to tell them that they were worthy all along.

What I dislike most is not the standard he offers, but the way it is delivered. Even if $100,000 is a reasonable savings goal, and even if spending less time on one’s phone and more on action is right, his method begins by making you feel bad.
